## Epigraph / 题记

===Original===
> “And thine eye shall not pity.”
> — Deuteronomy 19:21

===Chinese===
> “你的眼睛不可怜惜。”
> ——《申命记》19:21

## Dedication / 献词

===Original===
> To 

 E. A. S

===Chinese===
> 献给

 E. A. S

## I Mr. Incoul / 第一章 因库尔先生

===Original===
When Harmon Incoul’s wife died, the world in which he lived said that he would not marry again. The bereavement which he had suffered was known to be bitter, and it was reported that he might betake himself to some foreign land. There was, for that matter, nothing to keep him at home. He was childless, his tastes were too simple to make it necessary for him to reside as he had, hitherto, in New York, and, moreover, he was a man whose wealth was proverbial. Had he so chosen, he had little else to do than to purchase a ticket and journey wheresoever he listed, and the knowledge of this ability may have been to him not without its consolations. Yet, if he attempted to map some plan, and think which spot he would prefer, he probably reflected that whatever place he might choose, he would, in the end, be not unlike the invalid who turns over in his bed, and then turns back again on finding the second position no better than the first. However fair another sky might be, it would not make his sorrow less acute.
He was then one of those men whose age is difficult to determine. He had married when quite young, and at the time of his widowerhood he must have been nearly forty, but years had treated him kindly. His hair, it is true, was inclined to scantiness, and his skin was etiolated, but he was not stout, his teeth were sound, he held himself well, and his eyes had not lost their lustre. At a distance, one might have thought him in the thirties, but in conversation his speech was so measured, and about his lips there was a compression such that the ordinary observer fancied him older than he really was.
His position was unexceptionable. He had inherited a mile of real estate in a populous part of New York, together with an accumulation of securities sufficient for the pay and maintenance of a small army. The foundations of this wealth had been laid by an ancestor, materially increased by his grandfather, and consolidated by his father, who had married a Miss Van Tromp, the ultimate descendant of the Dutch admiral.
His boyhood had not been happy. His father had been a lean, taciturn, unlovable man, rigid in principles, stern in manner, and unyielding in his adherence to the narrowest tenets of Presbyterianism. His mother had died while he was yet in the nursery, and, in the absence of any softening influence, the angles of his earliest nature were left in the rough.

===Chinese===
哈蒙·因库尔（Harmon Incoul）的妻子去世时，他所生活于其中的那个社交圈便断言他不会再娶了。人所共知，他遭的丧恸惨痛已极，又有传言说他或将远走异邦。其实，也没有什么把他留在家国。他膝下无子；以他那样简素的趣味，本不必像先前那样定居纽约，何况他又是个财富尽人皆知的人物。他若愿意，只消买一张车票，便可随意漫游到任何地方；而自知有此能力，于他或许也不无慰藉。然而，他若试图筹划什么方案，思量自己更中意哪一处，多半又会想到：无论选了何处，到头来，他都免不了像床上的病人，翻了个身，发觉第二个姿势不见得比第一个好，便又翻了回去。别处的天空再晴美，也断不能使他的哀痛稍减。
他属于那种年纪难以估量的男人。他成婚甚早，鳏居之时想必已年近四十，但岁月待他不薄。诚然，他的头发渐见稀疏，肤色也苍白发暗，但他并不臃肿，牙齿坚实，体态挺拔，眼睛也不曾失去光采。远远望去，人家或许当他三十几岁；可是一交谈，他言语那样字斟句酌，唇边又抿得那样紧，寻常的旁观者便觉得他比实际年纪还要老些。
他的地位无可挑剔。他继承了纽约人烟稠密地段绵延一英里的地产，外加一笔证券，其数额足以支应一支小型军队的薪饷与给养。这份家业的根基由一位先祖奠下，经祖父大力扩充，再由父亲一手巩固；父亲娶的是范·特龙普小姐（Miss Van Tromp），那位荷兰海军上将的末代后裔。
他的少年时代并不快乐。他父亲是个精瘦、寡言、不讨人喜欢的男人，原则僵死，态度严峻，对长老会最偏狭的教条恪守不让。他母亲在他尚在稚龄时便去世了；既没有任何柔化的影响，他幼年天性的诸般棱角便任其保持着粗粝的原样。

===Original===
At school, he manifested a vindictiveness of disposition which made him feared and disliked. One day, a comrade raised the lid of a desk adjoining his own. The raising of the lid was abrupt and possibly intentional. It jarred him in a task. The boy was dragged from him senseless and bleeding. In college, he became aggrieved at a tutor. For three weeks he had him shadowed, then, having discovered an irregularity in his private life, he caused to be laid before the faculty sufficient evidence to insure his removal. Meanwhile, acting presumably on the principle that an avowed hatred is powerless, he treated the tutor as though the grievance had been forgotten. A little later, owing to some act of riotous insubordination, he was himself expelled, and the expulsion seemed to have done him good. He went to Paris and listened decorously to lectures at the Sorbonne, after which he strayed to Heidelberg, where he sat out five semesters without fighting a duel or making himself ill with beer. In his fourth summer abroad, he met the young lady who became his wife. His father died, he returned to New York, and thereafter led a model existence.
He was proud of his wife and indulgent to her every wish. During the years that they lived together, there was no sign or rumor of the slightest disagreement. She was of a sweet and benevolent disposition, and though beyond a furtive coin he gave little to the poor, he encouraged her to donate liberally to the charities which she was solicited to assist. She was a woman with a quick sense of the beautiful, and in spite of the simplicity of his own tastes, he had a house on Madison Avenue rebuilt and furnished in such a fashion that it was pointed out to strangers as one of the chief palaces of the city. She liked, moreover, to have her friends about her, and while he cared as much for society as he did for the negro minstrels, he insisted that she should give entertainments and fill the house with guests. In the winter succeeding the fifteenth anniversary of their marriage, Mrs. Incoul caught a chill, took to her bed and died, forty-eight hours later, of pneumonia.
It was then that the world said that he would not marry again. For two years he gave the world no reason to say otherwise, and for two years time hung heavy on his hands. He was an excellent chess-player, and interested in archaeological pursuits, but beyond that his resources were limited. He was too energetic to be a dilettante, he had no taste for horseflesh, the game of speculation did not interest him, and his artistic tendencies were few. Now and then, a Mr. Blydenburg, a florid, talkative man, a widower like himself, came to him of an evening, and the chessboard was prepared. But practically his life was one of solitude, and the solitude grew irksome to him.
Meanwhile his wound healed as wounds do. The cicatrix perhaps was ineffaceable, but at least the smart had subsided, and in its subsidence he found that the great house in which he lived had taken on the silence of a tomb. Soon he began to go out a little. He was seen at meetings of the Archaeological Society and of an afternoon he was visible in the Park. He even attended a reception given to an English thinker, and one night applauded Salvini.

===Chinese===
在学校里，他便显出一种睚眦必报的性情，令人畏而恶之。一日，一个同学猛然掀起邻着他课桌的桌盖。掀得突兀，或许是有意的，打断了他手头的一门功课。那孩子随即被他拖开，人事不省，鲜血淋漓。进了大学，他与一位导师结了怨。他雇人盯了对方三个星期的梢，探得此人私生活里的不端之后，便向教授会呈上足够的证据，务必使其去职。其间——想必是本着“明言的仇恨便无力量”的原则——他待那位导师一如平素，仿佛嫌隙早已忘了。稍后，他因某桩闹嚷嚷犯上的行径自己也被开除，而这开除似乎于他大有益处。他去了巴黎，在索邦（Sorbonne）规规矩矩地听讲；随后流落到海德堡（Heidelberg），在那里坐满五个学期，既没决斗，也没拿啤酒喝坏身子。留洋的第四个夏天，他遇见了后来成为他妻子那位年轻小姐。父亲故世，他回到纽约，从此过着一种模范的生活。
他以妻子为荣，对她每一个愿望都迁就。共同生活的那些年里，最轻微的不睦也不曾有过形迹或传闻。她性情温婉仁厚；他本人除了偷偷布施几枚钱币之外，对穷人几乎一毛不拔，却鼓励她凡有人来劝募的慈善事业，尽可慷慨解囊。她是个对美感受敏锐的女人；他尽管自己趣味简素，却把麦迪逊大道（Madison Avenue）上的宅邸翻修装点得那般气象，以致外来客指点着它，称它是城中数一数二的府邸。再者，她喜欢朋友环绕左右；而他对社交场面的在意，也不过如他对黑人吟游艺人（negro minstrels）的在意，他却执意要她大开宴乐，使满宅宾客盈门。婚后十五周年纪念次年的冬天，因库尔太太染了风寒，卧床不起，四十八小时后，死于肺炎。
正是在那时，世人说他不会再娶了。整整两年，他没有给世人半点改口的理由；整整两年，时光在他手上沉得难挨。他是个一流的棋手，又留心考古之学，但除此之外，他的凭借实在有限。他精力太旺，当不成浅尝辄止的雅士；他不喜马，投机一局引不起他的兴致，艺术上的嗜好也寥寥无几。偶尔，有位布莱登堡先生（Mr. Blydenburg）——面色红润、爱说话的男人，同他一样是个鳏夫——晚间来访，棋盘便摆起来。但实际上，他的日子是一个人的日子，而这孤身渐渐使他觉得难堪了。
其间，他的创伤照着创伤的常理愈合了。疤痕也许无法磨灭，但至少灼痛已然平伏；痛既平伏，他发觉自己栖身的那座巨宅，已然染上了一座坟墓的寂静。不久，他开始略略出门。考古学会的集会上有人见到他，午后在公园里也望得见他。他甚至出席了款待一位英国思想家的招待会，有一夜还给萨尔维尼（Salvini）鼓了掌。

===Original===
At first he went about with something of that uncertainty which visits one who passes from a dark room to a bright one, but in a little while his early constraint fell from him, and he found that he could mingle again with his fellows.
At some entertainment he met a delicious young girl, Miss Maida Barhyte by name, whom for the moment he admired impersonally, as he might have admired a flower, and until he saw her again, forgot her very existence. It so happened, however, that he saw her frequently. One evening he sat next to her at a dinner and learning from her that she was to be present at a certain reception, made a point of being present himself.
This reception was given by Mrs. Bachelor, a lady, well known in society, who kept an unrevised list, and at stated intervals issued invitations to the dead, divorced and defaulted. When she threw her house open, she liked to have it filled, and to her discredit it must be said that in that she invariably succeeded. On the evening that Mr. Incoul crossed her vestibule, he was met by a hum of voices, broken by the rhythm of a waltz. The air was heavy, and in the hall was a smell of flowers and of food. The rooms were crowded. His friend Blydenburg was present and with him his daughter. The Wainwarings, whom he had always known, were also there, and there were other people by whom he had not been forgotten, and with whom he exchanged a word, but for Miss Barhyte he looked at first in vain.

===Chinese===
起初，他四处走动时带着几分恍惚，正如人从暗屋走进明室时那样；但不多时，早先的拘谨便从他身上褪落，他发觉自己又能与同侪相往来了。
在某场游艺会上，他遇见一位娇美可人的少女，名叫玛伊达·巴海特小姐（Miss Maida Barhyte）。他一时欣赏她，纯然不带私意，如同欣赏一朵花；待到再次见她之前，索性把她存在过这回事忘了个干净。然而事有凑巧，他偏偏频频见到她。一晚宴席上他坐在她邻座，从她口中得知她将出席某场招待会，便特意自己也到场了。
这场招待会出自巴切勒太太（Mrs. Bachelor）之手，社交界一位知名太太；她的名册从不修订，隔些时日便向死者、离了婚的、亏了空的照旧发出请帖。每逢敞宅待客，她总要宾客满座——说来有损她的令誉，这一点她无一次不成功。因库尔先生跨进她门厅的那个晚上，迎接他的是一片人声嗡嗡，间或被一曲华尔兹的节拍划破。空气沉浊，厅里浮着花气和食物气。各间屋子都拥挤不堪。他的朋友布莱登堡在场，与他同来的还有他的女儿。他一向熟识的韦恩韦林一家（the Wainwarings）也在，另外还有些不曾把他忘却的人，他同他们逐一寒暄；只是巴海特小姐，他起初遍寻不着。

===Original===
He would have gone, a crowd was as irksome to him as solitude, but in passing an outer room elaborately supplied with paintings and bric-a-brac, he caught a glimpse of the girl talking with a young man whom he vaguely remembered to have seen in earlier days at his own home.
He walked in: Miss Barhyte greeted him as an old friend: there were other people near her, and the young man with whom she had been talking turned and joined them, and presently passed with them into another room.
Mr. Incoul found a seat beside the girl, and, after a little unimportant conversation asked her a question at which she started. But Mr. Incoul was not in haste for an answer, he told her that with her permission, he would do himself the honor of calling on her later, and, as the room was then invaded by some of her friends, he left her to them, and went his way.

===Chinese===
他本要走了——人群之于他，正与孤独一般难堪——却在经过一间满陈图画与古玩的外厅时，瞥见那少女正与一个青年男子说话；那人他依稀记得早年间在自己家里见过。
他走了进去：巴海特小姐拿老朋友的口气招呼他：她近旁还有旁人，方才同她说话的那青年便转过身去同他们作一处，不多时便随他们进了另一间屋子。
因库尔先生在少女身旁寻了个座位，略叙了几句无关紧要的闲话之后，向她提了一个问题，问得她倏然一动。但因库尔先生并不急于讨得回答；他告诉她，若蒙俯允，他将引以为荣，改日登门拜谒。这时屋里涌进了她的几位朋友，他便把她留给她们，自去了。

## II Miss Barhyte Agrees to Change Her Name / 第二章 巴海特小姐同意改姓

===Original===
A day or two after Mrs. Bachelor’s reception, Mr. Incoul walked down Madison Avenue, turned into one of the adjacent streets and rang the bell of a private boardinghouse.
As he stood on the steps waiting for the door to be opened, a butcher-boy passed, whistling shrilly. Across the way a nursemaid was idling with a perambulator, a slim-figured girl hurried by, a well-dressed woman descended from a carriage and a young man with a flower in his buttonhole issued from a neighboring house. The nursemaid stayed the perambulator and scrutinized the folds of the woman’s gown; the young man eyed the hurrying girl; from the end of the street came the whistle of the retreating butcher, and as it fused into the rumble of Fifth Avenue, Mr. Incoul heard the door opening behind him.
“Is Miss Barhyte at home?” he asked.
The servant, a negro, answered that she was.
“Then be good enough,” said Mr. Incoul, “to take her this card.”

===Chinese===
巴切勒太太的招待会之后过了一两天，因库尔先生沿麦迪逊大道步行而下，拐进旁边一条街，按响了一所私人供膳公寓的门铃。
他站在台阶上候着开门，一个屠户学徒吹着尖利的口哨走过。街对面，一个保姆推着婴儿车在消磨光阴；一个身段苗条的姑娘匆匆走过；一个衣着讲究的女人从马车上下来；一个钮孔里簪着花的青年从邻宅出来。保姆停住婴儿车，细看那女人裙裾的褶皱；青年目送着匆匆而过的姑娘；街尾尽头传来屠户学徒远去的口哨，待它融进第五大道的车声隆隆，因库尔先生听见身后的门开了。
“巴海特小姐在家吗？”他问。
仆人是个黑人，答说她在。
“那么劳驾，”因库尔先生说，“把这张名片递给她。”

===Original===
The drawing-room, long and narrow, as is usual in many New York houses, was furnished in that fashion which is suggestive of a sheriff’s sale, and best calculated to jar the nerves. Mr. Incoul did not wince. He gave the appointments one cursory, reluctant glance, and then went to the window. Across the way the nursemaid still idled, the young man with a flower was drawing on a red glove, stitched with black, and as he looked out at them he heard a rustle, and turning, saw Miss Barhyte.
“I have come for an answer,” he said simply.
“I am glad to see you,” she answered, “very glad; I have thought much about what you said.”
“Favorably, I hope.”
“That must depend on you.” She went to a bell and touched it. “Archibald,” she said, when the negro appeared, “I am out. If any visitors come take them into the other room. Should anyone want to come in here before I ring, say the parlor is being swept.”

===Chinese===
客厅狭而长，一如纽约许多房屋的通例，屋内的陈设俨然一场警长拍卖（sheriff's sale）的模样，最易叫人神经震颤。因库尔先生并未皱眉。他对那些摆设勉强扫了一眼，便走向窗前。街对面，保姆依旧闲立着，簪花的青年正戴上一只黑线锁边的红手套；他朝他们望着，忽听得一阵窸窣，转过身，看见了巴海特小姐。
“我是来讨一个答复的。”他直截了当地说。
“见到你我很高兴，”她答道，“非常高兴；你的话，我思量了很久。”
“想来是有利的吧。”
“那得看你了。”她走到呼唤铃前，碰了一下。“阿奇博尔德（Archibald），”黑人进来时她说，“我不在家。若有客来，把他们领到另一间屋去。我摇铃之前，若有人要进这间屋，就说客厅正在打扫。”

===Original===
The man bowed and withdrew. He would have stood on his head for her. There were few servants that she did not affect in much the same manner. She seemed to win willingness naturally.
She seated herself on a sofa, and opposite to her Mr. Incoul found a chair. Her dress he noticed was of some dark material, tailor-made, and unrelieved save by a high white collar and the momentary glisten of a button. The cut and sobriety of her costume made her look like a handsome boy, a young Olympian as it were, one who had strayed from the games and been arrayed in modern guise. Indeed, her features suggested that combination of beauty and sensitiveness which was peculiar to the Greek lad, but her eyes were not dark﻿—they were the blue victorious eyes of the Norseman﻿—and her hair was red, the red of old gold, that red which partakes both of orange and of flame.
“I hope﻿—” Mr. Incoul began, but she interrupted him.
“Wait,” she said, “I have much to tell you of which the telling is difficult. Will you bear with me a moment?”
“Surely,” he answered.

===Chinese===
那人躬身退下。为她，他便是倒立起来也肯。仆人们几乎没有一个不被她这样打动的。她仿佛天生就能赢得人的殷勤。
她在一张沙发上落座，因库尔先生在她对面寻了一把椅子。他留意到她的衣裳是深色料子，取的男装裁法，除一条高高的白领和钮扣偶尔一闪的光泽，别无点缀。这剪裁与素净使她看上去像个俊美的少年，俨然一位年轻的奥林匹斯健将，不过是从竞赛场中走岔了道，换上了今世的装束。的确，她的容貌正是那种美与敏感的合体，希腊少年独有的合体；但她的眼睛并不黑——那是诺斯人湛蓝而凯旋的眼睛——她的头发是红的，古金色的红，一种半近橙黄、半近火焰的红。
“我希望——”因库尔先生开口，却被她拦住了。
“且慢，”她说，“我有许多话要对你说，只是难以出口。你肯容我片刻吗？”
“自然。”他答道。

===Original===
“It is this: It is needless for me to say I esteem you; it is unnecessary for me to say that I respect you, but it is because I do both that I feel I may speak frankly. My mother wishes me to marry you, but I do not. Let me tell you, first, that when my father died he left very little, but the little that he left seems to have disappeared, I do not know how or where. I know merely that we have next to nothing, and that we are in debt beside. Something, of course, has had to be done. I have found a position. Where do you suppose?” she asked, with a sudden smile and a complete change of key.
But Mr. Incoul had no surmises.
“In San Francisco! The MacDermotts, you know, the Bonanza people, want me to return with them and teach their daughter how to hold herself, and what not to say. It has been arranged that I am to go next week. Since the other night, however, my mother has told me to give up the MacDermotts and accept your offer. But that, of course, I cannot do.”
“And why not?”
To this Miss Barhyte made no answer.

===Chinese===
“是这么回事：不必说我器重你，也不必说我敬服你；正因两样都有，我才觉得可以直言相告。我母亲愿我嫁你，我却不愿。先告诉你：我父亲去世时留下的东西寥寥，而那寥寥的一点似乎也不知去向了，我不知道怎么去的、去了哪里。我只知道我们几乎一无所有，此外还负着债。当然，总得有个办法。我寻到了一个职位。你猜在哪儿？”她问，忽地一笑，腔调全然一变。
可因库尔先生猜不着。
“在旧金山！麦克德莫特一家（the MacDermotts），你知道，就是那波南扎（bonanza）豪富，要我跟他们回去，教他们的女儿仪态举止、什么话不该说。已经商定我下星期动身。可是自前晚起，我母亲又叫我对麦克德莫特家罢手，应下你的求婚。这个么，我自然做不到。”
“为什么做不到？”
这话巴海特小姐没有回答。

===Original===
“You do not care for me, I know; there is slight reason why you should. Yet, might you not, perhaps, in time?”
The girl raised her eyebrows ever so slightly. “So you see,” she continued, “I shall have to go to San Francisco.”
Mr. Incoul remained silent a moment. “If,” he said, at last, “if you will do me the honor to become my wife, in time you will care. It is painful for me to think of you accepting a position which at best is but a shade better than that of a servant, particularly so when I am able﻿—nay, anxious,” he added, pensively﻿—“to surround you with everything which can make life pleasant. I am not old,” he went on to say, “at least not so old that a marriage between us should seem incongruous. I find that I am sincerely attached to you﻿—unselfishly, perhaps, would be the better word﻿—and, if the privilege could be mine, the endeavor to make you happy would be to me more grateful than a second youth. Can you not accept me?”
He had been speaking less to her than to the hat which he held in his hand. The phrases had come from him haltingly, one by one, as though he had sought to weigh each mentally before dowering it with the wings of utterance, but, as he addressed this question he looked up at her. “Can you not?” he repeated.
Miss Barhyte raised a handkerchief to her lips and bit the shred of cambric with the *disinvoltura* of an heiress.

===Chinese===
“你并不爱我，这我知道；你也没什么理由该爱我。然而，假以时日，或许你会？”
姑娘的眉毛微微一挑。“所以你瞧，”她接着说，“我只好去旧金山了。”
因库尔先生沉默了片刻。“倘若，”他终于说，“倘若你肯赏光做我的妻子，日久你是会爱上我的。想到你去就一个至多不过比仆人略高一筹的职位，我心里难受；尤其在我力能——不，是热望，”他若有所思地补了一句——“把你围在一切足以使人生适意的东西中间的时候。我不算老，”他继续说，“至少没有老到使我们结缡显得不称。我发觉我真心恋慕着你——或许说‘别无所图’更为贴切——而这殊荣若能归我，那么使你幸福的努力，在我便比第二次青春更加可感。你就不能应允我吗？”
他这一番话，与其说是对她讲的，不如说是对他手中那顶帽子讲的。字句迟疑地、一句一句从他口中出来，仿佛每一句都要先在心中掂过分量，才赋予它言语的翅膀；但问到这一句时，他抬起眼来看她。“能不能？”他又问了一遍。
巴海特小姐举起一方手帕送到唇边，带着女继承人那种从容自若（*disinvoltura*），咬着那一角细麻纱。

===Original===
“Why is it,” she queried, “why is it that marriage ever was invented? Why cannot a girl accept help from a man without becoming his wife?”
Mr. Incoul was about to reply that many do, but he felt that such a reply would be misplaced, and he called a platitude to his rescue. “There are wives and wives,” he said.
“That is it,” the girl returned, the color mounting to her cheeks; “if I could but be to you one of the latter.”
He stared at her wonderingly, almost hopefully. “What do you mean?” he asked.
“Did you ever read *Eugénie Grandet*?”

===Chinese===
“为什么呢，”她问，“当初为什么要发明婚姻？为什么一个姑娘接受男人的帮助，就非得做他的妻子不可？”
因库尔先生正想答一句“不做的也多得很”，却觉得这样的回答不合时宜，便请了一句老生常谈来救场。“妻子和妻子，原是不同的。”他说。
“正是这句话，”姑娘回道，红晕爬上双颊；“但愿我能做你那后一种。”
他惊异地、几乎是怀着希望地盯着她。“你是什么意思？”他问。
“你读过《欧也妮·葛朗台》（*Eugénie Grandet*）吗？”

===Original===
“No,” he answered, “I never have.”
“Well, I read it years ago. It is, I believe, the only one of Balzac’s novels that young girls are supposed to read. It is tiresome indeed; I had almost forgotten it, but yesterday I remembered enough of the story to help me to come to some decision. In thinking the matter over and over again as I have done ever since I last saw you it has seemed that I could not become your wife unless you were willing to make the same agreement with me that Eugénie Grandet’s husband made with her.”
“What was the nature of that agreement?”
“It was that, though married, they were to live as though they were not married﻿—as might brother and sister.”
“Always?”

===Chinese===
“没有，”他答道，“从未读过。”
“是这样的：我多年前读过。我想，巴尔扎克的小说里，小姑娘家被准许读的怕只有这一本。确实沉闷，我几乎把它忘了；可昨天我想起了足够的情节，帮自己拿定了主意。自上次见你，我翻来覆去想了又想，只觉得：除非你肯同我立下欧也妮·葛朗台的丈夫同她立下的那个约定，我便做不成你的妻子。”
“那是个什么样的约定？”
“就是：虽已成婚，两人却要过不成婚的日子——如同一对兄妹。”
“永远如此？”

===Original===
“Yes.”
“No,” Mr. Incoul answered, “to such an agreement I could not consent. Did I do so, I would be untrue to myself, unmanly to you. But if you will give me the right to aid you and yours, I will﻿—according to my lights﻿—leave nothing undone to make you contented; and if I succeed in so doing, if you are happy, then the agreement which you have suggested would fall of itself. Would it not?” he continued. “Would it not be baseless? See﻿—” he added, and he made a vague gesture, but before he could finish the phrase, the girl’s hands were before her face and he knew that she was weeping.
Mr. Incoul was not tenderhearted. He felt toward Miss Barhyte as were she some poem in flesh that it would be pleasant to make his own. In her carriage as in her looks, he had seen that stamp of breeding which is coercive even to the dissolute. In her eyes he had discerned that promise of delight which it is said the lost goddesses could convey; and at whose conveyance, the legend says, the minds of men were enraptured. It was in this wise that he felt to her. Such exhilaration as she may have brought him was of the spirit, and being cold by nature and undemonstrative, her tears annoyed him. He would have had her impassive, as befitted her beauty. Beside, he was annoyed at his own attitude. Why should there be sorrow where he had sought to bring smiles? But he had barely time to formulate his annoyance into a thing even as volatile as thought﻿—the girl had risen and was leaving the room.
As she moved to the door Mr. Incoul hastened to open it for her, but she reached it before him and passed out unassisted.
When she had gone he noticed that the sun was setting and that the room was even more hideous than before. He went again to the window wondering how to act. The entire scene was a surprise to him. He had come knowing nothing of the girl’s circumstances, and suddenly he learned that she was in indigence, unable perhaps to pay her board bills and worried by small tradesmen. He had come prepared to be refused and she had almost accepted him. But what an acceptance! In the nature of it his thoughts roamed curiously: he was to be a little more than kin, a little less than kind. She would accept him as a husband for out-of-door purposes, for the world’s sake she would bear his name﻿—at arm’s length. According to the terms of her proposition were she ever really his wife it would be tantamount to a seduction. He was to be with her, and yet, until she so willed it, unable to call her his own. And did he refuse these terms, she was off, no one knew whither. But he had not refused, he told himself, he had indeed not refused, he had merely suggested an amendment which turned an impossibility into an allurement. What pleasanter thing could there be than the winning of one’s own wife? The idea was so novel it delighted him. For the moment he preferred it to any other; beside it his former experience seemed humdrum indeed. But why had she wept? Her reasons, however, he had then no chance to elucidate. Miss Barhyte returned as abruptly as she had departed.

===Chinese===
“是的。”
“不行，”因库尔先生答道，“这样的约定，我不能应承。若应承了，我便对不起自己，也对你有失丈夫气概。但你若肯给我援助你和你家人的权利，我必——尽我所知所能——想方设法使你称心；倘使我做到了，倘使你快活了，那么你提的约定自会自行消解。不是么？”他接着说，“它不是会失了凭据么？你瞧——”他补了一句，做了个含糊的手势；可话没说完，姑娘的双手已掩在脸前，他知道她在哭。
因库尔先生并非心肠软的人。他对巴海特小姐的心情，仿佛她是一首有血肉的诗，收为己有当是一桩乐事。在她的仪态里，一如在她的容貌里，他见过那种教养的印记，连放荡之徒也要受它约束。在她的眼睛里，他辨出过那种欢乐的允诺，据说失落的女神们正能传递这种允诺；而传说又云，经此传递，人心为之陶醉。他对她便是这样一番心情。她所能带给他的欢愉属于精神一类；而他天性冷淡、不形于色，她的眼泪叫他恼火。他倒宁愿她无动于衷，那才配她的美貌。再者，他对自己此刻的态度也恼火。他原要引来微笑的地方，何来悲伤？可他连把这点恼怒整理成一件哪怕如思绪般轻飘的东西的工夫都没有——姑娘已起身出屋去了。
她向门口走去时，因库尔先生赶上去为她开门，却被她先到了门前，不劳他扶，径自出去了。
她走后，他发觉日头正在西沉，屋子比先前更加难看了。他又走到窗前，盘算着如何行事。方才这一场全然出乎他的意料。他来时对姑娘的境况一无所知，却突然得知她陷于困顿，或许连膳宿费都付不出，还被小商贩们纠缠。他来时备好了遭拒的准备，而她几乎是应允了他。可这是怎样一种应允啊！他就着这件事的性质好奇地驰想：他要做的，是比亲族多一分、比亲人少一分的角色。她要他做丈夫，是为着门外、为着世人；她要姓他的姓——却隔着一条手臂的距离。照她提的条款，她若有一日真做了他的妻子，那便无异于一场诱奸。他要同她在一起，然而在她自愿之前，不能称她为自己的。他若拒绝这些条款，她便一走了之，去向无人知晓。可他没有拒绝，他对自己说，他确实没有拒绝，他不过提了一项修正案，把一桩不可能变成了一个诱惑。还有什么比赢得自己的妻子更殹意的事么？这念头如此新奇，令他心喜。此刻他宁愿要它，别的都不要；与之相比，他从前的经验实在平淡无奇。可她为什么要哭？然而她的缘由，他当时已无从究诘。巴海特小姐去得突然，回来得也一样突然。

===Original===
“Forgive me,” she said, advancing to where he stood, “it was stupid of me to act as I did. I am sorry﻿—are we still friends?” Her eyes were clear as had she never wept, but there were circles about them, and her face was colorless.
“Friends,” he answered, “yes, and more﻿—” He hesitated a moment, and then hastily added, “It is agreed, then, is it not, you will be my wife?”
“I will be your wife?”
“As Balzac’s heroine was to her husband?”
“You have said it.”

===Chinese===
“饶恕我，”她说，走到他立处，“我方才那样太傻了。对不起——我们还是朋友么？”她的眼睛清明如故，仿佛从不曾哭过，但眼周有了一圈圈暗影，脸上没有血色。
“朋友，”他答道，“是，还不止——”他迟疑了片刻，随即匆匆加了一句：“那么说定了，好么，你愿意做我的妻子？”
“我愿意做你的妻子？”
“照巴尔扎克女主人公对她丈夫的那样？”
“正是你说的那样。”

===Original===
“But not always. If there come a time when you care for me, then I may ask you to give me your heart as today I have asked for your hand?”
“When that day comes, believe me,” she said, and her delicious face took on a richer hue, “when that day comes there will be neither asking nor giving, we shall have come into our own.”
With this assurance Mr. Incoul was fain to be content, and, after another word or two, he took his leave.
For some time after his departure, Miss Barhyte stood thinking. It had grown quite dark. Before the window a streetlamp burned with a small, steady flame, but beyond, the azure of the electric light pervaded the adjacent square with a suggestion of absinthe and vice. One by one the opposite houses took on some form of interior illumination. A newsboy passed, hawking an extra with a noisy, aggressive ferocity as though he were angry with the neighborhood, and dared it come out and wrestle with him for his wares. There was a thin broken stream of shopgirls passing eastward; at intervals, men in evening dress sauntered leisurely to their dinners, to restaurants, or to clubland, and over the rough pavement there was a ceaseless rattle of traps and of wagons; the air was alive with the indefinable murmurs of a great city.
Miss Barhyte noticed none of these things. She had taken her former seat on the sofa and sat, her elbow on her crossed knee, her chin resting in her hand, while the fingers touched and barely separated her lips. The light from without was just strong enough to reach her feet and make visible the gold clock on her silk stocking, but her face was in the shadow as were her thoughts.

===Chinese===
“但不是永远。倘若有一天你爱上了我，那时我或许可以请你把心给我，如今天我请你的手一样？”
“那一天真到了，请相信我，”她说，她那张娇美的脸上泛起更浓的红晕，“那一天到了，便无所谓求，也无所谓给，我们彼此已是彼此的了。”
得了这个保证，因库尔先生也就乐于满足，又交谈了一两句，便告辞了。
他走后，巴海特小姐立在那里想了许久。天已全黑。窗前一盏街灯燃着小而稳的火苗；更远处，电灯的青光浸透了邻近的广场，带着一点苦艾酒与罪恶的意味。对面的房屋一间间亮起了各式内里的灯火。一个报童走过，叫卖号外，嗓门凶横逼人，仿佛在同整条街区怄气，激它出来同他抢买卖似的。女店员们三三两两、断断续续向东走去；隔一阵，着晚礼服的男人们从容地踱向他们的晚宴、餐馆或俱乐部地带；粗石路面上，轻便马车与货车辚辚不息；空气里浮动着大城市那难以名状的嘁喳。
这些巴海特小姐一样也没有留意。她坐回沙发上原处，一肘支在交叠的膝上，下巴搁在手里，指尖抵着微微分开的嘴唇。外面的光刚够照到她的脚，照见她丝袜上的金色织花，她的脸埋在暗处，她的心思也是。

===Original===
Presently she rose and rang the bell. “Archibald,” she said, when the man came, and who at once busied himself with lighting the gas, “I want to send a note; can’t you take it? It’s only across the square.”
“I’ll have to be mighty spry about it, miss. The old lady do carry on most unreasonable if I go for anybody but herself. She has laws that strict they’d knock the Swedes and Prussians silly. Why, you wouldn’t believe if I told you how﻿—”
And Archibald ran on with an unbelievable tale of recent adventure with the landlady. But the girl feigned no interest. She had taken a card from her case. On it she wrote, *Viens ce soir*, and after running the pencil through her name, she wrote on the other side, Lenox Leigh, esq., Athenaeum Club.
“There,” she said, interrupting the negro in the very climax of his story, “it’s for Mr. Leigh; you are sure to find him, so wait for an answer.”
A fraction of an hour later, when Miss Barhyte took her seat at the dinner table, she found beside her plate a note that contained a single line: “Will be with you at nine. I kiss your lips. L. L.”

===Chinese===
不多时她起身摇铃。“阿奇博尔德，”那人进来便忙着点煤气灯，她说，“我要送张便条；你能跑一趟么？就在广场对面。”
“那俺可得脚底抹油快着点，小姐。俺要是替旁人跑腿，那位老太太闹起来可没个道理讲。她立的规矩严得能把瑞典人普鲁士人都吓傻。嗐，俺要跟您说她怎么——您都不会信——”
阿奇博尔德便滔滔不绝地讲起他同女房东近来一场令人难以置信的遭遇。可姑娘连假装的兴致也没有。她从名片盒里取出一张名片，在上面写了 *Viens ce soir*（今夜来），随即用铅笔把自己的名字划去，又在背面写上：伦诺克斯·利先生（Lenox Leigh, esq.），雅典娜神庙俱乐部（Athenaeum Club）。
“喏，”她说，恰在黑人故事讲到最热闹处打断了他，“这是给利先生的；你一定找得到他，所以要等个回话。”
不到一小时之后，巴海特小姐在餐桌前落座时，发觉盘边有一张便条，上面只有一行字：“九点到你处。我吻你的唇。L. L.”

## III After Darkness / 第三章 黑暗之后

===Original===
When Miss Barhyte was one year younger she had gone with her mother to pass the summer at Mt. Desert; and there, the morning of her arrival, on the monster angle of Rodick’s porch, Lenox Leigh had caused himself to be presented.
A week later Miss Barhyte and her new acquaintance were as much gossiped about as was possible in that once unconventional resort.
Lenox Leigh was by birth a Baltimorean, and by profession a gentleman of leisure, yet as the exercise of that profession is considered less profitable in Baltimore than in New York, he had, for some time past, been domiciled in the latter city. From the onset he was well received; one of the Amsterdams had married a Leigh, his only sister had charmed the heart of Nicholas Manhattan, and being in this wise connected with two of the reigning families, he found the doors open as a matter of course. But even in the absence of potent relatives, there was no reason why he should not have been cordially welcomed. He was, it is true, better read than nineteen men out of twenty; when he went to the opera he preferred listening to the music to wandering from box to box; he declined to figure in cotillons and at no dinner, at no supper had he been known to drink anything stronger than claret and water.
But as an offset to these defects he was one of the most admirably disorganized young men that ever trod Fifth Avenue. He was without beliefs and without prejudices; added to this he was indulgent to the failings of others, or perhaps it would be better to say that he was indifferent. It may be that the worst thing about him was that he was not bad enough; his wickedness, such as there was of it, was purely negative. A poet of the decadence of that period in fact when Rome had begun to weary of debauchery without yet acquiring a taste for virtue, a pre-medieval Epicurean, let us say, could not have pushed a creedless refinement to a greater height than he. There were men who thought him a prig, and who said so when his back was turned.
It was in the company of this patrician of a later day that Miss Barhyte participated in the enjoyments of Mt. Desert. Leigh was then in his twenty-fifth year, and Miss Barhyte was just grazing the twenties. He was attractive in appearance, possessed of those features which now and then permit a man to do without beard or moustache, and his hair, which was black, clung so closely to his head that at a distance it might have been taken for the casque of a Saracen. To Miss Barhyte, as already noted, a full share of beauty had been allotted. Together they formed one of the most charming couples that it has ever been the historian’s privilege to admire. And being a charming couple, and constantly together, they excited much interest in the minds of certain ladies who hailed from recondite Massachusettsian regions.

===Chinese===
巴海特小姐比如今小一岁那年，曾随母亲去沙漠山岛（Mt. Desert）避暑；就在那里，抵达的那个早晨，在罗迪克旅馆（Rodick's）门廊那巨大的拐角处，伦诺克斯·利（Lenox Leigh）设法请人把自己引见给了她。
一周之后，巴海特小姐与她这位新相识，便已在那处曾经不拘俗礼的避暑地被人嚼舌到无以复加的地步。
伦诺克斯·利生于巴尔的摩（Baltimore），以闲人为业；不过这门行业在巴尔的摩被认为不如在纽约有利可图，故而近来他已在后一城安了家。他从一开始便颇受欢迎：阿姆斯特丹家（the Amsterdams）有一位小姐嫁过利家，他唯一的姐姐又迷住了尼古拉斯·曼哈顿（Nicholas Manhattan）的心；这样同两个当令望族攀上了亲，各家的门自然为他敞开。但即使没有得力的亲戚，他也不愁不受热诚的接纳。诚然，他的博览胜过二十人中的十九个；他上歌剧院，宁可听音乐，也不愿从这间包厢游荡到那间；他不肯在集体舞里露头角；无论正餐夜宴，从未有人见他喝过比掺水红葡萄酒更烈的东西。
可这些短处自有补偿：他属于踏上过第五大道的青年中最散漫得令人叹赏的那一类。他没有信仰，也没有偏见；此外，他对旁人的过失一味姑息——或者说他漠不关心，或许更贴切。他身上最坏的一点，也许正是他坏得不够；他那点恶，若有也算有的话，纯属消极的恶。罗马已开始厌倦荒淫、却尚未养成对德行的胃口的那段颓废时代的诗人——比方说，一位中世纪以前的伊壁鸠鲁信徒——也未必能把一种无信条的雅致推到比他更高的地步。有些人当他是个自命不凡的家伙，背地里也这么说。
正是伴着这位晚近时代的贵族，巴海特小姐共享了沙漠山岛的种种乐趣。利那年二十五岁，巴海特小姐则刚蹭着二十的边。他相貌讨喜，生就那种偶尔可以让人免去须髯的眉目；他的头发乌黑，紧贴着头，远看简直可以当作萨拉森人的头盔。至于巴海特小姐，如前所述，造化也分给了她满满的一份美貌。两人合在一起，便是史家有幸激赏过的最动人的一对。既是动人的一对，又朝夕不离，他们便引得了几位出身于马萨诸塞（Massachusetts）幽僻地带的太太极大的关切。

===Original===
To this interest they were indifferent. At first, during the early evenings when the stars were put out by the Northern Lights, they rowed to the outermost shore of a neighboring island and lingered there for hours in an enchanted silence. Later, in the midsummer nights, when the harvest-moon was round and mellow, they wandered through the open fields back into the Dantesque forests and strayed in the clinging shadows and inviting solitudes of the pines.
From one such excursion they returned to the hotel at an hour which startled the night porter, who, in that capricious resort, should have lost his ability to be startled at anything.
That afternoon Mrs. Bunker Hill﻿—one of the ladies to whom allusion has been made﻿—approached Miss Barhyte on the porch. “And are you to be here much longer?” she asked, after a moment or two of desultory conversation.
“The holidays are almost over,” the girl answered, with her radiant smile.
“*Holidays* do you call them? *Holidays* did I understand you to say? *I* should have called them *fast* days.” And, with that elaborate witticism, Mrs. Bunker Hill shook out her skirts and sailed away.

===Chinese===
对这份关切，他们全不理会。起初，在星辰被北极光熄灭的初晚，他们摇桨到邻岛最远的一岸，在迷人的寂静里流连几个钟头。后来，仲夏之夜，收获月又圆又温，他们穿过旷野，走进但丁式的森林，在松林那黏人的影子和诱人的幽寂里迷了路。
一次这样的漫游之后，他们回到旅馆，时辰之晚叫守夜人吃了一惊——在那处喜怒无常的胜地，他本该早已失掉对任何事情吃惊的能力。
那天下午，邦克山太太（Mrs. Bunker Hill）——前文影射的那几位太太之一——在门廊上凑近巴海特小姐。东拉西扯了两三句之后，她问：“你们还要在这里多待些日子吗？”
“假期差不多完了。”姑娘答道，带着她那灿烂的笑。
“*假期*？你管它们叫*假期*？我没听错吧，你说的是*假期*？换了*我*，可要管它们叫*斋戒*日。”说完这句精心结撰的俏皮话，邦克山太太抖开裙裾，扬帆而去。

===Original===
Meanwhile an enveloping intimacy had sprung up between the two young people. Their conversation need not be chronicled. There was in it nothing unusual and nothing particularly brilliant; it was but a strain from that archaic duo in which we have all taken part and which at each repetition seems an original theme.
For the first time Miss Barhyte learned the intoxication of love. She gave her heart ungrudgingly, without calculation, without forethought, wholly, as a heart should be given and freely as had the gift been consecrated in the nave of a cathedral. If she were generous why should she be blamed? In the giving she found that mite of happiness, that one unclouded day that is fair as June roses and dawns but once.
In September Miss Barhyte went with her mother on a visit in the Berkshire Hills. Leigh journeyed South. A matter of business claimed his attention in Baltimore, and when, early in November, he reached New York the girl had already returned.
Since the death of Barhyte père she had lived with her mother in a small house in Irving Place, which they rented, furnished, by the year. But on this particular autumn affairs had gone so badly, some stock had depreciated, some railroad had been mismanaged, or some trustee had speculated﻿—something, in fact, had happened of which no one save those personally interested ever know or ever care, and, as a result, the house in Irving Place was given up, and the mother and daughter moved into a boardinghouse.
Of all this Lenox Leigh was made duly aware. Had he been able, and could such a thing have been proper and conventional, he would have been glad indeed to offer assistance; he was not selfish, but then he was not rich, a condition which always makes unselfishness easy. Matrimony was out of the question; his income was large enough to permit him to live without running into debt, but beyond that its flexibility did not extend, and in money matters, and in money matters alone, Lenox Leigh was the most scrupulous of men. Beside, as the phrase goes, he was not a marrying man﻿—marriage, he was accustomed to assert, means one woman more and one man less, and beyond that definition he steadfastly declined to look, except to announce that, like some other institutions, matrimony was going out of fashion.

===Chinese===
其间，两个年轻人之间滋长起一种无所不包的亲昵。他们的谈话不必尽录。其中既无别致之处，也谈不上如何隽妙；不过是从那支古旧的二重奏里抽出的一段曲调——那支曲子我们人人都曾参与，而且每逢重奏，都仿佛是一阕新题。
巴海特小姐第一次尝到了爱的迷醉。她把心交了出去，不吝惜、不计较、不前瞻，整个儿地，如一颗心所应有的交付，又自由得仿佛这份馈赠曾在大教堂的中堂里受过祝圣。她若是慷慨，何罪之有？在交付中她得到了那一星幸福，那一个无云的日子——美如六月的蔷薇，一生只破晓一次。
九月，巴海特小姐随母亲去伯克希尔山（Berkshire Hills）做客。利则南下。巴尔的摩有一桩生意要他照管；十一月初他到纽约时，姑娘已经回来了。
自老巴海特先生（Barhyte père）去世后，她同母亲住在欧文广场（Irving Place）一幢小宅里，宅子带家具按年租下。可这年秋天景况糟得很：有股票跌了价，有铁路经营失当，或有受托人挪款投机——总之出了点事，除了当事人，谁也不会知晓，也不会在意——结果，欧文广场的宅子退了租，母女俩搬进了一家供膳公寓。
这一切，伦诺克斯·利都一一得知。倘若他力所能及，倘若此事又合体统，他也确实乐于援手；他并非自私，但他也不阔——不阔这种境况，向来让人容易显得无私。婚姻则无从谈起：他的进项够他衣食无债地过活，再多一分它的弹性便不容了；而在金钱事务上——唯在金钱事务上——伦诺克斯·利是最一丝不苟的人。再说，套句俗话，他不是个肯娶亲的男人——婚姻，他惯于断言，意味着多一个女人、少一个男人；这个定义之外，他便执意不肯多看一眼，只除了一句话：婚姻这制度，同另外几样制度一样，正在过时。

===Original===
That winter Miss Barhyte was more circumspect. It was not that her affection had faltered, but in the monochromes of a great city the primal glamour that was born of the fields and of the sea lost its lustre. Then, too, Lenox in the correctness of evening dress was not the same adorer who had lounged in flannels at her side, and the change from the open country to the boardinghouse parlor affected their spirits unconsciously.
And so the months wore away. There were dinners and routs which the young people attended in common, there were long walks on avenues unfrequented by fashion, and there were evenings prearranged which they passed together and during which the girl’s mother sat upstairs and thought her own thoughts.
Mrs. Barhyte had been a pretty woman and inconsequential, as pretty woman are apt to be. Her girlhood had been of the happiest, without a noteworthy grief. She married one whose perfection had seemed to her impeccable, and then suddenly without a monition the tide of disaster set in. After the birth of a second child, Maida, her husband began to drink, and drank, after each debauch with a face paler than before, until disgrace came and with it a plunge into the North River. Her elder child, a son, on whom she placed her remaining hopes, had barely skirted manhood before he was taken from her to die of smallpox in a hospital. Then came a depreciation in the securities which she held and in its train the small miseries of the shabby genteel. Finally, the few annual thousands that were left to her seemed to evaporate, and as she sat in her room alone her thoughts were bitter. The pretty inconsequential girl had developed into a woman, hardened yet unresigned. At forty-five her hair was white, her face was colorless as her widow’s cap, her heart was dead.
On the night when her daughter, under the chaperonage of Mrs. Hildred, one of her few surviving relatives﻿—returned from the reception, she was still sitting up. At Mrs. Hildred’s suggestion a position, to which allusion has been made, had been offered to her daughter, and that position﻿—the bringing up or rather the bringing out of a child of the West﻿—she determined that her daughter should accept. Afterwards﻿—well, perhaps for Maida there were other things in store, as for herself she expected little. She would betake herself to some Connecticut village and there wait for death.
When her daughter entered the room she was sitting in the erect impassibility of a statue. Her eyes indeed were restless, but her face was dumb, and in the presence of that silent desolation, the girl’s tender heart was touched.

===Chinese===
那年冬天，巴海特小姐更谨慎了。倒不是她的情意有了动摇，而是在大都会的单色画里，那生于田野与海上的原始魅力失了光泽。再者，一身笔挺晚礼服的伦诺克斯，已不是那穿法兰绒衣衫懒懒倚在她身边的求爱者；从旷野搬到供膳公寓的客厅，两人的心境也在不知不觉间受了影响。
就这样，一个月一个月磨了过去。有两人一同赴的宴会与盛大的晚聚，有时髦人物不到的大道上的长步，也有预先安排好、两人共度的夜晚——那时姑娘的母亲便坐在楼上，想她自己的心事。
巴海特太太当年也是个美人，同美人惯常的那样，没心没肺。她的少女时代幸福无比，没有一桩值得一提的伤心事。她嫁了一个在她看来完美无瑕的人，随后灾祸的潮水毫无征兆地涌来。生下第二个孩子玛伊达之后，丈夫开始喝酒，每纵饮一次，脸色便比先前更苍白一分，直到耻辱临头，随之纵身跳进了北河（the North River）。她的长子——她仅存的指望所在——刚沾着成年的边，便被从她身边带走，出天花死在了医院里。接着，她持有的证券跌了价，贫寒体面人的种种小苦楚随之而来。最后，留给她的那几千块年入似乎也蒸发尽了，她独坐房中时，心思是苦的。那个没心没肺的漂亮姑娘长成了一个女人，硬了心，却不曾认命。四十五岁上，她的头发白了，脸色同她的寡妇帽一样没有血色，她的心死了。
女儿由希尔德雷德太太（Mrs. Hildred）——她寥寥可数的在世亲戚之一——照看着赴会归来，那晚她还没有就寝。那件前文提过的差事，便是希尔德雷德太太的主意，已经替她女儿谋定了；那差事——教养、不如说引荐一位西部人家的孩子进社交界——她已决意要女儿应下。往后呢——唔，玛伊达的前路或许还有别的安排；至于她自己，指望是不多的。她打算隐到康涅狄格（Connecticut）的什么村子里去，在那里等死。
女儿进屋时，她正端坐不动，如一尊雕像。她的眼睛确实不安地转动，脸却哑着；面对那无言的荒凉，姑娘柔软的心被触动了。

===Original===
“Mother!” she exclaimed, “why did you wait up for me?” And she found a seat on the sofa near her mother and took her hand caressingly in her own. “Why are you up so late,” she continued, “are you not tired? Oh, mother,” the girl cried, impetuously, “if you only knew what happened tonight﻿—what do you suppose?”
But Mrs. Barhyte shook her head, she had no thoughts left for suppositions. And quickly, for the mere sake of telling something that would arouse her mother if ever so little from her apathy, Maida related Mr. Incoul’s offer. Her success was greater, if other, than she anticipated. It was as though she had poured into a parching throat the very waters of life. It was the *post tenebras, lux*. And what a light! The incandescence of unexpected hope. A cataract of gold pieces could not have been more dazzling; it was blinding after the shadows in which she had groped. The color came to her cheeks, her hand grew moist. “Yes, yes,” she cried, urging the girl’s narrative with a motion of the head like to that of a jockey speeding to the post; “yes, yes,” she repeated, and her restless eyes flamed with the heat of fever.
“Wasn’t it odd?” Maida concluded abruptly.
“But you accepted him?” the mother asked hoarsely, almost fiercely.
“Accepted him? No, of course not﻿—he﻿—why, mother, what is the matter?”

===Chinese===
“妈妈！”她唤道，“您怎么等着我？”她在母亲身旁的沙发上坐下，把母亲的手爱抚地握在自己手里。“这么晚还不睡，”她接着说，“您不累么？哦，妈妈，”姑娘性急地叫道，“您若知道今晚出了什么事——您猜是什么？”
可巴海特太太摇了摇头，她已没有心思去猜。玛伊达便赶紧——只为讲点什么，好把母亲从麻木里哪怕唤起一丝——把因库尔先生的求婚讲了一遍。她这一炮的效力，比她预期的更大，只是方向不同。那仿佛是把生命之水灌进了一副焦渴的喉咙。正是那“黑暗之后，光明”（*post tenebras, lux*）。而且是怎样的光明！意外之希望的炽焰。倾泻的金币瀑布也不过这样耀眼；在她摸索惯了的阴影之后，这光叫人睁不开眼。血色涌上她的脸颊，她的手心潮了。“是啊，是啊，”她喊道，头一点一点地催着女儿往下讲，活像骑师催马奔向终点柱；“是啊，是啊，”她重复着，那双不安的眼睛烧着热病般的火。
“怪不怪？”玛伊达戛然收了尾。
“可你应了他？”母亲哑着嗓子问，几乎是凶狠狠的。
“应了他？没有，当然没有——他——怎么，妈妈，您这是怎么了？”

===Original===
Engrossed in the telling of her story, the girl had not noticed her mother’s agitation, but at her last words, at the answer to the question, her wrist had been caught as in a vise, and eyes that she no longer recognized﻿—eyes dilated with anger, desperation and revulsion of feeling﻿—were staring into her own. Instinctively she drew back﻿—“Oh, mother, what is it?” And the mother bending forward, even as the daughter retreated, hissed, “You shall accept him﻿—I say you shall!”
“Mother, mother,” the girl moaned, helplessly.
“You shall accept him, do you hear me?”
“But, mother, how can I?” The tears were rolling down her cheeks, she was frightened﻿—the acute, agonizing fright of a child pursued. She tried to free herself, but the hands on her wrist only tightened, and her mother’s face, livid now, was close to her own.
“You shall accept him,” she repeated with the insistence of a monomaniac. And the girl, with bended head, through the paroxysms of her sobs, could only murmur in piteous, beseeching tones, “Mother! mother!”

===Chinese===
姑娘讲得入神，没有留意母亲的激动；可听到最后这句答话，她的手腕已被一把钳住，如夹在老虎钳里，而一双她已认不出的眼睛——因愤怒、绝望与憎厌而睁大的眼睛——直瞪着她。她本能地缩了缩——“哦，妈妈，您怎么了？”母亲向前逼着，女儿向后退着，她嘶声道：“你必须应他——我说，你必须！”
“妈妈，妈妈，”姑娘呻吟着，全无办法。
“你必须应他，听见没有？”
“可是妈妈，我怎么能呢？”泪珠滚滚淌下她的脸颊，她害怕了——那种被人追赶的孩子又急又痛的怕。她想挣脱，钳在腕上的手却越钳越紧，母亲的脸——此刻已经发青——逼到了她的脸前。
“你必须应他，”她带着偏执狂的执拗，一遍遍地重复。姑娘垂着头，在一阵阵抽噎的痉挛里，只能用哀恳的声调低唤：“妈妈！妈妈！”

===Original===
But to the plaint the woman was as deaf as her heart was dumb. She indeed loosened her hold and the girl fell back on the lounge from which they had both arisen, but it was only to summon from the reservoirs of her being some new strength wherewith to vanquish. For a moment she stood motionless, watching the girl quiver in her emotion, and as the sobbing subsided, she stretched forth her hand again, and caught her by the shoulder.
“Look up at me,” she said, and the girl, obedient, rose from her seat and gazed imploringly in her mother’s face. No Neapolitan fishwife was ever more eager to barter her daughter than was this lady of acknowledged piety and refinement, and the face into which her daughter looked and shrank from bore no trace of pity or compassion. “Tell me if you dare,” she continued, “tell me why it is that you refuse? What more do you want? Are you a princess of the blood? Perhaps you will say you don’t love him! And what if you don’t? I loved your father and look at me now! Beside, you have had enough of that﻿—there, don’t stare at me in that way. I know, and so do you. Now take your choice﻿—accept this offer or get to your lover﻿—and this very night. As for me, I disown you, I﻿—”
But the flood of words was interrupted﻿—the girl had fainted. The simulachre of death had extended its kindly arms, and into them she had fallen as into a grateful release.
By the morrow her spirit was broken. Two days later Mr. Incoul called with what success the reader has been already informed, and on that same evening in obedience to the note, came Lenox Leigh.

===Chinese===
可这哀声，那女人充耳不闻，正如她的心哑口无言。她确实松了手，姑娘便跌回两人方才起身的那张长榻上；但那不过是为了从她性命的蓄水池里再唤起一分制胜的新力气。她木立了片刻，看姑娘在激动中发抖；待抽噎渐平，她再度伸出手，一把抓住她的肩。
“抬头看我。”她说。姑娘顺从地起身，哀求地望着母亲的脸。那不勒斯渔妇卖起女儿来，也从来没有比这位众口交称的虔敬文雅的太太更急切；女儿望着而畏缩的那张脸上，没有一丝怜悯或恻隐的痕迹。“你敢就说，”她接着道，“说说你为什么拒绝？你还想要什么？你是金枝玉叶么？或许你要说你不爱他！不爱又怎样？我爱过你父亲，看看我如今这样！再说，那种事你也有了够多的了——喏，别那样瞪着我。我知道，你也知道。如今你自己挑——应下这门亲事，还是找你那情人去——就在今夜。至于我，我没有你这个女儿，我——”
可这一片言语的洪流被打断了——姑娘晕了过去。死的幻影伸出了它仁慈的臂膀，她落进那臂弯里，如同落进一场如释重负的解脱。
到第二天，她的精神已被摧折。两天后，因库尔先生登了门——成败如何，读者已经知道；也就在那天晚上，循着那张便条，伦诺克斯·利来了。

## IV An Evening Call / 第四章 一次晚间来访

===Original===
When Leigh entered the drawing-room he found Miss Barhyte already there. “It is good of you to come,” she said, by way of greeting.
The young man advanced to where she stood, and in a tender, proprietary manner, took her hand in his; he would have kissed her, but she turned her face aside.
“What is it?” he asked; “you are pale as Ophelia.”
“And you, my prince, as inquisitive as Hamlet.”
She led him to a seat and found one for herself. Her eyes rested in his own, and for a moment both were silent.

===Chinese===
利进客厅时，发觉巴海特小姐已在里面。“你来了，真好。”她招呼道。
青年走到她立处，带着一种温柔的、宣示所有权似的姿态，把她的手握进自己手里；他本要吻她，她却把脸偏了过去。
“怎么了？”他问；“你白得像奥菲利娅。”
“你呢，我的殿下，好奇得像哈姆雷特。”
她引他入座，自己也寻了一个位子。她的眼睛栖在他眼里，两人一时都默然。

===Original===
“Lenox,” she asked at last, “do you know Mr. Incoul?”
“Yes, of course; everyone does.”
“I mean do you know him well?”
“I never said ten words to him, nor he to me.”
“So much the better. What do you suppose he did the other evening after you went away?”

===Chinese===
“伦诺克斯，”她终于问，“你认识因库尔先生么？”
“自然认识；谁不认识他。”
“我是说，你同他熟么？”
“我同他统共说过不到十个字，他同我也一样。”
“那就更好。你猜那天晚上你走之后，他做了什么？”

===Original===
“Really, I have no idea, but if you wish me to draw on my imagination, I suppose he went away too.”
“He offered himself.”
“For what?”
“To me.”
“Maida, that mummy! You are joking.”

===Chinese===
“实在说，我毫无所知；不过你若要我动用想象力，我想他也走了吧。”
“他求了婚。”
“求什么？”
“向我。”
“玛伊达，那个木乃伊！你在说笑吧。”

===Original===
“No, I am not joking, nor was he.”
“Well, what then?”
“Then, as you say, he went away.”
“And what did you do?”
“I went away too.”

===Chinese===
“不，我不是说笑，他也不是。”
“哦，后来呢？”
“后来，如你所说，他就走了。”
“那你呢？”
“我也走了。”

===Original===
“Be serious; tell me about it.”
“He came here this afternoon, and I﻿—well﻿—I am to be Mrs. Incoul.”
Lenox bit his lip. Into his face there came an expression of angered resentment. He stood up from his seat; the girl put out her hand as though to stay him: “Lenox, I had to,” she cried. But he paid no attention to her words and crossed the room.
On the mantel before him was a clock that ticked with a low, dolent moan, and for some time he stood looking at it as were it an object of peculiar interest which he had never before enjoyed the leisure to examine. But the clock might have swooned from internal pain, he neither saw nor heard it; his thoughts circled through episodes of the winter back to the forest and the fringes of the summer sea. And slowly the anger gave way to wonder, and presently the wonder faded and in its place there came a sentiment like that of sorrow, a doubled sorrow in whose component parts there was both pity and distress.
It is said that the rich are without appreciation of their wealth until it is lost or endangered, and it was not until that evening that Lenox Leigh appreciated at its worth the loveliness that was slipping from him. He knew then that he might tread the highroads and faubourgs of two worlds with the insistence of the Wandering Jew, and yet find no one so delicious as she. And in the first flood of his anger he felt as were he being robbed, as though the one thing that had lifted him out of the brutal commonplaces of the everyday was being caught up and carried beyond the limits of vision. And into this resentment there came the suspicion that he was not alone being robbed, that he was being cheated to boot, that the love which he had thought to receive as he had seemed to give love before, was an illusory representation, a phantom constructed of phrases.

===Chinese===
“正经些；讲给我听。”
“他今天下午到这儿来了，而我——唔——我就要做因库尔太太了。”
伦诺克斯咬住了嘴唇。他脸上浮起一种怒怨交集的神情。他从座位上站起来；姑娘伸出手，像要拦他：“伦诺克斯，我不得不这样。”她喊道。可他全不理会她的话，径直穿过了屋子。
他面前的壁炉架上有一只钟，滴答作响，声如低低的哀吟。他站在那里望了它半晌，仿佛它是一件特别的有趣之物，他从前竟不曾有闲细细察看。但纵使那钟因内里的疼痛而晕了过去，他也看不见、听不见；他的思绪兜着圈，从冬日的一段段情景，回到森林，回到夏海的边缘。怒气慢慢让位给了惊异；不久惊异也淡了，顶替上来的是一种近乎悲哀的情绪——加倍的悲哀，其中既有怜惜，又有苦痛。
常言道，富人不到财富失去或濒危之时，不知财富之可贵；也是直到那天晚上，伦诺克斯·利才掂出正从他身边溜走的那份可爱的分量。他那时才明白，自己便像流浪的犹太人那样执拗，把两个世界的大路和关厢都踏遍，也寻不出第二个像她这样妙的人儿。在怒潮初涌之际，他觉得仿佛遭了抢劫，仿佛那唯一曾把他从日常的粗鄙平庸里托举起来的东西，正被一把抓起，带出目力所及的范围。这怨愤里又渗进一层疑心：他不但被劫，还兼被骗；他以为照他从前表面付出爱那样收到的爱，原来是一幅虚妄的映像，一个用辞令搭成的幽灵。

===Original===
But this suspicion faded; he knew untold that the girl’s whole heart was his, had been his, was yet his and probably would be his for all of time, till the grave opened and closed again. And then the wonder came. He knew, none better, the purity of her heart, and knowing, too, her gentleness, the sweetness of her nature, her abnegation of self, he began to understand that some tragedy had been enacted which he had not been called upon to witness. Of her circumstances he had been necessarily informed. But in the sensitiveness of her refinement the girl had shrunk from unveiling to a lover’s eyes the increasing miseries of her position, and of the poignancy of those miseries he had now, uninformed, an inkling. If she sold herself, surely it was because the sale was imperative. The white impassible face of the girl’s mother rose before him and then, at once, he understood her cry, “Lenox, I had to.”
As he moved from her, Maida had seen the anger, and knowing the anger to be as just as justice ever is, she shook her head in helpless grief, yet her eyes were tearless as had she no tears left to shed. She had seen the anger, but ignorant of the phases of thought by which it had been transfigured she stole up to where he stood and touched his arm with a shrinking caress.
He turned and would have caught her to him, but she drew back, elusively, as might a swan. “No, not that, Lenox. Only say that you do not hate me. Lenox, if you only knew. To me it is bitterer than death. You are the whole world to me, yet never must I see you again. If I could but tell you all. If I could but tell him all, if there were anything that I could do or say, but there is nothing, nothing,” she added pensively, “except submission.”
Her voice had sunk into a whisper: she was pleading as much with herself as with him. Her arms were pendant and her eyes downcast. On the mantel the clock kept up its low, dolorous moan, as though in sympathy with her woe. “Nothing,” she repeated.
“But surely it need not be. Things cannot be so bad as that﻿—Maida, I cannot lose you. If nothing else can be done, let us go away; at its best New York is tiresome; we could both leave it without a regret or a wish to return. And then, there is Italy; we have but to choose. Why, I could take a palace on the Grand Canal for less than I pay for my rooms at the Cumberland. And you would love Venice; and in winter there is Capri and Sorrento and Palermo. I have known days in Palermo when I seemed to be living in a haze of turquoise and gold. And the nights! You should see the nights! The stars are large as lilies! See, it would be so easy; in a fortnight we could be in Genoa, and before we got there we would have been forgotten.”

===Chinese===
但这疑心淡去了；他心底深知，姑娘的整颗心是他的，一直是他的，仍旧是他的，多半直到时间的尽头、坟墓开了又合，也还是他的。接着惊异来了。她的心地何等纯净，他知道得比谁都清楚；又知道她的温婉、她天性的甜美、她的舍己，他开始懂得：有一场他未被召去目睹的悲剧演过了。她的境况，他自然是被告知过的。但她那精致的敏感，使她羞于向情人的眼睛揭开自己处境中日甚一日的困窘；而那些困窘有多刺心，他此刻虽不知情，也有了点预感。她若出卖自己，那必定因为这出卖势在必行。姑娘母亲那张惨白无感的脸浮现在他眼前，随即，他一下子懂了她那声喊：“伦诺克斯，我不得不这样。”
他从她身边走开时，玛伊达已看见了那怒气；也知道这怒气正如公道向来那样公道，她便无助而悲戚地摇了摇头，眼里却没有泪，仿佛再无泪可落。她看见的是怒气，却不知道那怒气已几经蜕化；她悄悄挨到他所立的地方，带着畏缩的爱抚，碰了碰他的手臂。
他转过身，本要把她揽进怀里，她却闪开了，难以捉摸地，像一只天鹅。“不，别这样，伦诺克斯。只消说你不恨我。伦诺克斯，你若知道就好了。这于我比死更苦。你是我的整个世界，可我从此再不能见你。但愿我能把一切都告诉你。但愿我能把一切都告诉他——倘若还有什么我能做、能说的；可是没有，什么都没有，”她幽幽地补了一句，“只有顺从。”
她的声音低成了耳语：她既在恳求他，同样也在恳求自己。两臂垂着，眼睛低着。壁炉架上，那钟继续着它低低的哀吟，仿佛在同情她的不幸。“什么都没有。”她重复道。
“可事情不一定非这样不可。情形总不至于糟到那地步——玛伊达，我不能失去你。倘若别无他法，我们就走；纽约再好也不过腻人；我们两个都可以毫无留恋、也不想回头地离开它。再说，还有意大利；我们只消挑一挑。嗐，大运河（Grand Canal）边上一座宫殿，租金还比不上我在坎伯兰（Cumberland）的客房钱多。你会爱上威尼斯的；冬天还有卡普里、索伦托、巴勒莫。我在巴勒莫经历过那样的日子：人仿佛活在绿松石与金子的薄雾里。还有夜晚！你真该看看那些夜晚！星星大得像百合花！瞧，这多容易；两星期后我们就能到热那亚，不等我们到，人家早把我们忘了。”

===Original===
He was bending forward speaking rapidly, persuasively, half hoping, half fearing, she would accept. She did not interrupt him, and he continued impetuously, as though intoxicated on his own words.
“When we are tired of the South, there are the lakes and that lovely Tyrol; there will be so much to do, so much to see. After New York, we shall really seem to live; and then, beyond, is Munich﻿—you are sure to love that city.” He hated Munich; he hated Germany. The entire land, and everything that was in it, was odious to him; but for the moment he forgot. He would have said more, even to praises of Berlin, but the girl raised her ringless hand and shook her head wearily.
“No, Lenox, it may not be. Did I go with you, in a year﻿—six months, perhaps﻿—we would both regret. It would be not only expatriation; it would, for me at least, be isolation as well, and, though I would bear willingly with both, you would not. You think so now, perhaps, I do not doubt”﻿—and a phantom of a smile crossed her face﻿—“and I thank you for so thinking, but it may not be.”
Her hand fell to her side, and she turned listlessly away. “You must forget me, Lenox﻿—but not too soon, will you?”
“Never, sweetheart﻿—never!”

===Chinese===
他俯身向前，说得又快又恳切，一半盼着她应允，一半又怕她应允。她不打断他，他便滔滔地接下去，仿佛被自己的言语灌醉了。
“待我们玩腻了南方，还有湖区，还有那可爱的蒂罗尔；要做的那么多，要看的那么多。离了纽约，我们才算真正活着；再过去，就是慕尼黑——你准会爱上那座城。”他恨慕尼黑；他恨德国。那整片国土，连同国土上的一切，他都厌恶之至；可此刻他忘了。他还想多说些，甚至夸一夸柏林，姑娘却举起那只没有戒指的手，倦倦地摇了摇头。
“不，伦诺克斯，这是不行的。我若跟你去了，不出一年——也许六个月——我们两个都要后悔。那不只是流落异国；至少于我，还要加上与世隔绝。两样我都甘愿忍受，你却不能。你如今或许这样想，我不怀疑”——一丝幽灵似的笑掠过她的脸——“我也谢谢你这样想；可这是不行的。”
她的手垂回身侧，她懒懒地转过身去。“你必须忘了我，伦诺克斯——只是别太快，好么？”
“绝不，心肝——绝不！”

===Original===
“Ah, but you must. And I must learn to forget you. It will be difficult. No one can be to me what you have been. You have been my youth, Lenox; my girlhood has been yours. I have nothing left. Nothing except regrets﻿—regrets that youth should pass so quickly and that girlhood comes but once.”
Her lips were tremulous, but she was trying to be brave.
“But surely, Maida, it cannot be that we are to part forever. Afterwards﻿—” the word was vague, but they both understood﻿—“afterwards I may see you. Such things often are. Because you feel yourself compelled to this step, there is no reason why I, of all others, should be shut out of your life.”
“It is the fact of your being the one of all others that makes the shutting needful.”
“It shall not be.”

===Chinese===
“啊，可你必须。我也得学着忘掉你。那会很难。谁也不能像你这样在我生命里存在过。你是我的青春，伦诺克斯；我的少女时代是你的。我一无所有了。除了悔恨——悔恨青春去得这样快，悔恨少女时代一生只有一次。”
她的嘴唇颤着，可她在努力坚强。
“可是玛伊达，我们总不至于就此永别。往后——”这词含糊，两人却都明白——“往后我还能见你。这样的事多得很。你既觉得这一步出于不得已，却没有理由偏偏把我，把我一个人，关在你的生活之外。”
“正因为你就是那一个人，这扇门才非关不可。”
“关不得。”

===Original===
“Lenox,” she pleaded, “it is harder for me than for you.”
“But how can you ask me, how can you think that I will give you up? The affair is wretched enough as it is, and now, by insisting that I am not to see you again, you would make it even worse. People think it easy to love, but it is not; I know nothing more difficult. You are the only one for whom I have ever cared. It was not difficult to do so, I admit, but the fact remains. I have loved you, I have loved you more and more every day, and now, when I love you most, when I love you as I can never love again, you find it the easiest matter in the world to come to me and say, ‘It’s ended; bonjour.’ ”
“You are cruel, Lenox, you are cruel.”
“It is you that are cruel, and there the wonder is, for your cruelty is unconscious, of your own free will you would not know how.”
“It is not that I am cruel, it is that I am trying to do right. And it is for you to aid me. I have been true to you, do not ask me now to be false to myself.”

===Chinese===
“伦诺克斯，”她恳求道，“这于我比于你更难。”
“可你怎么能这样要求我，怎么会以为我肯放弃你？这桩事本身已经够惨了，如今你又执意不许我再见你，把它弄得更惨。人们当爱是容易的事，其实不然；我不知道还有比它更难的。你是我唯一在乎过的人。在乎并不难，我承认，可事实总归是事实。我爱过你，一天比一天更爱；如今，在我最爱的时候，在我此生再不能如此爱的时候，你却觉得世上最容易的事，莫过于走到我跟前说一声：‘完了；日安（bonjour）。’”
“你真狠，伦诺克斯，你真狠。”
“狠的是你；奇怪也奇怪在这里，因为你的狠是无心的，若凭你自己的本心，你才不会呢。”
“我不是狠，我是想做得对。这正要你来帮我。我从未负过你，如今别叫我自己负自己。”

===Original===
If at that moment Mrs. Bunker Hill could have looked into the girl’s face, her suspicions would have vanished into air. Maida needed only a less fashionable gown to look like a medieval saint; and before the honesty that was in her eyes Lenox bowed his head.
“Will you help me?”
“I will,” he answered.
“I knew you would; you are too good to try to make me more miserable than I am. And now, you must go; kiss me, it is the last time.”
He caught her in his arms and kissed her full upon the mouth. He kissed her wet eyes, her cheeks, the splendor of her hair. And after a moment of the acutest pain of all her life, the girl freed herself from his embrace, and let him go without another word.

===Chinese===
倘若那一刻邦克山太太能看一眼姑娘的脸，她的疑心早化到空气里去了。玛伊达只消换一件不那么时髦的衣裳，便像一位中世纪的圣女；在她眼中那片诚实的面前，伦诺克斯低下了头。
“你肯帮我么？”
“我肯。”他答道。
“我知道你肯的；你心太好，不肯让我比现在更苦。如今，你走吧；吻我，这是最后一次。”
他把她搂进怀里，结结实实吻在她唇上。他吻她湿润的眼睛，她的脸颊，她那一头灿烂的头发。在承受过她一生中最锐痛的一刻之后，姑娘从他怀抱里挣脱出来，放他走了，再没有一句话。

## V A Yellow Envelope / 第五章 一只黄信封

===Original===
There is a peculiarity about Baden-Baden which no other watering-place seems to share﻿—it has the aroma of a pretty woman. In August it is warm, crowded, enervating, tiresome as are all warm and crowded places, but the air is delicately freighted and a pervasive fragrance is discerned even by the indifferent.
In the summer that succeeded Maida’s marriage Baden was the same tame, perfumed *zwei und funfzig* that it has ever been since the war. The ladies and gentlemen who were to regard it as a sort of continuation of the Bois de Boulogne had departed never to return. Gone was Benazet, gone, too, the click of the roulette ball. The echoes and uproars of the Second Empire had died away, as echoes and uproars ever must, and in place of the paint and cleverness of the *dames du-lac* had come the stupid loveliness of the *schwärmerisch Mädchen*.
But though Paris had turned her wicked back, the attitude of that decadent capital in no wise affected other cities. On the particular August to which allusion is made, interminable dinners were consumed by contingents from the politest lands, and also from some that were semi-barbaric.
In the Lichenthal Allée and on the promenade in front of the Kursaal one could hear six languages in as many minutes, and given a polyglottic ear the number could have been increased to ten. Among those who added their little quota to this summer Babel were Mr. and Mrs. Incoul.
The wedding had been very simple. Mrs. Barhyte had wished the ceremony performed in Grace Church, and to the ceremony she had also wished that all New York should be bidden. To her it represented a glory which in the absence of envious witnesses would be lustreless indeed. But in this respect her wishes were disregarded. On a melting morning in early June, a handful of people, thirty at most, assembled in Mrs. Hildred’s drawing-room. The grave service that is in usage among Episcopalians was mumbled by a diligent bishop, there was a hurried and heavy breakfast, and two hours later the bride and groom were on the deck of the “Umbria.”

===Chinese===
巴登-巴登（Baden-Baden）有一桩别处温泉胜地似乎都没有的特色——它浑身是一位美女的香气。八月里它温暖、拥挤、令人倦怠、惹人厌烦，同一切温暖拥挤的地方一样；可它的空气载着淡淡的馨香，连最无所谓的人也闻得出一股无所不在的芬芳。
玛伊达成婚后的那个夏天，巴登依旧是开战以来那个温驯熏香的老一套 *zwei und funfzig*（德语：五十二）。那些把它视作布洛涅森林（Bois de Boulogne）某种延伸的绅士淑女们，早已一去不返。贝纳泽（Benazet）去了，轮盘珠的脆响也去了。第二帝国的回声与喧嚣消歇了，回声与喧嚣向来如此；*dames du-lac*（“湖畔贵妇”，赌场雇来点缀的女子）的脂粉与机锋不见了，换上了 *schwärmerisch Mädchen*（德语：多情狂想的少女）那种痴憨的可人。
不过巴黎虽然把她邪恶的脊背转了过去，这座颓靡都城的姿态却全然影响不了别的城市。上文提到的那个八月里，来自最有礼之邦、也来自几个半蛮荒之邦的队伍，把一场场没完没了的宴席吃了下去。
在利希滕塔尔林荫道（Lichenthal Allée）上，在库尔豪斯（Kursaal）前的散步场里，几分钟便听得到六种语言；耳朵若是通晓多语，数目尽可以加到十。给这座夏日巴别塔添上自己一小份的，就有因库尔先生和因库尔太太。
婚礼办得很简素。巴海特太太本想在恩典堂（Grace Church）行礼，还想请全纽约都来观礼。在她看来，那是一场荣耀；没有艳羡的观礼者，这荣耀实在要黯然失色。可这方面她的愿望落了空。六月初一个暖得化冻的早晨，一小撮人——至多三十位——聚在希尔德雷德太太的客厅里。一位勤勉的主教咕哝着圣公会惯用的庄重仪文，接着是一顿仓促而丰盛的早餐，两小时后，新郎新娘已站在“乌姆布里亚号”（“Umbria”）的甲板上了。

===Original===
The entire affair had been conducted with the utmost dispatch. The *Sunday Sun* chronicled the engagement in one issue, and gave the date of the wedding in the next. It was not so much that Harmon Incoul was ardent in his wooing or that Miss Barhyte was anxious to assume the rank and privileges that belong to the wedded state. The incentives were other if equally prosaic. The ceremony if undergone needed to be undergone at once. Summer was almost upon them, and in the code which society has made for itself, summer weddings are reproved. There was indeed some question of postponing the rites until autumn. But on that Mrs. Barhyte put her foot. She was far from sure of her daughter, and as for the other contracting party, who could tell but that he might change his mind. Such changes had been, and instances of such misconduct presented themselves unsummoned to the woman’s mind. The fish had been landed almost without effort, a fish more desirable than any other, a very prize among fishes, and the possibility that he might slip away and without so much as a gill awry float off into clearer and less troubled seas, nerved her to her task anew.
In the interview which she enjoyed with her prospective son-in-law she was careful, however, to display no eagerness. She was sedate when sedateness seemed necessary, but her usual attitude was one of conciliatory disinterestedness. Her daughter’s choice she told him had met with her fullest approval, and it was to her a matter of deep regret that neither her husband nor her father﻿—the late Chief Justice Hildred, with whose name Mr. Incoul was of course familiar﻿—that neither of them had been spared to join in the expression of her satisfaction. Of Maida it was unnecessary to speak, yet this at least should be said, she was young and she was impressionable, as young people are apt to be, but she had never given her mother cause for the slightest vexation, not the slightest. “She is a sweet girl,” Mrs. Barhyte went on to say, “and one with an admirable disposition; she takes after her father in that, but she has her grandfather’s intellect.”
“Her beauty, madam, comes from you.”
To this Mrs. Barhyte assented. “She is pretty,” she said, and then in the voice of an actress who feels her role, “Do be good to her,” she pleaded, “she is all I have.”
Mr. Incoul assured her that on that score she need give herself no uneasiness, and a few days before the wedding, begged as a particular favor to himself that after the ceremony she would take up her residence in his house. The servants, he explained, had been instructed in that respect, and a checkbook of the Chemical Bank would be handed her in defrayment of all expenses. “And to think,” Mrs. Barhyte muttered to herself, “to think that I might have died in Connecticut!”

===Chinese===
整个事情办得雷厉风行。《星期日太阳报》（*Sunday Sun*）在一期里登了订婚，下一期便登了婚期。这倒不是因为哈蒙·因库尔求偶心热，也不是巴海特小姐急着想戴上已婚身份的头衔与特权。动机是别样的，虽然同样平淡无奇。礼既然要行，就得立刻行。夏天眼看就到，而社交界自立的章程里，夏日成婚是要遭非议的。本来确实有过把婚礼推迟到秋天之议。但这上头巴海特太太踩了脚。她对自己女儿毫无把握；至于另一位当事者，谁保得定他不回心转意。这样的变卦从前有过，这类失德的事例不请自来地涌上妇人心头。鱼几乎是毫不费力钓上了岸——比哪条都称心的一条鱼，鱼中真正的锦标；而它仍有可能脱钩而去，连鳃都不歪一下，漂进更清、更平静的海里去——这可能性又给她添了一把劲。
在她同未来女婿的那次晤谈里，她却小心着不露一点急切。该端凝的时候她端凝，但她平日的态度是一种抚慰性的漠不关心。她告诉他，女儿的选择得到了她百分之百的赞同；只是深以为憾的是，她的丈夫同她的父亲——已故首席大法官希尔德雷德（Chief Justice Hildred），这名字因库尔先生当然熟稔——两位都不曾留得下来，同她一道表示满意。玛伊达原不必说，不过至少有一句该讲：她年轻，她易动感情，如年轻人惯常的那样，但她从未给过母亲一丝一毫的烦忧，一丝一毫也没有。“她是个甜美的姑娘，”巴海特太太接着说，“性情再好没有；这一点随她父亲，脑子却随她外祖父。”
“她的美貌，太太，却是得自您。”
这话巴海特太太首肯了。“她是俊，”她说，随即换上一副入了戏的女伶的嗓子，“千万待她好，”她恳求道，“她是我仅有的一切。”
因库尔先生向她担保，这一层她尽可放心；婚礼前几日，又把她住进自己宅中一事，当作给他本人的特别恩宠恳求了下来。他解释说，仆人们已在那上头得了吩咐，一本化学银行（Chemical Bank）的支票簿也将交到她手里，以支付一切开销。“想想看，”巴海特太太自语道，“想想看，我差一点就死在康涅狄格了！”

===Original===
The voyage over was precisely like any other. There were six days of discomfort in the open, and between Queenstown and Liverpool unnumbered hours of gloomy and irritating delay. Mrs. Incoul grew weary of the captain’s cabin and her husband was not enthusiastic on the subject of the quarters which the first officer had relinquished to him. But in dear old London, as all good Americans are wont to call that delightful city, Mrs. Incoul’s spirits revived. The difference between Claridge’s and Rodick’s would have interested one far more apathetic than she, and as she had never before set her foot on Piccadilly, and as Rotten Row and Regent Circus were as unfamiliar to her as the banks of the Yang-tse-Kiang, she had none of that satiated feeling of the dejà-vu which besets the majority of us on our travels.
The notice of their arrival in the *Morning Post* had been followed by cards without limit and invitations without stint. An evening gazette published an editorial a column in length, in which after an historical review of wealth from Plutus to the Duke of Westminster, the reader learned that the world had probably never seen a man so rich and yet seemingly so unconscious of the power which riches give as was Harmon Incoul, esq., of New York, U.S.A.
During the few weeks that were passed in London the bride and groom were bidden to more crushes, dinners and garden parties than Maida had attended during the entire course of her bud-hood. There was the inevitable presentation and as the girl’s face was noticeably fair she and her husband were made welcome at Marlborough House. Afterwards, yet before the season drooped, there was a trip to Paris, a city, which, after the splendors of London, seemed cheap and tawdry indeed, and then as already noted came the *villeggiatura* at Babel-Baden.
Meanwhile Maida had come and gone, eaten and fasted, danced and driven in a constant chase after excitement. To her husband she had acted as she might have done to some middle-aged cousin with whom she was not precisely on that which is termed a familiar footing, one on whom chance not choice had made her dependent, and to whom in consequence much consideration was due. But her relations will be perhaps better understood when it is related that she had not found herself physically capable of calling him by his given name, or in fact anything else than You. It was not that she disliked him, on the contrary, in many ways he was highly sympathetic, but the wellsprings of her affection had been dried, and the season of their refreshment was yet obscure.
In the face of this halfhearted platonism Mr. Incoul had displayed a wisdom which was peculiar to himself; he exacted none of those little tributes which are conceded to be a husband’s due, and he allowed himself none of the familiarities which are reported to be an appanage of the married state. From the beginning he had determined to win his wife by the exercise of that force which, given time and opportunity, a strong nature invariably exerts over a weaker one. He was indulgent but he was also austere. The ordering of one gown or of five hundred was a matter of which he left her sole mistress. Had she so desired she might have bought a jewelry shop one day and given it back as a free gift on the morrow. But on a question of ethics he allowed no appeal. The Countess of Ex, a lady of dishonor at a popular court, had, during the London season, issued cards for a ball. On the evening on which it was to take place the bride and groom had dined at one house, and gone to a musicale at another. When leaving the latter entertainment Maida told her husband to tell the man “Park Lane.” Mr. Incoul, however, ordered the carriage to be driven to the hotel.

===Chinese===
横渡的航程同哪一次都毫无二致。露天的甲板上熬了六天不舒服的日子，昆斯敦与利物浦之间又搭上数不清的钟头阴郁恼人的耽搁。因库尔太太坐腻了船长室，她丈夫对大副让给他的那间舱房也提不起兴致。可是一到亲爱的老伦敦——凡是善良的美国人惯于这样称呼那座迷人的城——因库尔太太的精神便复活了。克拉里奇（Claridge's）同罗迪克的差别，比她冷淡得多的人也要发生兴趣；她从未踏过皮卡迪利，罗滕罗骑马道与摄政环岛之于她，同扬子江两岸一样陌生，于是她也就全然没有那种似曾相识（déjà-vu）的餍足之感——我们多数人旅行时都免不了它。
他们抵达的消息在《晨报》（*Morning Post*）上登出之后，名片便无穷无尽地涌来，请帖也毫无节制。一家晚报刊出一篇整栏的社论，在把财富从普路托斯（Plutus）到威斯敏斯特公爵（Duke of Westminster）作了一番历史的巡礼之后，读者方才知道：美利坚合众国纽约的哈蒙·因库尔先生这样有钱、却似乎对钱财所予的权势这样浑然不觉的人，世上恐怕还不曾见过。
在伦敦度过的那几个星期里，新婚夫妇应邀参加的拥挤招待会、宴席和游园会，比玛伊达整个初入社交界的花季里参加的还多。觐见是免不了的；姑娘的容貌又出挑地美，夫妇俩在马尔巴勒宫（Marlborough House）也大受欢迎。随后，还在社交季凋萎之前，去了巴黎一游——领略过伦敦的繁华之后，那座城显得着实寒伧而俗艳——接着，如前所述，便到巴别·巴登来过 *villeggiatura*（意语：避暑闲居）了。
其间，玛伊达来了又去，吃了又斋，跳了舞又坐了车，无休止地追逐着刺激。对丈夫，她的做派如同对某位中年的表亲——既谈不上什么亲近，又是机缘而非选择使她倚傍于他，故而礼数上分外周到。但说出这样一件事，两人的关系或许更易明白：她发觉自己在生理上竟叫不出他的教名，其实除了“你”之外什么也叫不出口。这倒不是她厌恶他；恰恰相反，在许多方面他极可亲；只是她感情的泉眼已经干涸，几时才能重新涌出，还渺茫得很。
面对这种三心二意的柏拉图式婚姻，因库尔先生显出了一种他独有的明智；他并不索取那些公认属于丈夫应得的小小供奉，也不给自己那些据说属于已婚状态附属品的亲昵。从一开始，他就决意凭那种力量去赢得妻子——只要有时间有机会，强悍的天性对柔弱的天性总要施出的那种力量。他纵容，却也严峻。定一件衣裳还是五百件，这等事他让她全权做主。她若高兴，今天买下一家珠宝铺、明天原样奉还也无不可。可在是非问题上，他不容上诉。埃克斯伯爵夫人（the Countess of Ex）——一个时兴宫廷里的失节贵妇——在伦敦社交季中发过一场舞会的请帖。舞会那晚，新婚夫妇在一户人家用了晚餐，又去另一户听了一场音乐晚会。离开后一处时，玛伊达吩咐丈夫告诉车夫“帕克巷”。因库尔先生却吩咐把车赶回旅馆。

===Original===
“Did you not understand me?” she asked. “I am going to the Countess of Ex’s.”
“She is not a woman whom I care to have you know,” he replied.
“But the Prince is to be there!”
To this he assented. “Perhaps.” And then he added in a voice that admitted of no further argument, “But not my wife.”
Maida sank back in the carriage startled by an unexperienced emotion. For the first time since the wedding she could have kissed the man whose name she bore. It was in this way that matters shaped themselves.

===Chinese===
“你没听懂我的话么？”她问，“我要去埃克斯伯爵夫人家。”
“她不是那种我愿意让你结交的女人。”他答道。
“可亲王殿下要到场呢！”
这话他认了。“也许。”随后他用一种不容再辩的嗓音加了一句：“但不是我的妻子。”
玛伊达靠回车座上，被一种从未经验过的情绪惊住了。婚礼以来，她头一回几乎想亲吻这个自己冠着他的姓的男人。事情就是这样成了形的。

===Original===
Soon after reaching Paris, Mr. Blydenburg called. He had brought his daughter abroad because he did not know what else to do with her, and now that he was on the Continent he did not know what to do with himself. He explained these preoccupations and Mr. Incoul suggested that in the general exodus they should all go to Germany. To this suggestion Blydenburg gave a ready assent and that very day purchased a translation of Tacitus, a copy of Mr. Baring-Gould’s *Germany*, a Baedeker, and a remote edition of Murray.
At the appointed date the little party started for Cologne, where, after viewing a bone of the fabulous virgin Undecemilla, they drifted to Frankfurt and from there reached the Oos. In Baden, Blydenburg and his daughter elected domicile at the Englischerhof, while through the foresight of a courier, good-looking, polyglottic, idle and useful, the Incouls found a spacious apartment in the Villa Wilhelmina, a belonging of the Mesmer House.
In the drawer of the table which Maida selected as a suitable place for superfluous rings was a yellow envelope addressed to the Gräfin von Adelsburg. On the back was an attempt at addition, a double column of figures which evidently represented the hotel expenses of the lady to whom the envelope was addressed. The figures were marked carefully that no mistake should be possible, but the sum total had been jotted down in hurried numerals, as though the mathematician had been irritated at the amount, while under all, in an indignant scrawl, was the legend “S. T.”
Maida was the least inquisitive of mortals, but one evening, a week or ten days after her arrival, when she happened to be sitting in company with the Blydenburgs and her husband on the broad terrace that fronts the Kursaal, she alluded, for the mere sake of conversation, to the envelope which she had found. The Gräfin von Adelsburg it then appeared was the name with which the Empress of a neighboring realm was accustomed to veil her rank, and the legend it was suggested could only stand for *schrecklich teuer*, frightfully dear. The Empress had vacated the Villa Wilhelmina but a short time before and it seemed not improbable that the figures and conclusion were in her own imperial hand.
While this subject was under discussion the Prince of Albion sauntered down the walk. He was a handsome man, with blue projecting eyes, somewhat stout, perhaps, but not obese. In his train were two ladies and a few men. As he was about to pass Mrs. Incoul he stopped and raised his hat. It was of soft felt, she noticed, and his coat was tailless. He uttered a few amiable commonplaces and then moved on. The terrace had become very crowded. The little party had found seats near the musicians, and from either side came a hum of voices. A Saxon halted before them, designating with pointing finger the retreating back of the Prince, his companion, a pinguid woman who looked as though she lived on fish, shouted, “*Herr Jesus! ist es ja möglich*,” and hurried on for a closer view. Nearby was a group of Brazilians and among them a pretty girl in a fantastic gown, whose voice was like the murmur of birds. To the left were some Russians conversing in a hard, cruel French. The girl seemed to have interested them. “But why,” asked one, “but why is it that she wears such loud colors?” To which another, presumably the wit of the party, answered idly, “Who knows, she may be deaf.” And immediately behind Mrs. Incoul were two young Americans, wonderfully well dressed, who were exchanging chaste anecdotes and recalling recent adventures with an accompaniment of smothered laughter that was fathomless in its good-fellowship.

===Chinese===
到巴黎不久，布莱登堡先生来拜访了。他带女儿出国，是因为不知道拿她还能怎么办；如今身在欧洲，又不知道拿自己怎么办。他陈述了这两桩心事，因库尔先生便提议：趁着大家都在出走，不如一同去德国。这提议布莱登堡一口应承，当天便买了一部塔西佗（Tacitus）的译本、一部巴林-古尔德先生（Mr. Baring-Gould）的《德国》（*Germany*）、一本贝德克尔旅行指南（Baedeker），还有一版久远的默里（Murray）。
约定之日，这一小队人马向科隆进发；看过传说中的童女翁德切米拉（Undecemilla）的一块骨头之后，便漂到法兰克福，再从那里到了奥斯河（the Oos）畔。在巴登，布莱登堡父女选定英国旅馆（Englischerhof）下榻；而因库尔夫妇靠一位随行侍从（courier）的先见——他漂亮、通几国语言、闲散而有用——在威廉明娜别墅（Villa Wilhelmina）寻到一套宽敞的居所，那是梅斯默旅馆（Mesmer House）的产业。
玛伊达挑了一张桌子，把抽屉当作多余戒指的合适去处；抽屉里却有一只黄色信封，抬头写给阿德斯堡伯爵夫人（Gräfin von Adelsburg）。信封背面是一番加法的尝试，两栏数字，显然是信封抬头那位夫人的旅馆开销。数字一个个标得仔细，唯恐出错；总数却是急草草的几笔，仿佛那位演算者被数目惹恼了；而在这一切底下，一行怒气冲冲的涂写：“S. T.”
玛伊达是世上最少打听的人；可是到后一周或十天的某个晚上，她恰同布莱登堡父女与丈夫坐在库尔豪斯前那片宽阔的露台上，为着有话可说，便提起了那只信封。原来，阿德斯堡伯爵夫人是一位邻邦皇后惯用来遮掩身份的名字；那行题记，有人断言，只能是 *schrecklich teuer*（德语：贵得吓人）。皇后不久前才腾出威廉明娜别墅，那两栏数字和那个结论出自她御笔亲书，看来并非不可能。
正议论着这话题，阿尔比恩亲王（the Prince of Albion）沿小径踱了下来。他是个俊美的男人，一双湛蓝的暴眼，或许略胖，却不算臃肿。随行的是两位夫人和几个男人。他正要从因库尔太太身边经过时，停了步，举了举帽。那帽子是软毡的，她留意到，他的上衣没有下摆。他讲了几句和蔼的常谈，便继续前行。露台已挤得满满当当。这一小队人马在乐师近旁坐定，两侧都是一片人语嗡嗡。一个萨克森人在他们面前站住，伸手指着亲王远去的背影；他的同伴，一个胖得流油、看那样子像专靠鱼活着的女人，嚷了一声：“*Herr Jesus! ist es ja möglich*（主耶稣！这竟可能么）”，便匆匆赶去近前细看。近旁是一群巴西人，其中有个穿奇装异服的漂亮姑娘，嗓音像鸟雀的啁啾。左边是几个俄国人，用一种生硬冷酷的法语交谈。那姑娘似乎引起了他们的兴趣。“可是为什么，”一个问，“可是为什么她要穿这样扎眼的颜色？”另一个——想必是那伙人里的俏皮人——懒懒地答：“谁知道呢，兴许她是聋子。”紧挨因库尔太太身后坐着两个年轻的美国人，衣着一等一的讲究，正交换着干净的趣闻，追述着新近的艳遇，伴着一阵强按下去的笑，那笑声里的交情深不见底。

===Original===
Maida paid no attention to the conversation about her. She was thinking of the yellow envelope, and for the first time she began to form some conception of her husband’s wealth. Apparently he thought nothing of prices that seemed exorbitant to one whose coffers notoriously overflowed. She had never spoken to him about money, nor he to her; she knew merely that his purse was open; yet, as is usual with one who has been obliged to count the pennies, she had in her recent shopping often hesitated and refused to buy. In Paris she had chaffered over handkerchiefs and been alarmed at Doucet’s bill. Indeed at Virot’s when she told that poetic milliner what she wished to pay for a bonnet, Virot, smiling almost with condescension, had said to her, “The *chapeau* that madame wants is surely a *chapeau en Espagne*.”
And now for the first time she began to understand. She saw how much was hers, how ungrudgingly it was given, how easy her path was made, how pleasant it might be for the rest of her days, and she half-turned and looked at her husband. If she could only forget, she thought, only forget and begin anew. If she could but tell him all! She moaned to herself. The moon was shining behind the Kursaal and in the air was the usual caress. The musicians, who had just attacked and subdued the *Meistersänger*, began a sob of Weber’s that had been strangled into a waltz, and as the measures flowed they brought her that pacification which music alone can bring.
The past was over and done, ill-done, she knew, but above it might grow such weeds of forgetfulness as would hide it even from herself. In a semi-unconsciousness of her surroundings she stared like a pretty sphinx into the future. The waltz swooned in its ultimate accords, but she had ceased to hear; it had lulled and left her; her thoughts roamed far off into distant possibilities; she was dreaming with eyes wide open.
Abruptly the orchestra attacked a score that was seasoned with red pepper﻿—the cancan of an opéra-bouffe: the notes exploded like firecrackers, and in the explosion brought vistas of silk stockings, whirlwinds of disordered skirts, the heat and frenzy of an orgy. And then, as the riot mounted like a flame, suddenly in a clash and shudder of brass the uproar ceased.
Maida, aroused from her revery by the indecency of the music, looked idly about her. The Russians were drinking beer that was as saffron as their own faces. The Brazilians had departed. The young Americans were smoking Bond Street cigarettes which they believed to be Egyptian, and discussing the relative merits of Hills and Poole.

===Chinese===
四下的谈话玛伊达一概没有留心。她在想那只黄信封，并且头一回开始对自己丈夫的财富有了些概念。那些在一个公认金银满库的人看来都嫌过分的价钱，他显然不当一回事。她从未同他谈过钱，他也从未同她谈过；她只知道他的钱袋是敞开的；然而，凡是数惯了铜板的人都这样，她新近买东西时还常常迟疑，常常不肯买。在巴黎，她为手帕讨过价，见了杜塞（Doucet）的账单吓了一跳。在维罗（Virot）店里，当这位诗人似的女帽商听她讲出想为一顶帽子付的价钱时，维罗带着近乎居高临下的微笑对她说：“太太要的那顶 *chapeau*（帽子），想必是一顶 *chapeau en Espagne*（化用‘西班牙城堡’，意即虚渺难求之物）了。”
此刻她才第一次开始懂得。她看见属于自己的何其多，给得何等不吝惜，她的路铺得何等平顺，余生又可能何等安逸；她便侧过一点身子，看了看丈夫。但愿能忘掉，她想，忘掉，从头来过。但愿能把一切都告诉他！她在心里呻吟。月亮照在库尔豪斯背后，空气里是惯常的爱抚。乐师们刚攻克并降服了《名歌手》（*Meistersänger*），又奏起韦伯的一段呜咽，被掐成了圆舞曲；曲声流转，带给她只有音乐才能带来的那种平静。
过去是完了，做下了——她知道，做得不好——但那上面未尝不能长出健忘的荒草，把它连她自己都遮没。她对四周陷入半昏迷的状态，像一尊美丽的司芬克斯般凝望着将来。圆舞曲在最后几组和弦里昏然睡去，她却已听而不闻；它哄过了她，又撇下她；她的思绪漫游到遥远的机会里去了；她睁着一双大眼在做梦。
冷不防，乐队奏起一支撒了红辣椒的谱子——一出轻歌剧（opéra-bouffe）里的康康舞：音符像爆竹般炸开，炸开处展现出丝袜的重重景象、裙裾纷乱的旋风、狂欢的热与狂。随后，那乱腾如火焰般越蹿越高时，铜钹一声铿锵震颤，喧嚣戛然而止。
音乐这般不成体统，把玛伊达从冥想里惊起，她懒懒地四下望了望。俄国人喝着藏红花色的啤酒，同他们自己的脸一个颜色。巴西人走了。两个美国青年抽着邦德街的香烟——他们只当是埃及烟——正讨论着希尔斯（Hills）与普尔（Poole）两家裁缝的短长。

===Original===
“While I was getting measured for that topcoat you liked so much,” said one, “Leigh came in.”
“Lee? What Lee? Sumpter?”
“No; Lenox Leigh.”
“Did he, though? How was he?”
“Finest form. Said he would take in Paris and Baden. He may be here now for all I know. Let’s ask the waiter for a *Fremden-List*.”

===Chinese===
“我正量那件你顶喜欢的大衣的时候，”一个说，“利进来了。”
“利？哪个利？萨姆特（Sumpter）么？”
“不；伦诺克斯·利。”
“是么？他气色如何？”
“好得很。说要逛逛巴黎和巴登。此刻说不定就在这儿。咱们叫侍者拿份 *Fremden-List*（旅客名录）来。”

===Original===
Maida had heard, and with the hearing there had come to her an enveloping dread. She felt that, did she see him, the love which she had tried to banish would return unfettered from its exile. Strength was not yet hers; with time, she knew, she could have sworn it would come; but, for the moment, she was helpless, and into the dread a longing mingled. At once, as though in search of a protection that should guard her against herself, she turned to her husband. To him, the Russians, Brazilians, and other gentry had been part of the landscape. He had little taste for music, and Blydenburg had bored him as that amiable gentleman was accustomed to bore everyone with whom he conversed, yet, nevertheless, through that spirit of paradox which is common to us all, Mr. Incoul liked the man, and for old association’s sake took to the boredom in a kindlier fashion than had it come from a newer and more vivacious acquaintance.
