Blydenburg had been explaining the value of recent excavations in Tirynth, a subject which Mr. Incoul understood better than the informist, but he noticed Maida’s movement and stopped short.
“Come, Milly,” he said to his daughter, “let’s be going.”
Milly had sat by his side the entire evening, in stealthy enjoyment of secular music, performed for the first time in her hearing on the Lord’s day. She was a pale, freckled girl, with hair of the shade of Bavarian beer. She was not beautiful, but then she was good﻿—a sort of angel bound in calf.
When Milly and her father had disappeared, Maida turned to her husband again. “Do you mind leaving Baden?” she asked.
Mr. Incoul eyed her a moment. “Why?” he asked. He had a trick of answering one question with another, yet for the moment she wondered whether he too had heard the conversation behind them, and then comforted by the thought that in any case the name of Lenox Leigh could convey but little to him, she shrugged her shoulders. “Oh, I don’t know,” she said; “I don’t like it; it’s hot and crowded. I think I would like the seashore better.”

===Chinese===
玛伊达听见了；随着这一听，一股包裹全身的恐惧攫住了她。她觉得，自己一旦见他，那被她放逐的爱便会挣脱锁链，从流放地里回来。力量还不是她的；假以时日——她可以起誓——力量会来；可眼下她无依无靠，恐惧里又渗进了渴望。她立刻转向丈夫，仿佛要寻一堵护卫，好提防她自己。在他，俄国人、巴西人及其他诸位不过是风景的一部分。他本不大嗜音乐，布莱登堡也叫他腻烦——那位和蔼的先生同谁交谈就把谁腻烦个够——然而，凭着我们人人共有的那点悖谬劲儿，因库尔先生倒喜欢这个人，念旧交情，这份腻烦也显得可亲，若出自一位更新的、更活泼的相识反倒不如。布莱登堡一直在讲梯林斯（Tirynth）新近发掘的价值，这题目因库尔先生其实比讲述者更懂；可他注意到玛伊达的动静，戛然收住了。
“来，米莉，”他对女儿说，“咱们走吧。”
米莉整晚坐在父亲身边，偷偷享受着世俗音乐——那是她生平头一遭在主日听人演奏。她是个苍白、有雀斑的姑娘，头发的颜色像巴伐利亚啤酒。她不美，可是她善良——一位用小牛皮装订起来的天使。
米莉同父亲走没影了，玛伊达又转向丈夫。“离开巴登，你介意么？”她问。
因库尔先生端详了她片刻。“为什么？”他问。他有个以问作答的脾气；她一时倒疑心他是否也听见了身后那段谈话；随即又安慰自己：无论如何，“伦诺克斯·利”这名字于他传达不了什么；她耸了耸肩。“哦，说不上来，”她说，“我不喜欢这儿；又热又挤。我想还是海边好。”

===Original===
“Very good,” he answered; “whatever you prefer. I will speak to Karl tonight.” (Karl was the courier.) “I don’t suppose,” he added, reflectively, “that you would care for Trouville﻿—I know I should not.”
He had risen, and Maida, who had risen with him, was looking down at the gravel, which she toyed nervously with her foot. The opera that had been given that evening was evidently over. A stream of people were coming from the direction of the theatre, and among them was the Prince. He was chatting with his companions, but his trained eye had marked Mrs. Incoul, and when he reached the place where she stood he stopped again.
“You didn’t go in tonight,” he said, collectively. “It was rather good, too.” And then, without waiting for an answer, he continued: “Won’t you both dine with us tomorrow?”
“Oh, we can’t,” Maida answered. She was tormented with the thought that at any moment Lenox might appear. “We can’t; we are going away.”

===Chinese===
“很好，”他答道，“随你挑。我今晚就同卡尔（Karl）说。”（卡尔便是那位随行侍从。）“我想，”他若有所思地补了一句，“你是不会中意特鲁维尔的——我知道我准不中意。”
他已起身；同他一起起身的玛伊达，正低头看着脚下的沙砾，一只脚紧张地拨弄着。那晚演的歌剧显然散场了。一川人流从剧场方向涌来，亲王也在其中。他正同随行们谈着，但那双训练有素的眼睛早盯住了因库尔太太；走到她立的地方，他又停了下来。
“你们今晚没进去看，”他冲着两位一齐说，“倒真不错呢。”不等回答，他又接下去：“明天两位肯赏光同我们共进晚餐么？”
“哦，不行，”玛伊达答道。她被一个念头煎熬着：伦诺克斯随时可能出现。“不行；我们要走了。”

===Original===
The Prince smiled in his brown beard. Americans were popular with him. He liked their freedom. There was, he knew, barely one woman in Baden, not utterly bedridden, who would have taken his invitation so lightly. “I am sorry,” he said, and he spoke sincerely. Like any other sensible man, he liked beauty and he liked it near him. He knew that Mrs. Incoul had been recently married, and in his own sagacious way, *il posait des jalons*. “You are to be at Ballaster in the autumn, I hear.” Ballaster was a commodious shooting-box in Scotland, the possession of an hospitable peer.
“Yes, I believe we are,” Maida answered.
“I hope to see you there,” and with these historic words, Prince Charming departed.

===Chinese===
亲王在他棕色的胡髭里笑了。美国人很讨他喜欢。他喜欢他们那份随意。他知道，巴登但凡还有一口气的女人，没有一个会把他的邀约等闲视之。“很遗憾，”他说，说得诚恳。同一切有见识的男人一样，他喜欢美，喜欢美在自己身边。他知道因库尔太太新婚不久，便以他那一副精明劲儿，*il posait des jalons*（法语：埋下界石，预作铺垫）。“听说你们秋天要去巴拉斯特（Ballaster）。”巴拉斯特是苏格兰一座宽敞的猎墅，主人是位好客的勋贵。
“是的，我想是要去的。”玛伊达答道。
“希望到时见到你们。”说完这句历史性的话，迷人的亲王殿下便离去了。

## VI Biarritz / 第六章 比亚里茨

===Original===
After a *frühstück* of coffee and honey, to which the innkeeper, out of compliment to the nationality of his guest, had added an ear of green corn﻿—a combination, be it said, that no one but a German could have imagined﻿—Mr. Incoul went in search of his friend.
He had questioned Karl, and the courier had spoken of Ostend with such enthusiasm that his employer suspected him of some personal interest in the place and struck it at once from the list of possible resorts which he had been devising. On the subject of other *bains de mer* the man was less communicative. There was, he said, nothing attractive about Travemunde, except the name; Scheveningen was apt to be chilly; Trouville he rather favored, but to his thinking Ostend was preferable.
When the courier had gone Mr. Incoul ran his eye down a mental map of the coast of France, and just as it reached the Spanish frontier he remembered that someone in his hearing had recently sounded the attractions of Biarritz. On that seaboard he ultimately decided, and it was with the idea that Blydenburg might go further and fare worse that he sought his friend and suggested the advantages of a trip to the Basque country.
Mr. Blydenburg had few objections to make. He had taken very kindly to the consumption of beer, but beer had not agreed with him, and he admitted, did he stay in Baden, that, in spite of the ill effects, he would still be unable to resist the allurements of that insidious beverage. “Act like a man, then,” said Mr. Incoul, encouragingly; “act like a man and flee from it.”
There was no gainsaying the value of this advice, but between its adoption and a journey to Biarritz the margin was wide. “It is true,” he said, reflectively, “I could study the language at the fountainhead.” (Mr. Blydenburg, it may be explained, was a gentleman who plumed himself on his familiarity with recondite tongues, but one whose knowledge of the languages that are current in polite society was such as is gleaned from the appendices of guidebooks.)

===Chinese===
用过一顿咖啡与蜂蜜的 *frühstück*（德式早餐）——店主为敬重客人的国籍，又添了一穗青玉米；这一搭配，说句公道话，除了德国人谁也想不出来——因库尔先生便去寻他的朋友。
他盘问过卡尔；那随行侍从一说起奥斯坦德（Ostend）便那样起劲，雇主疑心他在那地方有什么私人的干系，便把它立刻从自己拟着的避暑地名单上勾掉了。谈到别处 *bains de mer*（法语：海水浴场），那人便不那么健谈。特拉沃明德，他说，除了名字之外毫无动人之处；斯海弗宁恩多半嫌冷；特鲁维尔他颇有好感，但依他之见，还是奥斯坦德更胜一筹。
侍从走后，因库尔先生在心里把法国海岸的地图顺了一遍；目光刚到西班牙边境，便想起近来曾听人夸过比亚里茨的好处。他终于选定了那片海岸；他料想布莱登堡再走下去无非越走越糟，便去寻他的朋友，陈说去巴斯克地区（the Basque country）一游的种种好处。
布莱登堡先生没什么可反对的。他对喝啤酒早已十分投缘，可啤酒同他却不大相合；他承认，若留在巴登，明知恶果当前，他仍旧抵御不了那阴险饮料的诱惑。“那就拿出男子汉的样子来，”因库尔先生鼓劲道，“拿出男子汉的样子，逃了它。”
这忠告的价值无可否认；只是采纳它同去比亚里茨之间，距离还宽得很。“倒也是，”他若有所思地说，“我可以在源头上学那语言。”（不妨说明，布莱登堡先生是位以通晓冷僻语言自矜的绅士；至于通行于上流社会的几种语言，他的知识也就止于旅行指南附录里拾得的那一点。）

===Original===
Mr. Incoul nodded approvingly, “Certainly there would be no difficulty about that.”
Blydenburg looked at him musingly for a moment and nodded, too. “The name Biarritz,” he said, “comes, I am inclined to believe, from *bi haritz*﻿—two oaks. Minucius thinks that it comes from *bi harri*﻿—two rocks; but I have detected Minucius in certain errors which has made me wary of accepting his opinion. For instance, he claims that the Basques are descendants of the Phoenicians. Nothing could be more preposterous. They are purely Iberian, and probably the most ancient race in Europe. Why, you would be surprised﻿—”
Mr. Incoul interrupted him cruelly﻿—“I often am,” he said; “now tell me, will you be ready this afternoon?”
“The laundress has just taken my things.”
“Send after them, then. I make no doubt that there you can find another on the Bay of Biscay.”

===Chinese===
因库尔先生赞许地点了点头。“那自然毫无困难。”
布莱登堡出神地望了他片刻，也点了点头。“比亚里茨这名字，”他说，“我倾向于认为出自 *bi haritz*——两棵橡树。米努奇乌斯（Minucius）认为它出自 *bi harri*——两块石头；可我在米努奇乌斯那里查出过几处错，这叫我对他不敢轻信。比方说，他声称巴斯克人是腓尼基人的后裔。再荒唐不过了。他们是纯粹的伊比利亚人（Iberian），多半还是欧洲最古老的种族。嗐，说出来你要吃惊——”
因库尔先生狠心打断了他——“我常吃惊，”他说，“现在告诉我，今天下午你能动身么？”
“洗衣妇刚把我的衣物取走了。”
“那就打发人去追。我毫不怀疑，在比斯开湾（the Bay of Biscay）那边你还能再找一位。”

===Original===
“I wonder what Biscay comes from? *bi scai*, two currents, perhaps. Yes, of course, I will be ready.” And as his friend moved away, he pursed his lips abstractedly and made a note of the derivation.
A courier aiding, the journey from Baden to Biarritz can be accomplished without loss of life or reason. It partakes something of the character of a zigzag, the connections are seldom convenient, the wayside inns are not of the best, but if people go abroad to be uncomfortable, what more can the heart desire? The Incoul-Blydenburg party, impeded by Karl, a body-servant, and two maids, received their allotted share of discomfort with the very best grace in the world. They reached Bayonne after five days, not, it is true, of consecutive motion, but of such consecutive heat that they were glad to descend at the station of that excitable little city and in the fresh night air drive in open carriages over the few kilometres that remained to be traversed.
It was many hours before the journey was sufficiently a part of the past to enable the travelers to look about them, but on the evening succeeding their arrival, after a dinner on the verandah of the Continental, they sat with much contentment of spirit enjoying the intermittent showers of summer stars and the boom and rustle of the waves. Baden was unregretted. To the left, high above, on the summit of a projecting eminence, the white and illuminated Casino glittered like an aerian palace. To the right was the gardened quadrangle of the former Empress of the French, in the air was the scent of seaweed and before them the Infinite.
“It’s quite good enough for me,” Blydenburg confided to his companions, and the confidence in its inelegant terseness conveyed the sentiments of them all.
A week passed without bringing with it any incident worthy of record. In the mornings they met at the Moorish Pavilion which stands on the shore and there lounged or bathed. Maida’s beauty necessarily attracted much attention, and when she issued in a floating wrapper from the sedan-chair in which she allowed herself to be carried from the Pavilion to the sea, a number of amateurs who stood each day just out of reach of the waves, expressed their admiration in winning gutturals.

===Chinese===
“不知道‘比斯开’又是从哪儿来的？*bi scai*，两股潮流，也许。好，当然，我到时齐备。”朋友走开后，他心不在焉地咂咂嘴，把这条词源记了下来。
有随行侍从帮衬，从巴登到比亚里茨的旅程可以不丢性命、不失神智地完成。它带点之字形的性质，换车难得方便，路旁客栈也说不上最好；可人们出国若原为受苦，心里还能更求什么呢？因库尔-布莱登堡一行，拖着卡尔、一名贴身男仆和两个女仆，以世界上最好的风度领受了分派给他们的那份不适。五天之后他们到了巴约讷（Bayonne）——说真的，倒不是连续赶路，而是连续受热——于是乐得在那座容易激动的小城的车站下车，在清新的夜风里，乘敞篷马车驶完剩下的那几公里。
直到过了许多钟头，旅程才充分归入过去，旅人们方能四下打量；到后的那个晚上，在大陆旅馆（the Continental）的露台上用过晚餐，他们心满意足地坐着，享受夏日星辰间歇的阵雨，和海浪的轰隆与窸窣。巴登没有可留恋的。左边高处，一座凸出的山冈顶上，灯火通明的白色赌场（Casino）闪烁如一座凌空的宫殿。右边是法兰西前皇后那带花园的四合院落，空气里是海藻的气息，他们面前是无垠。
“我这就够受用了。”布莱登堡向同伴们交了心；这句不雅致的简短心声，道出了全体的感想。
一周过去，没有带来一件值得记载的事。早晨他们在岸边那座摩尔式凉亭（Moorish Pavilion）会齐，或闲卧，或入浴。玛伊达的美貌自然招来许多注目；当她披着一袭飘拂的罩衫，从那顶她任人抬着从凉亭到海边的轿椅里出来时，一群每日站在浪头刚好够不着的地方的看客们，便用悦耳的喉音表示倾倒。

===Original===
She was, assuredly, very beautiful, particularly so in comparison with the powdered sallowness of the ladies from Spain, and when, with a breezy gesture of her own, she tossed her wrap to the bather and with sandaled feet and a white and clinging costume of serge she stepped to the water there was one onlooker who bethought him of a nymph of the Aegean Sea. She was a good swimmer, as the American girl often is, and she breasted and dived through the wonderful waves with an intrepidity such as the accompanying *baigneur* had been rarely called upon to restrain.
From the shade of beach chairs, large and covered like wicker tents, her husband and the Blydenburgs would watch her prowess, and when, after a final ride on the crest of some great billow, she would be tossed breathless and deliciously disheveled into the steadying arms of the bather, the amateurs were almost tempted to applaud.
In the afternoons there were drives and excursions. One day to Bayonne along the white, hard road that skirts the Chambre d’Amour, through the peace and quiet of Anglet and on through kilometres of pines to the Adour, a river so beautiful in itself that all the ingenuity of man has been unable to make it wholly hideous, and thence by its banks to the outlying gardens of the city.
On other days they would loiter on the cliffs that overhang the Côte des Basques, or push on to Bidart, a chromatic village where the inhabitants are so silent that one might fancy them enchanted by the mellow marvels of their afternoons.
But of all other places Maida preferred Saint-Jean-de-Luz. It lies near the frontier on a bay of the tenderest blue, and for background it has the hazy amethyst of the neighborly Pyrenees. The houses are rainbows of blended colors; from the open doorways the passer, now and then, catches a whiff of rancid oil, the smell of victuals cooked in fat, from a mouldering square a cathedral casts an unexpected chill, but otherwise the town is charming, warm and very bright. On the shore stands an inn and next to it a toy casino.

===Chinese===
她确实非常美，同西班牙来的太太们扑了粉的菜色一比尤其如此；当她以她那爽利的姿势把罩衫抛给浴场侍者，趿着凉鞋、穿着白色紧身的哔叽浴衣踏进水里时，有一位看客想起爱琴海的仙娥。她泳得好，美国姑娘多半如此；她在奇妙的浪里破浪、潜泳，那份无畏，连随侍的 *baigneur*（法语：浴场侍者）也难得需要加以约束。
她丈夫和布莱登堡父女坐在海滩椅的荫下——那椅子又大又罩着篷，像柳条帐篷——看她逞技；待她最后骑上某个大浪的浪脊，气喘吁吁、鬓发散乱地被抛进浴场侍者扶稳的臂弯里，那些看客简直要鼓起掌来。
午后则有驱车与郊游。有一天去巴约讷，沿着绕爱情小屋（Chambre d'Amour）那条白硬的路，穿过昂格莱（Anglet）的安宁静谧，再穿过几公里的松林到阿杜尔河（the Adour）——一条本身那样美的河，人类百般巧思也无法把它弄得全然丑陋——再顺河岸到城郊的园圃。
别的日子，他们或在俯临巴斯克海岸（Côte des Basques）的崖头流连，或径直去比达尔（Bidart）——一个色彩斑斓的村子，村民安静得叫人疑心他们被自家午后的温醇奇景迷住了。
可在所有别的地方里，玛伊达最偏爱圣让-德吕兹（Saint-Jean-de-Luz）。它卧在边境附近一片至柔的蓝色海湾上，背景是近邻的比利牛斯山（the Pyrenees）朦胧的紫晶。房屋是诸色交融的彩虹；行人不时从敞开的门口吸进一股哈喇的油气，那是肥油炸出来的饭菜味；一座倾颓的广场后，大教堂投下一阵出其不意的寒意；除此之外，这镇子是迷人的、温暖的，而且非常明亮。岸上有一家客栈，旁边是一座玩具似的赌场。

===Original===
To this exotic resort the little party drove one afternoon. It had been originally arranged to pass the day there, but on the day for which the excursion was planned, a *Course Landaise* was announced at Biarritz, and it was then decided that they should first view the *course* and dine afterwards at Saint-Jean. At first both Maida and Miss Blydenburg refused to attend the performance and it was not until they were assured that it was a bullfight for ladies in which there was no shedding of blood that they consented to be present. The spectacle which they then witnessed was voted most agreeable. The bulls, which turned out to be heifers, very lithe and excitable, were housed in boxed stalls, which bore their respective names: Isabel, Rosa, Paquita, Adelaide, Carlota and Sofia. The ring itself was an improvised arrangement constructed in a great racquet court. The spectators, according to their means, found seats on either side, the poorer in the sun and the more wealthy in the shaded Tribune d’Honneur. After a premonitory blare from municipal brass the quadrille entered the arena. They were a good-looking set of men, more plainly dressed than their bloodier brothers of Spain, and very agile. Two of them carrying long poles stationed themselves at the sides, one, armed with a barb laid himself down a few feet from Isabel’s door, and a fourth threw his soft hat in the middle of the ring, put his feet in it and stood expectant. In a moment a latch was drawn, Isabel leaped from her stall, bounded over the prostrate form that pricked her on her way and made a straight rush for the motionless figure in the centre of the ring. When she reached him he was in the air and over her with his feet still in the hat. Isabel was bewildered, instead of goring a man she had run her horns into empty space and in her annoyance she turned viciously at one of the pole-bearing gentlemen who vaulted over her as easily as were he crossing a gutter, but in vaulting the pole slipped from him, and amid the applause of the audience Isabel chased him across the ring to a high fence opposite, and to which he rose like a bird with Isabel’s horns on his heels. There was more of this amusement, and then Isabel, a trifle tired, was lured back to her box; Rosa was loosed and the performance repeated.
The escapes seemed so hairbreadth that Mr. Blydenburg announced his intention of witnessing a genuine bullfight, and on the way to Saint-Jean urged his companions to accompany him over the border and view the real article. “There is one announced for next Sunday,” he said, “at San Sebastián, a stone’s throw from here.” The appetite of all had been whetted, and during the rest of the drive, Mr. Blydenburg discoursed on the subject with such learning and enthusiasm that even his daughter consented to forget her Sabbath principles and make one of the projected party.
When the meal was done, they went into the toy Casino. There was a band playing at one end of the hall, the which was so narrow that the director had been obliged to select thin musicians, and beyond was a paperless reading-room, a vague café, a dwarf theatre, and a *salle-de-jeu* in white and gamboge. In the latter division, where the high life of Saint-Jean had assembled, stood a table that resembled a roulette. In its centre were miniature revolving bulls, which immediately attracted Mr. Blydenburg’s attention, and on the green baize were painted the names of cities.
“*Banderilla! Ruego! Sevilla!*” the croupier called, as the party entered. In one hand he held a rake, with which he possessed himself of the stakes of those who had lost, and with the other hand he tossed out coin to those who had won. The machinery was again set in motion, and when the impulse had ceased to act he called out anew, “*Espada! Nero! Madrid!*”
Mr. Blydenburg was thoroughly interested. In the residue of twenty-five French lessons, which he had learned in his boyhood from a German, he made bold to demand information.

===Chinese===
这个异域的胜地，那伙人是某天下午驱车去的。原本安排在那里消磨一日，可预定出游的那天，比亚里茨贴出了一场兰德斯斗牛（Course Landaise）的告示，于是大家议定先看斗牛，再回圣让晚餐。起初玛伊达和布莱登堡小姐都拒绝去看；直到得了保证，说这是一场专为女士们设的斗牛，不见血，她们才答应到场。随后她们看到的场面，众人公认为再惬意不过。那几头“公牛”原来是些小母牛，极灵巧，也极易激动，分装在板条牛栏里，栏上各标其名：伊莎贝尔、罗莎、帕基塔、阿德莱德、卡洛塔、索菲亚。斗牛场本身是临时搭在一座大网球场里的装置。看客按财力分坐两侧，穷些的坐太阳地里，阔些的坐荫凉的荣誉看台（Tribune d'Honneur）。市政铜号预鸣过一阵，四对舞班进了场。是一班漂亮的汉子，衣裳比他们西班牙那些见血的同行朴素，身手又极矫捷。两个持长竿的分站两侧；一个持倒钩的，在伊莎贝尔的栏门前几步伏下；第四个把软帽掷到场心，双脚踏进帽子里，屹立以待。顷刻间门闩一抽，伊莎贝尔从栏里跃出，跃过那个横伏在地、顺路扎了她一下的身子，直冲场心那尊纹丝不动的身影。冲到他跟前时，他已腾身而起，双脚踏着帽子从她头上翻了过去。伊莎贝尔懵了：非但没顶到人，双角扎了个空；她一恼，狠狠转身撞向一位持竿的先生；那人从她身上一跃而过，轻易得像跨一条阳沟，可跃时竿子脱了手，在满场掌声里，伊莎贝尔把他追过全场，逼到对面一道高栅前，他鸟一般腾上栅顶，伊莎贝尔的角紧跟着他的脚跟。这一类把戏还有许多；随后伊莎贝尔略觉乏了，被诱回栏中；罗莎放了出来，戏码从头再演。
那些脱险瞧着都悬于一发，布莱登堡先生便宣布有意去看一场真正的斗牛；去圣让的路上，他撺掇同伴们随他越过边境，见识见识真货。“下个礼拜天就有一场，”他说，“在圣塞巴斯蒂安（San Sebastián），离这儿一箭之地。”众人的胃口都被吊起来了；剩下的一路上，布莱登堡先生就这题目讲得那样博学而热烈，连他的女儿也答应忘掉安息日的信条，加入这筹划中的一伙。
饭罢，他们进了那座玩具赌场。大厅一端有个乐队在奏乐；厅那样窄，乐队长不得不专挑瘦乐师。再往里是一间没有报纸的阅览室、一家影影绰绰的咖啡馆、一座侏儒似的戏院，和一间白与藤黄配色的 *salle-de-jeu*（法语：赌戏厅）。后一处聚集着圣让的上流生活，立着一张貌似轮盘的赌桌。桌心是几头微型旋转的牛，立时引住了布莱登堡先生的目光；绿台呢上画着一个个城市的名字。
“*Banderilla*！*Ruego*！*Sevilla*！（花镖！雷戈！塞维利亚！）”他们进屋时，荷官正报着名。他一手执一柄耙子，把输家的注一一搂进自己手里，另一手把硬币抛给赢家。机括再度转起；等那股冲力停了，他又报：“*Espada*！*Nero*！*Madrid*！（剑！内罗！马德里！）”
布莱登堡先生大感兴趣。凭着童年从一个德国人那里学来的二十五课法文的残余，他斗胆讨教起来。

===Original===
“It’s the neatest game in the world,” the croupier replied; “six for one on the cities, even on the colors, even on banderilla or *espada*, and twenty for one on Frascuelo.” And, as he gave the latter information, he pointed to a little figure armed with a sword, which was supposed to represent that famous matador. “The minimum,” he added, obligingly, “is fifty centimes; the maximum, forty sous.”
“I’ll go Frascuelo,” said Blydenburg, and suiting the action to the word, he placed a coin on the table. Maida, meanwhile, had put money on everything﻿—cities, colors, banderilla, espada, and Frascuelo as well. To the surprise of everyone, but most to that of the croupier, Frascuelo won. Maida saw twenty francs swept from her and forty returned. Blydenburg, who had played a closer game, received forty also, but he lost nothing, and he beamed as joyously as had the University of Copenhagen crowned an essay of his own manufacture.
It was by means of these mild amusements that the first week of their sojourn was helped away. Through the kindness of an international acquaintance, Mr. Incoul had been made welcome at the Cercle de Biarritz, and in that charming summer club, where there is much high play and perfect informality, he had become acquainted with a Spaniard, the Marquis of Zunzarraga.
One day when the latter gentleman had wearied of the columns of the *Epoca*, and Mr. Incoul sought in vain for some refreshment from *Galignani*, they drew their chairs together and exchanged cigarettes.
In answer to the question which is addressed to every newcomer, Mr. Incoul expressed himself pleased with the country, adding that were not hotel life always distasteful he would be glad to remain on indefinitely.

===Chinese===
“这是世上最精巧的赌戏，”荷官答道；“押城市一赔六，押颜色对赔，押花镖或 *espada*（剑）对赔，押弗拉斯库埃洛（Frascuelo）一赔二十。”说到末一项，他指了指一个持剑的小人像，据说代表的就是那位名噪一时的斗牛士。“下限，”他殷勤地补充，“五十生丁；上限，四十苏。”
“我押弗拉斯库埃洛。”布莱登堡说着，便言出行随，把一枚硬币放上台面。玛伊达却什么都押了——城市、颜色、花镖、剑，还有弗拉斯库埃洛。出人意料、最出于荷官意料的，偏偏是弗拉斯库埃洛赢了。玛伊达眼看着二十法郎从自己面前被搂走，四十法郎推了回来。布莱登堡打得谨慎，也得了四十，分文未失，喜得满面生光，仿佛哥本哈根大学给他自撰的一篇论文戴上了桂冠。
靠了这些温和的消遣，旅居的头一个星期给打发过去了。承一位国际相识的情，因库尔先生在比亚里茨俱乐部（Cercle de Biarritz）受了欢迎；在那座可爱的夏季俱乐部里——那里豪赌成风，全无拘束——他结识了一个西班牙人，松萨拉加侯爵（the Marquis of Zunzarraga）。
一日，那位先生看腻了《时代报》（*Epoca*）的栏栏行行，因库尔先生也想从《加利尼亚尼报》（*Galignani*）里讨些新鲜而不可得，两人便把椅子挪到一处，交换了香烟。
回答了那句对每个新来者都要问的话之后，因库尔先生表示他喜欢这地方，又说，若不是旅馆生活总归碍口，他乐意无限期住下去。

===Original===
“You might take a villa,” the marquis suggested. To this Mr. Incoul made no reply. The nobleman fluttered his fingers a moment and then said, “take mine, you can have it, servants and all.”
The Villa Zunzarraga was near the hotel and its airy architecture had already attracted Mr. Incoul’s eye. It was a modern improvement on a feudal château, there were turreted wings in which the *machicoulis* were replaced by astragals and a broad and double stairway of marble led up to the main entrance.
“If you have nothing better to do today,” the marquis continued, “go in and take a look at it. I have never rented it before, but this summer the marquesa is with the queen, my mistress, and I would be glad to have it off my hands.”
After consulting Maida in regard to her wishes, Mr. Incoul determined to act on the suggestion, and that afternoon they went together to view the villa. In its appointments there was little fault to be found. There was no vestibule, unless, indeed, the entrance hall, which was large enough to accommodate a small cotillon, could be so considered; on the right were reception-rooms, to the left a dining-room, all facing the sea, while at the rear, overlooking a quiet garden that seemed to extend indefinitely and lose itself in the lilac fringes of the tamaris, was a library. On the floor above were bed and sitting rooms. In one wing were the offices, kitchen and servants’ quarters, in another was the coach-house and stables.
Under the guidance of the host, Mr. Incoul went to explore the place, while Maida remained in the library. It was a satisfactory room, lined on three sides with low, well-filled bookcases, the windows were doors and extended nearly to the ceiling, but the light fell through pink awnings under which was a verandah, with steps that led to the garden below. From the walls hung selections of Goya’s “Proverbios” and “Tauromaquia,” a series of nightmares in black and white. Among them was a picture of a lake of blood haunted by evil spirits; a vertiginous flight of phantoms more horrible than any Doré ever saw; a reunion of sorcerers with cats for steeds; women tearing teeth from the mouths of the gibbeted; a confusion of demons and incubes; a disordered dance of delirious *manolas*; caricatures that held the soul of Hoffmann; the disembowelment of fantastic *chulos*; horses tossed by bulls with chimerical horns; but best of all, a skeleton leaning with a leer from the tomb and scrawling on it the significant legend, *Nada*, nothing.

===Chinese===
“您可以租一座别墅。”侯爵提议。这话因库尔先生没有接。贵人手指颤了一阵，随后说：“住我那座吧，您尽可用去，仆人一并奉送。”
松萨拉加别墅（Villa Zunzarraga）就在旅馆近旁，那轻盈的建筑早已入了因库尔先生的眼。它是封建城堡的一种近代改良：两翼塔楼高耸，*machicoulis*（法语：堞口）换成了玻璃窗棂，一道宽阔的双跑大理石阶通向正门。
“您今天若没有更要紧的事，”侯爵接着说，“就进去看一看。我从不曾出租过它；只是今年夏天侯爵夫人随王后——我的女主子——去了，我巴不得把它脱手。”
因库尔先生征询过玛伊达的意愿，决计照这建议办；当天下午他们便一同去看别墅。屋里陈设几乎无可挑剔。没有门厅——除非那间敞得下一小场科蒂永舞的进门大厅可以算数；右边是几间客厅，左边是饭厅，一律面海；后部是一间书房，俯临一座幽静的花园，园子仿佛无休止地延伸下去，隐没在柽柳的丁香色边缘里。楼上是卧室与起居间。一翼是厨房、杂房与仆役下处，另一翼是车房与马厩。
由主人引着，因库尔先生去查看各处，玛伊达留在书房里。这是间令人满意的屋子，三面排着低矮而充盈的书橱；窗即是门，直抵天花板；光线透过粉色遮篷筛下，篷下是一道游廊，廊前的台阶通到下面的花园。墙上挂着戈雅（Goya）《谚语》（“Proverbios”）与《斗牛术》（“Tauromaquia”）的选页，一组黑白的梦魇。其中有血湖一帧，恶灵出没其间；有群鬼凌空的一阵天旋地转，比多雷（Doré）见过的任何东西都可怖；有术士聚会，坐骑皆是猫；有妇人从绞刑犯口里拔牙；有群魔与淫魈的混沌；有狂乱的马诺拉（*manolas*）们一场错乱的舞蹈；有攥着霍夫曼魂灵的漫画；有奇幻丘洛（*chulos*）们的开膛破肚；有被生着奇形犄角的公牛挑上天的马；而最好的一幅，是一具骷髅从坟墓里斜身狞笑，在墓石上涂下那个意味深长的题记：*Nada*——虚空。

===Original===
In one corner, on a pedestal, there glittered a Buddha, the legs crossed and a smile of indolent apathy on its imbecile features. Behind it was a giant crucifix with arms outstretched like the wings of woe.
Maida wandered from bookcase to bookcase, examining the contents with incurious eye. The titles were strange to her and new. In one division were the works of Archilaus, Albert le Grand, Raymond Lulle, Armand de Villenova, Nostradamus, and Paracelsus, the masters of occult science. Another was given up to Spanish literature. There were the poems of Berceo, the *romancero* of the church; the codex of Alphonso X, the Justinian of medieval Spain; *El Tesoro*, a work on alchemy by the same royal hand and the *Conquista d’ultramar*. There was the *Libro de consejos*, by Sanchez IV; and *Beccerro*, the armorial of the nobility, by his son, Alphonso XI. Therewith was a collection of verse of the troubadours, the songs of Aimeric de Bellinsi, Foulque de Lunel, Carbonel, Nat de Tours, and Riquier, the last of the knight-errants. Then came the poems of Juan de Mena, the Dante of Castille; the Rabelaisian relaxations of the Archbishop of Hita; the *cancionero* of Ausias March, that of Baena, of Stuñiga, and that of Ixar.
Another bookcase was filled with the French poets, from Villon to Soulary. The editions were delicious, a pleasure to hold, and many of them bore the imprint of Lemerre. Among them was the *Fleurs du Mal*, an unexpurgated copy, and by it were the poems of Baudelaire’s decadent descendants, Paul Verlaine and Mallarmé.
There were other bookcases, and of these there was one of which the door was locked. In it were *Justine* and *Juliette*, by the Marquis de Sade; the works of Piron; the works of Beroalde de Verville; a copy of *Mercius*; a copy of *Thérèse Philosophe*; the *De Arcanis Amoris*; Mirabeau’s *Rideau levé*; *Gamaini*, by Alfred de Musset and George Sand; Boccaccio; the *Heptameron*; *Paphian Days*; Crébillon’s *Sopha*; the *Erotika Biblion*; the *Satyricon* of Petronius; an illustrated catalogue of the Naples Museum; Voltaire’s *Pucelle*; a work or two of Diderot’s; Maizeroy’s *Deux Amies*; the *Clouds*; the *Curée*; everything, in fact, from Aristophanes to Zola.
The collection was meaningless to Maida, and she turned aside and went out on the verandah. Below, on the gravel walk, was a cat with a tail like a banner, and a neck furred like a ruff. Maida crumpled a bit of paper and threw it down. The cat jumped at it at once, toyed with it for a moment, and then, sliding backwards with a crab-like movement, its back arched, and its ears drawn down, it caught a glimpse of Maida’s unfamiliar figure, and fled to the bushes with a shriek of feigned terror. A servant passed, and ignorant of Maida’s presence, apostrophized the retreating feline as a loafer and a liar.

===Chinese===
一角的小座上，闪着一尊佛像，盘着腿，那痴憨的面容上挂一缕慵懒冷漠的笑。它背后是一座巨大的十字架，双臂平张，像忧患的两翼。
玛伊达从一橱走到一橱，用淡漠的眼光察看内容。那些书名于她是生疏而新鲜的。一格是阿尔基劳斯（Archilaus）、大阿尔贝（Albert le Grand）、雷蒙·吕尔（Raymond Lulle）、阿尔芒·德·维勒诺瓦（Armand de Villenova）、诺斯特拉达穆斯（Nostradamus）与帕拉塞尔苏斯（Paracelsus）——神秘学诸大师——的著作。另一格尽属西班牙文学：有贝尔塞奥（Berceo）的诗，教会的罗曼采罗；有阿方索十世（Alphonso X）——中世纪西班牙的查士丁尼——的法典；有同一只御笔的炼金术著作《宝鉴》（*El Tesoro*），还有《海外征服记》（*Conquista d'ultramar*）；有桑切斯四世（Sanchez IV）的《谏言书》（*Libro de consejos*）；有其子阿方索十一世（Alphonso XI）所编贵族纹章录《贝塞罗》（*Beccerro*）。此外是一卷吟游诗人的歌集：贝兰西的艾梅里克（Aimeric de Bellinsi）、吕内尔的富尔克（Foulque de Lunel）、卡尔博内尔（Carbonel）、图尔的纳（Nat de Tours），还有里基耶（Riquier）——末一位游侠骑士——的歌。再往后是卡斯蒂利亚的但丁胡安·德·梅纳（Juan de Mena）的诗；伊塔大主教那拉伯雷式的消遣；奥西亚斯·马尔赫（Ausias March）、巴埃纳（Baena）、斯图尼加（Stuñiga）与伊萨尔（Ixar）诸家的歌谣集。
又一橱是法国诗人，从维庸（Villon）到苏拉里（Soulary）。版本都可人，拿在手里便是享受，许多还钤着勒梅尔（Lemerre）的书坊印记。其中有一部《恶之花》（*Fleurs du Mal*），未删节的版本；旁边便是波德莱尔那班颓废后裔——保罗·魏尔伦（Paul Verlaine）与马拉美（Mallarmé）——的诗。
还有别的书橱，其中一橱门上上了锁。橱里是萨德侯爵（Marquis de Sade）的《朱斯蒂娜》（*Justine*）与《朱丽叶特》（*Juliette*）；皮龙（Piron）全集；贝罗阿尔德·德·韦维尔（Beroalde de Verville）全集；一部《梅尔丘斯》（*Mercius*）；一部《哲人泰蕾兹》（*Thérèse Philosophe*）；《爱之秘辛》（*De Arcanis Amoris*）；米拉波（Mirabeau）的《幕启》（*Rideau levé*）；阿尔弗雷·德·缪塞（Alfred de Musset）与乔治·桑（George Sand）合撰的《加玛妮尼》（*Gamaini*）；薄伽丘（Boccaccio）；《七日谈》（*Heptameron*）；《帕福斯的日子》（*Paphian Days*）；克雷比永（Crébillon）的《沙发》（*Sopha*）；《爱经文库》（*Erotika Biblion*）；佩特罗尼乌斯（Petronius）的《萨蒂利孔》（*Satyricon*）；一册那不勒斯博物馆的图册；伏尔泰（Voltaire）的《圣女》（*Pucelle*）；狄德罗（Diderot）的一两部作品；梅泽罗瓦（Maizeroy）的《两女友》（*Deux Amies*）；《云》（*the Clouds*）；《掠夺》（*la Curée*）——总之，从阿里斯托芬到左拉，应有尽有。
这一橱藏书于玛伊达毫无意义；她转过身，出到游廊上。下面的碎石径上有一只猫，尾巴像一面旌旗，颈毛蓬起像一副拉夫领。玛伊达把一张纸揉皱，丢了下去。猫立刻扑上，玩弄片刻，随后横着倒退几步，背弓起来，耳朵抿下去；它瞥见玛伊达这陌生的身影，装出一声骇叫，逃进树丛里去了。一个仆人走过，不知玛伊达在场，冲着那退去的猫喊，骂它是懒汉，是骗子。

===Original===
A moment later Mr. Incoul and the marquis reappeared.
“I have been admiring your Angora,” Maida said, “but I fear I startled it.”
The marquis rubbed his hands together thoughtfully. “It is a wonderful animal,” he answered, “but it is not an Angora, it is a Tibetian cat, and though it does not talk, at least it converses. It is so odd in its ways that I called it Mistigris, as one might a familiar spirit, but my children prefer Ti-Mi; they think it more Tibetian, I fancy.” He coughed slightly and looking at the points of his fingers, he added, “I will leave it with you of course.”
And then Maida understood that the matter was settled and that the house was hers.

===Chinese===
片刻之后，因库尔先生同侯爵又出现了。
“我方才正赏您那只安哥拉猫，”玛伊达说，“只怕我惊了它。”
侯爵若有所思地搓着双手。“它是只奇兽，”他答道，“可它不是安哥拉猫，是只西藏猫；虽不会说话，至少会谈心。它行事太古怪，我便叫它米斯蒂格里（Mistigris），像人唤一个家宅精灵那样；可孩子们偏爱叫它蒂-米（Ti-Mi）；我想是觉得这样更西藏些。”他轻轻咳了一声，眼望着指尖，又加一句：“自然，我把它留给您。”
于是玛伊达明白了：事情已定，这宅子是她的了。

## VII What May Be Seen from a *Palco* / 第七章 包厢里看得见的

===Original===
The installation was accomplished without difficulty. The marquis migrated to other shores and it took Maida but a short time to discover the pleasures of being luxuriously housed. The apartment which she selected for herself was composed of four rooms; there was a sitting-room in an angle with windows overlooking the sea and others that gave on a quiet street which skirted one wing of the villa. Next to it was a bedroom also overlooking the street, while back of that, on the garden side, was a bath and a dressing-room. A wide hall that was like a haunt of echoes separated these rooms from those of her husband.
Through the street, which was too steep to be much of a thoroughfare, there came each morning the clinging strain of a pastoral melody, and a pipe-playing goatherd would pass leading his black, long-haired flock to the doors of those who bought the milk. When he had gone the silence was stirred by another sound, a call that rose and fell with exquisite sweetness and died away in infinite vibrations: it came from a little old woman, toothless and bent, who, with summer in her voice, hawked crisp gold bread of crescent shape, vaunting its delicacy in birdlike trills. There were other venders who announced their wares in similar ways, and one, a fisher, chaunted a low and mournful measure which he must have caught from the sea.
It was pleasant to be waked in this wise, Maida thought, and as she lay in her great bed of odorous wood, she listened to the calls, and when they had passed, the boom and retreating rustle of the waves occupied and lulled her. In such moments the thoughts that visited her were impermanent and fleeting. She made no effort to stay them, preferring the vague to the outlined, watching the changes and transformations of fancy as though her soul and she were separate, as were her mind a landscape, some

> Paysage choisi,
 

 Que vont charmant masques et bergamasques
 

 Jouant du luth et dansant et quasi
 

 Tristes sous leurs déguisements fantasques.

The room was an accomplice of her languor. The windows were curtained with filmy yellow. Before them were miniature balconies filled with flowers, and as the sun rose the light filtered through flesh-colored awnings striated with ochre. The floor was a mosaic of variegated and lacquered wood. On either side of the bed were silk rugs, sea green and pink, seductive to the foot; the ceiling was a summer sky at dawn, a fresco in cinnabar and smalt.

===Chinese===
安顿一事毫无周折地办妥了。那位侯爵迁往别处海岸去了，玛伊达没用多久便发现了豪华居所里的种种乐处。她给自己挑定的那套房间共有四间：客厅踞在楼角，几扇窗子望得见海，另几扇开向一条傍着别墅一侧翼的僻静小街；紧挨着客厅是一间同样临街的卧室，再往里去，靠花园的一侧，是一间浴室和一间梳妆室。一条宽阔的走廊——恍如回声出没的巢穴——把这些房间与她丈夫的那几间隔开。
每天早晨，那条街上都会飘来一支牧歌的余韵，缠绵不散——那街太陡，算不上什么要道。一个吹着短笛的牧羊人赶着他黑色长毛的羊群走过，把羊赶到买奶人家的门前。他去后，寂静又被另一种声音搅动：一声叫卖，抑扬之间甜美已极，在无穷的颤音里消散。那声音来自一个牙齿掉光、腰背佝偻的小老太婆，她嗓音里带着夏天，叫卖新月形的金黄脆皮面包，用鸟儿一般的啭鸣夸耀它的细美。还有别的货贩用类似的法子唱着各自的货色，其中一个渔贩哼着一支低回哀伤的调子，那调子想必是从海上捕来的。
这样被唤醒是很惬意的，玛伊达想。她躺在那张香木大床上听那些叫卖声；叫卖声过去之后，浪涛的轰鸣与退潮般的沙沙声便占住了她，把她哄入梦乡。在这样的时刻，来访她的念头都无常而倏忽。她也不去挽留，宁取朦胧，不取分明，看着幻想的变幻迁流，仿佛她的灵魂与她本人是两相分离的，仿佛她的心神是一片风景，恰似——

> Paysage choisi,
 

 Que vont charmant masques et bergamasques
 

 Jouant du luth et dansant et quasi
 

 Tristes sous leurs déguisements fantasques. ——（一片选中的风景，／假面与滑稽戏的角儿们正为它添着媚态，／他们弹起琉特琴，翩翩起舞，却几乎／在奇幻的化装之下各怀幽怨。）

这房间是她慵懒的同谋。窗上挂着轻纱般的黄帘。窗前是几座堆满花草的小阳台，日头升起时，光线便透过绘着赭黄条纹的肉色凉篷滤洒下来。地板是一幅杂色上漆木料拼成的镶嵌画。床两侧各铺一块丝毯，一海绿，一粉红，诱着人的脚；天花板是拂晓时分的夏日晴空，一幅朱砂与钴蓝的湿壁画。


===Original===
The Blydenburgs, less luxuriously inclined, remained at the hotel. Mr. Blydenburg had not as yet enjoyed an opportunity of conversing in Basque; he had indeed attempted to address a mildewed little girl whom he encountered one day when loitering on the cliffs, but the child had taken flight, and a mule that was pasturing on a bramble, threw back its ears, elongated its tail and curving its lips, brayed with such anguish that Mr. Blydenburg was fain to delay his studies until fortune offered a more favorable opportunity.
It was at San Sebastián, he thought, that such an opportunity would be found ready made, and on the morning of the projected excursion he was in great and expectant spirits.
The morning itself was one of those delicious forenoons that reminded one of Veronese. In the air was a caress and in the breeze an exhilaration and a tonic. In the streets and about the squares there was an unusual liveliness, much loud talking, a great many oaths, and the irritation and excitement which is the prelude to a festival. The entire summer colony seemed to be on its way to Spain.
In the courtyard of the Villa Zunzarraga four horses harnessed to a landau stood in readiness. On the box the driver glistened with smart buttons and silver braid. His coat was short, his culottes were white, his waistcoat red, and he had made himself operatic with the galloons and trappings of an eighteenth-century postilion. It was not every day in the year that he drove to a corrida. By way of preparation for the coming spectacle, Karl, who stood at the carriage door, had already engaged a palco.

===Chinese===
布莱登堡一家不甚讲究奢华，仍住在旅馆里。布莱登堡先生至今还没捞着用巴斯克语会话的机会；他确实试过同一个姑娘搭话——那是他在崖顶闲荡的一天遇见的一个霉乎乎的小姑娘——可那孩子拔腿就逃了，一头在荆棘丛边啃草的骡子竖起耳朵，抻直尾巴，抿起嘴唇，发出一声痛楚万状的嘶叫，弄得布莱登堡先生只得把研究推迟，等命运赐下一个更有利的时机。
他想，这样的机会在圣塞巴斯蒂安准是现成的；于是预定出游的那天早晨，他意气昂扬，满怀期待。
这天早晨正是那种令人想起韦罗内塞（Veronese）画作的适意上午。空气里有一番抚爱，微风里有一种振奋与滋补。街头巷尾、广场四周是异乎寻常的活跃：高声的谈笑、成堆的咒骂，还有那作为节日前奏的躁动与激奋。整个避暑的人马仿佛都在往西班牙去。
尊萨拉加别墅（Villa Zunzarraga）的院子里，四匹马拉着一辆兰道马车（landau），整装待发。御座上的车夫一身潇洒的纽扣和银色绦带，亮闪闪的。他的上衣短短的，短裤雪白，背心鲜红，又用十八世纪驿车骑手的绦带与饰件把自己装点得像出歌剧。一年里可不是天天都有斗牛会（corrida）可赶的。为了给即将到来的场面预作筹备，立在车门边的卡尔早已订下一个包厢（palco）。


===Original===
When Blydenburg and Milly arrived, and the party had entered the landau, there was a brisk drive through the town and a long sweep down the Route d’Espagne than which even the Corniche is not more lovely. The vaporous Pyrenees seemed near enough to be in reach of the hand, the elms that lined the roadside were monstrous, like the elms in a Druid forest, the fields were as green as had they been painted. There were pink villas with blinds of pale yellow, white houses roofed with tiles of mottled red, gardens splendid with the scent of honeysuckle, and children, bright-eyed, clear-featured, devoured by vermin and greed, ran out in a bold, aggressive way and called for coin.
“*Estamos en España!*” The carriage had come to a sudden halt. In the beauties of the landscape the journey had been forgotten. But at the driver’s word there came to each of them that sudden thrill which visits everyone that crosses a new frontier. Blydenburg looked eagerly about him. He had expected to be greeted by *alcaldes* and *alguacils*, he had fancied that he would view a *jota*, or at the very least a roadside bolero. “Are we really in Spain?” he wondered. In places of ladies in mantillas and short skirts there was a group of mangy laborers, the alcaldes and alguacils were represented by a sullen *aduenaro*, and the only trace of local color was in a muttered “*Coño de Dios*” that came wearily from a bystander. Certainly they were in Spain.
The customhouse officer made a motion, and the carriage swept on. To the left was Fuenterrabia, dozing on its gulf of blue, and soon they were in Irun. There was another halt for lunch and a change of horses, and then, on again. The scenery grew wilder, and the carriage jolted, for the road was poor. They passed the Jayzkibel, the Gaintxurizketa, the hamlet of Lezo, Passaje, from whence Lafayette set sail; Rentería, a city outside of the year of our Lord; they crossed the Oyarzun, they passed Altza, another stream was bridged and at last the circus hove in sight.

===Chinese===
布莱登堡先生和米莉（Milly）到了，一行人坐进兰道马车，先轻快地驶过城区，再沿西班牙大道（Route d’Espagne）长长地兜转而下——这条路，连科尔尼什崖道（Corniche）也不能比它更可爱。雾霭蒸腾的比利牛斯山近得仿佛伸手可及；夹道的榆树大得骇人，像德鲁伊森林里的榆树；田野绿得如同涂绘上去一般。有配着浅黄百叶窗的粉色别墅，有覆着斑驳红瓦的白房子，有被金银花香熏得华光四溢的园子；还有孩子们，眼睛明亮，眉目清秀，却被虱子和贪婪啃噬着，大模大样、气冲冲地跑出来讨钱。
「*Estamos en España!*」（我们到西班牙了！）马车陡然停住。一路景色太美，行程本身已被忘在脑后。可听见车夫这一声，每个人心里都掠过那股突然的震颤——凡是头一回跨过一道新国境的人，都会被它造访。布莱登堡急切地环顾四周。他本指望受到镇长（*alcalde*）们和差役（*alguacil*）们的迎接，还设想自己会看上一场霍塔舞（*jota*），至少也该有一支路边的波莱罗。他心里嘀咕：「我们真是在西班牙么？」本该站着披头纱、着短裙的女士们的地方，只立着一群疥癞满身的劳工；镇长与差役由一位阴沉着脸的关员（*aduenaro*）权充代表；至于本地色彩，唯一的痕迹来自旁边一个看客有气无力地咕哝的一声「*Coño de Dios*」。他们确确实实是在西班牙了。
关员做了个手势，马车重新辚辚前行。左边是丰特拉维亚（Fuenterrabia），在它那一湾蓝色上海打着盹，不多时他们已进了伊伦（Irun）。又停了一回，吃午饭，换马，然后接着赶路。景色渐次荒野起来，车身颠簸，路实在太糟。他们经过哈伊兹基贝尔（Jayzkibel）山、盖恩丘里斯克塔（Gaintxurizketa）、莱索（Lezo）村、拉法耶特（Lafayette）当年从此扬帆的帕萨赫（Passaje）、仿佛置身公元纪年之外的古城伦特里亚（Rentería）；他们渡过奥亚尔松（Oyarzun）河，经过阿尔察（Altza），又跨过另一条溪流，那座圆形斗牛场终于出现在眼前。


===Original===
The bullring of San Sebastián is sufficiently vast for a battalion to maneuver in at its ease. It is circled by a barrier some five feet high, back of which is another and a higher one. Between the two is a narrow passage. Above the higher barrier rise the *tendidos*﻿—the stone benches of the amphitheatre﻿—slanting upwards until they reach a gallery, in which are the *gradas*﻿—the wooden benches﻿—and directly over these, on the flooring above, are the palcos or boxes. Each box holds twenty people. They are all alike save that of the President’s, which is larger, decorated with hangings and furnished with chairs, the other boxes having only seats of board. Under the President’s box, and beneath the tendidos, is the *toril* from which the bulls are loosed. Opposite, across the arena, is the *matedero*, the gateway through which the horses enter and the dead are dragged out. In the passage between the two barriers are stationed the “supes,” who cover up the blood, unsaddle dead horses, and attend to other matters of a similar and agreeable nature. There, too, the carpenters stand ready to repair any injury to the woodwork, and among them is a man in black, who at times issues furtively and gives a coup de grâce to a writhing beast. There also are usually a few privileged amateurs who seek that vantage ground much as the dilettanti seek the side scenes of the theatre.
These arrangements, which it takes a paragraph to describe, are absorbed at a glance, but with that glance there comes an aftershock﻿—a riot of color that would take a library to convey. For the moment the eye is dazzled; a myriad of multicolored fans are fluttering like fabulous butterflies; there are unimagined combinations of insolent hues; a multitude of rainbows oscillating in a deluge of light. And while the eye is dazzled the ear is bewildered, the pulse is stirred. The excitement of ten thousand people is contagious; the uproar is as deafening as the thunder of cannon. And then, at once, almost without transition, a silence. The President has come, and the most magnificent of modern spectacles is about to begin.

===Chinese===
圣塞巴斯蒂安的斗牛场宽敞得足够一个营在里面从容操演。场缘围着一道五英尺来高的栅栏，栏后又有一道更高的。两道之间是一条窄巷。高栏之上便是 *tendidos*（环形石阶看台），层层向上倾斜，直达一道回廊，廊中是 *gradas*（木板看凳），而在这上头一层的楼面上，便是 palco，即包厢。每间包厢坐二十人。包厢全都一个模样，唯独主席那间更大，垂着帷幔，设着座椅，其余包厢只有木板座位。主席包厢底下、看台之下，是放出公牛的 *toril*（牛栏）。对面隔着沙场，是 *matedero*（牲口门），马匹由此进场，死物由此拖出。两栏之间的窄巷里驻着「supes」（场务杂役），他们负责盖血迹、给死马卸鞍，以及其他种种性质相似、妙不可言的差事。木匠们也待在那里，随时准备修理木栏上的一切损伤；其中混着一个黑衣人，不时悄悄溜出来，给一头还在抽搐的牲口补上一记 coup de grâce（了命的一击）。那里通常还聚着几个有特权的业余看客，他们占那块有利地势，恰如内行票友占剧院的侧幕边。
这些布置，写下来要费一段文字，看上去却一眼收尽；可就在那一眼里，跟着来一记余震——一场颜色的暴动，要传达它，得动用整整一座图书馆。一时间眼睛被晃花了：千万把彩色纷呈的扇子翻飞如传说中的蝴蝶；有种种想都想不到的、傲慢无礼的色调组合；有一大片虹霓在光的洪流里荡漾。眼睛发花之际，耳朵发懵，脉搏骚动。一万人的兴奋是会传染的；那喧腾震耳欲聋，赛过大炮的轰鸣。随即，一下子，几乎不假过渡——一片寂静。主席到了，现代最壮观的场面就要开演。


===Original===
Almost simultaneously with the appearance of the chief magistrate of the town, the Incouls and Blydenburgs entered their box. There was a blast from a trumpet and an official in the costume known as that of Henri IV issued on horseback from the matedero. The ring which a moment before had been peopled with amateurs was emptied in a trice. The principal actor, the espada, Mazzantini, escorted by his cuadrilla and followed by the picadors, advanced to the centre of the arena and there amid an explosion of bravos, bowed with a grace like that which Talma must have possessed, first to the President, who raised his high hat in return, and then in circular wise to the spectators.
He was young and exceedingly handsome, blue of eye and clear-featured; he smiled in the contented way of one who is sure of his own powers, and the applause redoubled. The Basques have made a national idol of him, for by birth he is one of them and very popular in Guipuzcoa. He was dressed after the fashion of Figaro in the *Barbiere*, his knee breeches were of vermilion silk seamed with a broad spangle, his stockings were of flesh color, he wore a short, close-fitting jacket, richly embroidered; the vest was very low but gorgeous with designs; about his waist was a scarlet sash; his shoulders were heavy with gold and on his head was a black pomponed turban, the torero variety of the “Tam O’Shanter.” His costume had been imitated by the *chulos* and banderilleros. Nothing more seductive could be imagined. They were all of them slight, lithe and agile, and behind them the picadors in the Moorish splendor of their dress looked like giants on horseback.
The President dropped from his box the key of the toril. The alguacil is supposed to catch it in his hat, but in this instance he muffed it; it was picked up by another; the alguacil fled from the ring, the picadors stationed themselves lance in hand at equal distances about the barrier, the chulos prepared their mantles, there was a ringing fanfare, the doors of the toril flew open, and a black monster with the colors of his *ganaderia* fastened to his neck shot into the arena.

===Chinese===
几乎与镇上的行政长官露面的同一刻，因库尔夫妇和布莱登堡一家进了包厢。号角一声长鸣，一名穿着世称亨利四世（Henri IV）式服装的官员骑马从牲口门出场。方才还满是业余看客的场子，眨眼间空了。主角——剑手（espada）马赞蒂尼（Mazzantini）——由他的斗牛班底（cuadrilla）护卫着，长矛手（picador）们随后，走到沙场中央，在一片 Bravo 的爆响里鞠躬，风度之优雅想必塔尔马（Talma）也不过如此：先向主席——主席举帽还礼——再环着圈儿向观众致意。
他年轻，俊美非凡，蓝眼睛，五官清朗；他微笑着，那是笃信自身身手的人心满意足的笑法，于是掌声加倍。巴斯克人已把他奉为全民族的偶像，因为他生来就是他们的同胞，在吉普斯夸（Guipuzcoa）极得人心。他穿得像《塞维利亚的理发师》（*Barbiere*）里的费加罗：及膝短裤是朱红绸子，裤缝上镶着一道宽宽的亮片；长袜肉色；短短的上衣紧贴身躯，绣工华贵；背心开得极低，纹样却堂皇；腰间束一条猩红腰带；肩头缀满金饰；头上是一方缀绒球的黑色小帽，即「塔姆·奥香特」（Tam O’Shanter）帽的斗牛士款式。*chulos*（斗牛跑腿）和花镖手（banderilleros）们模仿了他这身行头。再没有比这更惑人的装束可想见了。他们个个瘦削、柔韧、矫捷；在他们身后，长矛手们一身摩尔式的辉煌衣装，骑在马上宛如巨人。
主席从包厢里掷下牛栏的钥匙。按规矩差役该用帽子接住，这一回他却失了手；钥匙让旁人拾去；差役逃出场外，长矛手们持枪沿栅栏等距站定，跑腿们抖开披风，号声嘹亮一响，牛栏的门豁然洞开，一头黑色巨兽——脖颈上系着它所属牧场（*ganaderia*）的色标——一头射进了沙场。


===Original===
If he hesitated no one knew it. There was a confused mass of horse, bull and man, he was away again, another picador was down, and then attracted by the waving cloak of a chulo he turned and chased it across the ring. The chulo was over the fence in a second, and the bull rose like a greyhound and crossed it, too. Truly a magnificent beast. The supes and amateurs were in the ring in an instant and back again when the bull had passed. A door was opened, and surging again into the ring he swept like an avalanche on a picador, and raising him horse and all into the air flung him down as it seemed into the very pits of death. The picador was under the horse and the bull’s horns were seeking him, but the brute reckoned without the espada. Mazzantini had caught him by the tail, which he twisted in such exquisite fashion that he was fain to turn, and as he turned the espada turned with him. The chulos meanwhile raised the picador over the barrier, for his legs and loins were so heavy with iron that once down he could not rise unassisted. Across the arena a horse lay quivering in a bath of gore, his feet entangled in his entrails, and another, unmounted, staggered along dyeing the sand with zigzags of the blood that spouted, fountain-like, from his breast. And over all was the tender blue of the sky of Spain.
When Mazzantini loosed his hold, he stood a moment, folded his arms, gave the bull a glance of contempt, turned on his heel and sauntered away. The applause was such as no *cabotin* has ever received. It was the delirious plaudits of ten thousand people drunk with the sun, with excitement﻿—intoxicated with blood. Mazzantini bowed as calmly as were he a tenor, whose *ut de poitrine* had found appreciation in the stalls. And while the applause still lasted, the bull caught the staggering, blindfolded, unprotected horse and tossed him sheer over the barrier, and would have jumped after him had he not perceived a fourth picador ambling cautiously with pointed lance. At him he made a fresh rush, but the picador’s lance was in his neck and held him away. He broke loose, however, and with an under lunge disemboweled the shuddering horse.
There was another blast of the trumpets, the signal for the banderilleros whose office it is to plant barbs in the neck of the bull﻿—a delicate operation, for the banderillero must face the bull, and should he trip he is dead. This ceremony is seldom performed until the bull shows signs of weariness; then the barbs act like a tonic. In this instance the bull seemed as fresh as were he on his native heath, and the spectators were clamorous in their indignation. They called for more horses; they accused the management of economy; men stood up and shook their fists at the President; it was for him to order out fresh steeds, and, as he sat impassible, *pollice verso*, as one may say, they shouted “*Fuego al presidente, perro de presidente*”﻿—dog of a president; set him on fire. And there were catcalls and the screech of tin horns, and resounding and noisy insults, until the general attention was diverted by the pose of the banderillas and the leaping and kicking of the bull, seeking to free his neck from the torturing barbs. At last, when he had been punctured eight times, he sought the centre of the ring, and stood there almost motionless, his tufted tail swaying nervously, his tongue lolling from his mouth, a mist of vapor circling from his nostrils, seething about his splendid horns and wrinkled neck, and in his great eyes a look of wonder, as though amazed that men could be crueler than he.

===Chinese===
它有没有迟疑，谁也无从知道。只见人、马、牛乱作一团，它又脱身出来，又一名长矛手落了马；随后它被一个跑腿挥舞的披风引诱，转身追着那披风横穿全场。跑腿一眨眼翻过栅栏，公牛像灵缇似的腾身而起，竟也跟着越了过去。真是一头神骏的牲口。杂役和业余看客们转瞬间进了场，牛一冲过又翻了回来。一扇门开了，它再度涌入场中，雪崩似的扫向一名长矛手，连人带马掀上半空，掼将下来，仿佛直掼进死的深渊。长矛手压在马下，牛角正寻着他挑，可这畜生没把剑手算计在内。马赞蒂尼已揪住它的尾巴，拧得那样精妙，它只得回身；它一转身，剑手也随着它转。与此同时，跑腿们把那名长矛手抬过栅栏——他腿上腰间铁器坠得太沉，一旦倒地，没人搭手便起不来。沙场那头，一匹马躺在血泊里抽搐，蹄子缠在自己流出的肠子里；另一匹没了骑手的，踉踉跄跄，胸前如泉喷涌的血在沙地上染出一道道之字。笼罩这一切的，是西班牙天空那一派温柔的蓝。
马赞蒂尼一撒手，站了片刻，抱起双臂，朝公牛投去轻蔑的一瞥，转身踱开了。这样的掌声，哪一个 *cabotin*（戏子）都不曾领受过。那是一万人的狂热喝彩——他们被太阳灌醉，被兴奋灌醉——被血灌醉了。马赞蒂尼从容鞠躬，恬静得像一位胸声高音（*ut de poitrine*）终获正厅池座赏识的男高音。掌声未歇，公牛又逮住一匹蒙着眼、踉踉跄跄、无人护持的马，干脆利落把它抛过了栅栏；若不是瞥见第四名长矛手端着长枪小心碎步而来，它真要跟着纵身追过去。它朝他重新猛冲，可长矛手的枪已抵在它颈上，把它撑开。它却猛地挣脱，自下往上一挑，把那匹战栗的马开了膛。
号角又是一声长鸣——这是给花镖手们的信号，他们的职分是把带倒钩的花镖插上牛颈：一桩精细的活计，因为花镖手必须正面对着牛，脚下一绊便没命。这仪式照例要等公牛显出疲态才举行；那时花镖便如强心剂。此刻这头牛却新鲜得仿佛还站在自家荒原上，看客们的义愤于是沸反盈天。他们要求添马；他们指控主办方方面吝啬；人们站起身来朝主席挥拳头——换新马上场，全凭他一句话——而他不为所动地坐着，可以说 *pollice verso*（拇指朝下），于是众人喊：「*Fuego al presidente, perro de presidente*」——主席是条狗；烧死他。嘘声四起，铁皮号角尖啸，骂声轰轰不绝，直到众人的注意被花镖的姿势和公牛的蹦跳腾踢引开——它正想甩脱颈上那折磨人的倒钩。最后，挨满八镖之后，它挪到场子中央，几乎一动不动地站着，簇茸的尾巴不安地摆动，舌头垂出口外，鼻孔里呼出的雾气一圈圈，绕着它壮丽的双角和褶皱的颈项蒸腾；那双大眼睛里透着一种惊异，仿佛诧异人类竟能比它更残忍。


===Original===
Again the trumpets sounded. Mazzantini, with a sword concealed in a muleta of bright scarlet silk, and accompanied by the chulos, approached him. The chulos flaunted their vivid cloaks, and when the bull, roused by the hated colors to new indignation, turned to chase them, they slipped aside and in the centre of the ring stood a young man dressed as airily as a dancer in a ballet, in a costume that a pin would have perforated, and before him a maddened and a gigantic brute.
In a second the bull was on him, but in that second a tongue of steel leaped from the muleta, glittered like a silver flash in the air, and straight over the lowered horns it swept and then cleaved down through the parting flesh and touched the spring of life. At the very feet of the espada the bull fell; he had not lost a drop of blood; it was the supreme expression of *tauromaquia*, the recognition that skill works from force.
And then the applause! There was a whirl of hats and cigars and cigarettes, and had San Sabastian been richer there would have been a shower of coin. Women kissed their hands and men held out their arms to embrace him. It was the delirium of appreciation. And Mazzantini saluted and bowed and smiled. He was quite at home, and calmer and more tranquil than any spectator. Suddenly there was a rush of caparisoned mules, ropes were attached to the dead horses, the bull was dragged out, the blood was concealed with sand, the toilet of the ring was made, the trumpets sounded and the last act of the first of the wonderful cycle of dramas was done.

===Chinese===
号角再度吹响。马赞蒂尼带着藏在猩红绸 *muleta*（红布）里的一柄剑，由跑腿们陪伴着向它走近。跑腿们炫舞鲜艳的披风，公牛被这可憎的颜色激起新的怒火，转身追来，他们便闪到一旁；场子中央立着一个年轻人，穿得轻盈得像芭蕾里的舞者，一身别枚别针都能扎穿的行头，面前是一头狂怒的庞然巨兽。
转瞬之间公牛已扑到他跟前，可就在那一瞬里，一线钢舌从红布间跃出，在空中亮如银光一闪，掠过压低的双角，径直劈进向两旁分开的血肉，触到了生命的发条。公牛倒在剑手脚边；它一滴血也不曾流。这是 *tauromaquia*（斗牛术）的至高表达——确认了巧自力出这一条道理。
接着便是那掌声！帽子、雪茄、纸卷烟旋成一片；圣塞巴斯蒂安若再阔气些，还会落下一场钱雨来。女人们飞吻，男人们张开双臂要拥抱他。那是激赏的迷狂。而马赞蒂尼行礼、鞠躬、微笑。他安然自得，比任何看客都更镇定、更平静。忽然一队披挂彩饰的骡子跑进场来，绳子系上死马，公牛被拖走，血用沙子掩了，沙场梳洗打扮了一番，号角吹响，这奇妙连台大戏里头一出的末一幕，就此完结。


===Original===
There were five more bulls to be killed that day, but with their killing the action with which these pages have to deal need not be further delayed. From the box in the *sombra* Mr. Incoul had watched the spectacle with unemotional curiosity. Blydenburg, who had fortified himself with the contents of a pocket flask, manifested his earliest delight by shouting Bravo, but with such a disregard of the first syllable, and such an explosion of the second, that Mr. Incoul mistaking the applause for an imitation of the bark of a dog had at last begged him to desist.
The adjoining box was crowded, and among the occupants was a delicious young girl, with the Orient in her eyes, and lips that said Drink me. To her the spectacle was evidently one of alluring pathos. “*Pobre caballo*,” she would murmur when a horse fell, and then with her fan she would hide the bridge of her nose as though that were her organ of vision. But no matter how high the fan might be raised she always managed to see, and with the seeing there came from her compassionate little noises, a mingling of “*ay*” and “*Dios mio*,” that was most agreeable to listen to. Miss Blydenburg, who sat so near her that she might have touched her elbow, took these little noises for signals and according to their rise and fall learned when and when not to look down into the terrible ring below.
In the momentary intermission that occurred after the duel between the espada and the first bull, a *mozo*, guided by Karl, appeared in the box bearing with him cool liquids from the caverns beneath. Blydenburg, whose throat was parched with brandy and the strain of his incessant shouts, swallowed a *naranjada* at a gulp. Mr. Incoul declined to take anything, but the ladies found much refreshment in a concoction of white almonds which affects the tonsils as music affects the ear.

===Chinese===
那天还有五头牛要杀，但它们的被杀，不必再来耽搁本书要叙的情节。因库尔先生在阴面看台（*sombra*）的包厢里，以一种不动声色的好奇观看了这场奇观。布莱登堡靠随身扁酒瓶里的存货加了固，最早表露欢欣的方式是高喊 Bravo——只是头一个音节含混得厉害，第二个音节又炸得石破天惊，以致因库尔先生把这场喝彩错当成了学狗吠，末了只得恳请他免开尊口。
隔壁包厢坐得满满的，座中有一位娇滴滴的少女，眼睛里含着东方，嘴唇仿佛在说「饮我」。在她眼里，这场奇观显然是一出动人的哀情戏。每当一匹马倒下，她便低吟一声「*Pobre caballo*」（可怜的马儿），随即用扇子遮起鼻梁，仿佛那才是她的视觉器官。可不论扇子举到多高，她总有法子看见；而且一看之下，她那里便发出怜惜的细碎声响，「*ay*」（哎呀）与「*Dios mio*」（我的上帝）交织一片，听起来煞是悦耳。布莱登堡小姐坐得离她近得能碰着她的臂肘，便把这些细碎声响当成了信号，凭它们的起落，学会了何时该往下面那可怕的场子里看、何时不该看。
剑手与第一头牛决斗之后有一小段间歇，一个由卡尔引进的 *mozo*（伙计）来到包厢里，捧着从地底洞窖取来的清凉饮品。布莱登堡的喉咙叫白兰地和不曾停歇的呐喊烤干了，一口吞下一杯 *naranjada*（橙味汽水）。因库尔先生什么也不要，女士们却在一剂白杏仁调成的饮品里大得滋润——那东西之于扁桃体，恰如音乐之于耳朵。


===Original===
It was not until this potion had been absorbed that Maida began to take any noticeable interest. She had been fatigued by the drive, enervated by the heat, and the noise and clamor was certainly not in the nature of a sedative. But the almonds brought her comfort. She changed her seat from the rear of the box to the front, and sat with one arm on the balustrade, her hand supporting her delicate chin, and as her eyes followed the prowess of the bull she looked like some fair Pasiphaë in modern guise.
It must have been the novelty of the scene that interested her. The light, the unusual and brilliant costumes, the agility of the actors, and the wonder of the sky, entered, probably, as component parts into any pleasure that she experienced. Certainly it could have been nothing else, for she was quick to avert her eyes whenever blood seemed imminent. The second bull, however, was far less active than the first. He had indeed accomplished a certain amount of destruction, but his attacks were more perfunctory than angered, and it was not until he had been irritated by the colored barbs that he displayed any lively sense of resentment. Then one of the banderilleros showed himself either awkward or timid; he may have been both; in any event his success was slight, and as the Spanish audience is not indulgent, he was hissed and hooted at. “Give him a pistol,” cried some﻿—the acmé of sarcasm﻿—“*Torero de las marinas*,” cried others. He was offered a safe seat in the tendidos. One group adjured the President to order his instant imprisonment. One might have thought that the tortures of the Inquisition could not be too severe for such a lout as he.
Maida, who was ignorant of the duties of a banderillero, looked down curiously at the gesticulating crowd below. The cause of their indignation she was unable to discover, and was about to turn to Mr. Blydenburg for information, when there came a singing in her ears. The question passed unuttered from her thoughts. The ring, the people, the sky itself had vanished. Near the toril, on a bench of stone, was Lenox Leigh.

===Chinese===
直到这剂饮品下了肚，玛伊达才开始露出些看得出来的兴趣。车程累了她，暑气委顿了她，那喧嚣鼎沸又断然不是什么安神的药剂。可杏仁饮给了她慰藉。她把座位从包厢后部挪到前头，一条手臂搭在栏杆上，手托着纤细的下巴；当她的目光追逐着公牛的威风时，她那模样，恰似一位换了现代装束的美貌帕西法厄（Pasiphaë）。
引她入胜的，想必是这场景的新奇。光线，那些不寻常的华丽行头，演员们的矫捷，还有天空的奇观，多半都作为成分，掺进了她所尝到的任何欢愉。肯定不会是别的，因为一有见血之虞，她总是飞快移开目光。然而第二头牛远不及第一头活跃。它固然也造成了若干毁伤，可它的进击与其说是出于愤怒，不如说是敷衍了事；直到被彩色花镖激恼，它才显出一点活泛的怨愤。这时，一名花镖手露了怯，不是笨拙，就是胆小——也许二者兼有——总之收效甚微，而西班牙的看客是不肯姑息的，嘘声倒彩齐轰向他。有人喊「给他一把手枪」——挖苦的极致——另一些人喊「*Torero de las marinas*」（海滨来的斗牛士）。有人给他让出看台上的一个安全座。还有一伙人恳请主席下令把他立刻收监。照那阵仗，人们简直要以为，对这样一条蠢汉，宗教裁判所的酷刑也不为过分。
玛伊达不懂花镖手的职责，好奇地俯视着底下那群指手画脚的人。他们激愤的缘由她探不出来，正要转向布莱登堡先生讨个明白，耳边忽然响起一阵鸣唱。那问题未曾出口，便从她的念头里溜走了。场子，人群，连天空本身，都消隐了。牛栏近旁的一方石凳上，坐着伦诺克斯·利（Lenox Leigh）。

## VIII An Unexpected Guest / 第八章 一位不速之客

===Original===
Gradually the whirling ceased, the singing left her ears. Leigh raised his hat and Maida bowed in return. His eyes lingered on her a moment, and then he turned and disappeared.
“A friend of mine, Mr. Leigh, is down there,” the girl announced. Her husband looked over the rail. “He’s gone,” she added. “I fancy he is coming up here.”
“Who’s coming?” Blydenburg inquired, for he had caught the words.
“A friend of my wife’s,” Mr. Incoul answered. “A man named Leigh﻿—do you know him?”
“Mrs. Manhattan’s brother, isn’t he? No, I don’t know him, but Milly does, I think. Don’t you, Milly?”
Milly waved her head vaguely. She indeed knew the young man in question, but she was not overconfident that he had ever been more than transiently aware of her maidenly existence. She had, however, no opportunity to formulate her uncertainty in words. There was a rap on the door and Leigh entered.

===Chinese===
天旋地转之感渐渐止了，耳中的鸣唱也退去了。利举了举帽，玛伊达点头还礼。他的目光在她身上流连了片刻，随即转身，不见了。
「我的一位朋友，利先生，就在下面。」姑娘通报说。她丈夫朝栏杆外望去。「他走了。」她又补一句。「我猜他要上这儿来。」
「谁要来？」布莱登堡问道，他听见了这话。
「我太太的一位朋友。」因库尔先生答道。「一个姓利的——你认识么？」
「曼哈顿夫人的兄弟，是不是？不，我不认识他，不过我想米莉认识。你不是么，米莉？」
米莉含糊地摆了摆头。她确实认识所说的这位年轻人，只是她不太有把握，他对她这位少女的存在，是否曾有过一时的留意。不过她来不及把这份不确定用言语表达。门上响起敲击声，利走了进来。


===Original===
Mr. Incoul rose as becomes a host. The young man bowed collectively to him and the Blydenburgs. He touched Maida’s hand and found a seat behind her. A bullfight differs from an opera in many things, but particularly in this, that there may be exclamations, but there is no attempt at continuous conversation. Lenox Leigh, though not one to whom custom is law, said little during the rest of the performance. Now and then he bent forward to Maida, but whatever he may have said his remarks were fragmentary and casual. This much Miss Blydenburg noticed, and she noticed also that Maida appeared more interested in her glove than in the spectacle in the ring.
When the sixth and last bull had been vanquished and the crowd was leaving the circus, Mr. Incoul turned to his guest. “We are to dine at the Inglaterra, will you not join us?”
“Thank you,” Lenox answered, “I shall be glad to. I came here in the train and I have had nothing since morning. I have been ravenous for hours, so much so,” he added lightly, “that I have been trying to poison my hunger by thinking of the dishes that I dislike the most, beer soup, for instance, stewed snails, carp cooked in sweetmeats or unseasoned salads of cactus hearts.”
“I don’t know,” Mr. Incoul answered gravely. “I don’t know what we will have tonight. The dinner was ordered last week. They may have cooked it then.”
“Possibly they did. On a fiesta San Sebastián is impossible. There are seven thousand strangers here today and the accommodations are insufficient for a third of them.”

===Chinese===
因库尔先生站起身来，尽主人之谊。年轻人向他并布莱登堡一家集体鞠了一躬。他碰了碰玛伊达的手，在她身后寻了个座位。斗牛与歌剧有种种不同，但尤在于此：可以有惊叹呼喊，却不作连贯的交谈。伦诺克斯·利虽非以习俗为法律之人，余下的节目里也说得很少。他不时俯身向玛伊达，但无论说了些什么，都是些零碎的、随口的话。这一点布莱登堡小姐注意到了；她还注意到，玛伊达对场上奇观的兴致，似乎还不及对她自己的手套。
第六头也是最后一头牛被了结、人群正离场时，因库尔先生转向他的客人。「我们要在因格拉特拉旅馆（Inglaterra）用晚餐，你不来同席么？」
「谢谢你，」伦诺克斯答道，「我很乐意。我是坐火车来的，从早晨起还什么都没吃。我饿了好几个钟头了，饿得厉害，」他轻快地补了一句，「以至于我一直试着用想那些我最厌恶的菜来毒杀饥饿：譬如啤酒汤、炖蜗牛、蜜饯烧鲤鱼，或者不加调味的仙人掌心沙拉。」
「说不好，」因库尔先生郑重答道，「不知今晚我们吃的是什么。晚餐是上礼拜订下的。也许那时候就已经做好了。」
「也许真是那时做的。逢着节庆（fiesta），圣塞巴斯蒂安就什么都没法指望。今天这儿有七千外客，膳宿连三分之一也接待不下。」


===Original===
“I want to know﻿—” exclaimed Blydenburg, always anxious for information. They had moved out of the box and aided by the crowd were drifting slowly down the stair.
At the *salida* Karl stood waiting to conduct them to the carriage.
“If you will get in with the ladies,” said Mr. Incoul, “Blydenburg and myself will walk. The hotel can’t be far.”
To this proposal the young man objected. He had been sitting all day, he explained, and preferred to stretch his legs. He may have had other reasons, but if he had he said nothing of them. At once, then, it was arranged that the ladies, under Karl’s protection, should drive to the Inglaterra, and that the others should follow on foot.
Half an hour later the entire party were seated at a table overlooking the Concha. The sun had sunk into the ocean as though it were imbibing an immense blue syrup. On either side of the bay rose miniature mountains, Orgullo and Igueldo tiara’d with fortresses and sloped with green. To the right in the distance was a great unfinished casino, and facing it, beneath Orgullo, was a cluster of white ascending villas. The dusk was sudden. The sky after hesitating between salmon and turquoise had chosen a lapis lazuli, which it changed to indigo, and with that for flooring the stars came out and danced.

===Chinese===
「竟有这种事——」布莱登堡嚷道，他向来巴望着长见识。一行人已出了包厢，借着人流缓缓向楼梯下漂移。
在散场出口（*salida*），卡尔立候着，预备引他们上马车。
「你若肯陪女士们上车，」因库尔先生说，「布莱登堡和我就走着去。旅馆想必不远。」
年轻人对这提议表示反对。他解释说，他坐了一整天，宁可伸伸腿。他也许另有缘由，但若有，他一字未提。于是当下议定：女士们在卡尔的护卫下乘车去因格拉特拉，其余人随后步行。
半小时后，一行人已在一张俯瞰孔查湾（Concha）的桌边坐定。太阳沉进了海洋，仿佛在痛饮一大瓶蓝色糖浆。湾的两侧耸起迷你群山，奥尔古略（Orgullo）与伊格尔多（Igueldo）以堡垒为冠，以绿坡为裾。右边远处是一座未完工的大赌场，与它相对、在奥尔古略山脚下，是一簇拾级而上的白色别墅。暮色来得突然。天空先在鲑红与绿玉之间踌躇，终于择了一色青金，又转为靛蓝；以此为地，星星便出来跳舞了。


===Original===
The dinner passed off very smoothly. In spite of his boasted hunger, Lenox ate but sparingly. He was frugal as a Spaniard, and in the expansion which the heavy wine of the country will sometimes cause, Mr. Blydenburg declared that he looked like one. Each of the party had his or her little say about the corrida and its emotions, and Blydenburg, after discoursing with much learning on the subject, declared, to whomsoever would listen, that for his part he regretted the gladiators of Rome. As a topic, the bullfight was inexhaustible. Every thread of conversation led back to it, and necessarily, in the course of the meal, Lenox was asked how it was that he happened to be present.
“I arrived at Biarritz from Paris last night,” he explained, “and when I learned this morning that there was to be a bullfight, I was not in a greater hurry to do anything else than to buy a ticket and take the train.”
“Was it crowded?” Blydenburg asked in his florid way.
“Rather. It was comfortable enough till we reached Irun, but there I got out for a Spanish cigar, and when I returned, the train was so packed that I was obliged to utilize a first-class ticket in a third-class car. None of the people who lunched at the buffet were able to get back. I suppose three hundred were left. There was almost a riot. The stationmaster said that Irun was the head of the line, and to reserve a seat one must sit in it. Of course those who had seats were hugely amused at those who had none. One man, a Frenchman, bullied the stationmaster dreadfully. He said it was every kind of an outrage; that he ought to put on more cars; that he was incompetent; that he was imbecile; that he didn’t know his business. ‘It’s the law,’ said the stationmaster. ‘I don’t care that for your law!’ cried the Frenchman. ‘But the *Préfet*, sir.’ ‘To blazes with your Préfet!’ But that was too strong. The Frenchman might abuse what he saw fit, but the Préfet evidently was sacred. I suppose it was treasonable to speak of him in that style. In any event, the stationmaster called up a file of soldiers and had the Frenchman led away. The onlookers were simply frantic with delight. If the Frenchman had only been shot before their eyes it would indeed have been a charming prelude to a bullfight.” And then with an air that suggested retrospects of unexpressed regret, he added pensively, “I have never seen a man shot.”

===Chinese===
晚餐进行得十分顺遂。伦诺克斯虽说饿得如何了得，吃得却很节制。他节俭得像个西班牙人；本地的烈酒有时叫人胸怀开阔，布莱登堡先生便趁着这股酒兴宣称，他看着就像一个。席间每个人都就斗牛会和它的种种刺激发了点小议论；布莱登堡就此题目掉了一阵书袋之后，又向凡肯听的人宣称，就他个人而言，他怀念的是罗马的角斗士。斗牛这个话题是讲不完的。每一线谈锋最终都绕回它身上；于是在席间，伦诺克斯自然被问到，他是怎么恰好到此的。
「我昨晚从巴黎到的比亚里茨，」他解释说，「今早听说有斗牛，我干什么都不如买张票上火车来得急切。」
「车上挤么？」布莱登堡以他花哨的方式问道。
「相当挤。到伊伦之前还够舒服，可我在那儿下车去买一支西班牙雪茄，回来时车已挤得我只好拿头等票去坐三等车厢。在小卖部吃午饭的人一个也没能挤回去。我想落下了总有三百人，几乎闹出一场暴动。站长说伊伦是铁路的起点，要留座位就得人坐在里面。有座位的那些人自然大大地取笑了没座位的一番。有一个人，一个法国人，把站长骂了个狗血淋头。他说这是形形色色的暴行；说该加挂车厢；说他不称职；说他是低能；说他不懂自己的行当。『这是法律。』站长说。『你的法律给我拿去！』法国人嚷道。『可是省长（*Préfet*）先生——』『让你的省长见鬼去！』可这就说过了头。法国人爱骂什么骂什么，省长却显然是神圣不可侵犯的。我想，用那种口气议论他怕是叛国。总之，站长召来一列士兵，把法国人带走了。旁观者简直乐疯了。那法国人若真能当场被枪毙，倒真是斗牛会一段妙不可言的序曲。」随后他带着一点暗示着未及言明的憾恨的神气，若有所思地补了一句：「我还从没见过人被枪毙呢。」


===Original===
“No?” said Milly, boldly; “no more have I. Not that I want to, though,” she hastened to explain. “It must be horrid.”
Lenox looked up at her and then his eyes wandered to Maida, and rested caressingly in her own. But the caress was transient. Immediately he turned and busied himself with his plate.
“Are you to be in Biarritz long?” Mr. Incoul asked. The tone was perfectly courteous, friendly, even, but at the moment from the very abruptness of the question Lenox feared that the caress had been intercepted and something of the mute drama divined. Mentally he arranged Mr. Incoul as one constantly occupied in repeating *J’ai de bon tabac, tu n’en auras pas*, and it was his design to disarm that gentleman of any suspicion he might harbor that his good tobacco, in this instance at least, was an envied possession or one over which he would be called to play the sentinel. The role of *mari sage* was frequent enough on the Continent, but few knew better than Lenox Leigh that it is rarely enacted in the States, and his intuitions had told him long before that it was one for which Mr. Incoul was ill adapted. Yet between the *mari sage* and the suspicionless husband there is a margin, and it was on that margin that Lenox determined that Mr. Incoul should tread. “No,” he answered at once, and without any visible sign of preoccupation. “No, a day or two at most; I am on my way to Andalucia.”
Blydenburg, as usual, was immediately interested. “It’s very far, isn’t it?” he panted.
“Not so far as it used to be. Nowadays one can go all the way in a sleeping car. Gautier, who discovered it, had to go in a stagecoach, which must have been tedious. But in spite of the railways the place is pretty much the same as it has been ever since the Middle Ages. Even the cholera has been unable to banish the local color. There are trains in Seville precisely as there are steamboats on the Grand Canal. But the sky is the same, and in the Sierra Morena there are still Moors and as yet no advertisements.”

===Chinese===
「是么？」米莉大着胆子说；「我也没见过。倒不是想见，」她忙解释道，「那一定吓死人。」
伦诺克斯抬眼看她，随后目光游到玛伊达脸上，脉脉地驻在她的眼睛里。可那温存转瞬即逝。他立刻转过身去，忙起自己的盘子来。
「你要在比亚里茨久住么？」因库尔先生问。语气再客气、再友好不过了，可在那一刻，正因问得这样突兀，伦诺克斯生怕那道温存的目光已被截获，那出无言的小戏已被窥破。他在心里把因库尔先生归了档：一个念念不忘「*J’ai de bon tabac, tu n’en auras pas*」（我有好烟丝，你可分不着）的人；而他的算计，正是要解除此公的疑心——至少这一次，别让他以为他那好烟丝是件招人艳羡的家当，或是件需要他亲自把守的东西。*mari sage*（法语：「懂事的丈夫」）这个角色在欧陆上演得够多了，但很少人比伦诺克斯·利更清楚，它在美国几乎绝迹；而且他的直觉早就告诉他，因库尔先生不是演这个角儿的料。不过在「懂事的丈夫」与毫无疑心的丈夫之间还有一线余地，伦诺克斯打定的主意，正是让因库尔先生行在这线余地之上。「不，」他立刻答道，不带一丝分神的痕迹，「不久，至多一两天；我正要往安达卢西亚（Andalucia）去。」
布莱登堡照例立刻来了兴致。「那很远，是不是？」他喘着气问。
「不像从前那么远了。如今可以一路睡卧车。发现那地方的戈蒂耶（Gautier）当年只好坐驿马车，想必乏味得很。可尽管有了铁路，那地方还同中世纪以来一模一样。连霍乱都没能把本地色彩撵走。塞维利亚照样通火车，正如大运河上照样行驶轮船。可天还是那个天，莫雷纳山（Sierra Morena）里还有摩尔人，而且至今没有广告。」


===Original===
“You have been there then?”
“Yes, I was there some years ago. You ought to go yourself. I know of nothing so fabulous in its beauty. It is true I was there in the spring, but the autumn ought not to be a bad time to go. The country is parched perhaps, but then you would hardly camp out.”
“What do you say, Incoul?” Blydenburg asked. “Wouldn’t you like it?” he inquired of Maida.
“I could tell better when we get there,” she answered; “but we might go,” she added, looking at her husband.
“Why,” said Blydenburg, “we could see Madrid and Burgos and Valladolid. It’s all in the way.”

===Chinese===
「那么你去过那儿？」
「去过，几年前。你自己也该去一趟。我不知道还有什么地方美得这样离奇。不错，我去时是春天，可秋天去也不该是坏时节。乡野或许晒得焦干，不过你们总不至于露宿。」
「你说呢，因库尔？」布莱登堡问。「你会喜欢么？」他又探问玛伊达。
「到了那儿才说得准，」她答道；「不过倒也可以去，」她添了一句，望着她的丈夫。
「哎呀，」布莱登堡说，「咱们可以看马德里、布尔戈斯（Burgos）、巴利亚多利德（Valladolid）。都是顺路。」


===Original===
Lenox interrupted him. “They are tiresome cities though, and gloomy to a degree. Valladolid and Burgos are like congeries of deserted prisons, Madrid is little different from any other large city. Fuenterrabia, next door here, is a thousand times more interesting. It is Cordova you should visit and Ronda and Granada and Sevilla and Cadix.” And, as he uttered the names of these cities, he aromatized each of them with an accent that threw Blydenburg into stupors of admiration. Pronounced in that way they seemed worth visiting indeed.
“Which of them do you like the best?”
“I liked them all,” Lenox answered. “I liked each of them best.”
“But which is the most beautiful?”
“That depends on individual taste. I prefer Ronda, but Grenada, I think, is most admired. If you will let me, I will quote a high authority:

> “ ‘Grenade efface en tout ses rivales; Grenade
 

 Chante plus mollement la molle sérénade;
 

 Elle peint ses maisons des plus riches couleurs,
 

 Et l’on dit, que les vents suspendent leurs haleines,
 

 Quand, par un soir d’été, Grenade dans ses plaines,
 

 Répand ses femmes et ses fleurs.’ ”

===Chinese===
伦诺克斯打断了他。「那些城市却乏味，而且阴沉到十二分。巴利亚多利德和布尔戈斯像一堆废弃的监狱，马德里和别的大城市没有多大分别。就在此间隔壁的丰特拉维亚，倒有趣一千倍。该去的是科尔多瓦（Cordova）、隆达（Ronda）、格拉纳达（Granada）、塞维利亚（Sevilla）和加的斯（Cadix）。」他念着这些城名，每念一个便给它熏上一口乡音，把布莱登堡熏得赞叹入迷。那样念出来，那些城确实像值得一游了。
「这里头你最喜欢哪个？」
「我都喜欢，」伦诺克斯答道。「每一个我都最喜欢。」
「可哪个最美呢？」
「那看各人口味。我偏爱隆达，不过我想，受最多赞美的是格拉纳达。容我的话，我引一位高明的权威：

> “ ‘Grenade efface en tout ses rivales; Grenade
 

 Chante plus mollement la molle sérénade;
 

 Elle peint ses maisons des plus riches couleurs,
 

 Et l’on dit, que les vents suspendent leurs haleines,
 

 Quand, par un soir d’été, Grenade dans ses plaines,
 

 Répand ses femmes et ses fleurs.’ ” ——（格拉纳达处处压倒她的对手；格拉纳达把柔曼的小夜曲唱得更柔曼；她给自己的屋宇涂上最富丽的色彩；据说连风也屏住呼吸，当夏夜降临，格拉纳达在她的原野上，撒遍她的女子和她的花朵。）


===Original===
In private life, verse is difficult of recitation, but Lenox recited well. He made such music of the second line that there came with his voice the sound of guitars; the others he delivered with the vowels full as one hears them at the Comédie, and therewith was a little pantomime so explanatory and suggestive that Blydenburg, whose knowledge of French was of the most rudimentary description, understood it all, and, in consequence, liked the young man the better.
The dinner was done, and they moved out on the terrace. The moon had chased the stars, the Concha glittered with lights, and before the hotel a crowd circled in indolent coils as though wearied with the holiday. There were many people, too, on the terrace, and in passing from the dining-room the little party, either by accident or design, got cut in twain. For the first time since the spring evening, Maida and Lenox were alone. Their solitude, it is true, was public, but that mattered little.
Maida utilized the earliest moment by asking her companion how he got there. “You should not have spoken to me,” she added, before he could have answered.
“Maida!”
“No, you must go, you﻿—”

===Chinese===
私下场合里，诗是难朗诵的，伦诺克斯却朗诵得很好。第二行让他念出了这样的音乐，以至于他的嗓音里真带着吉他的声响；其余各行他以法兰西喜剧院（Comédie）里听到的那种饱满元音道出，还配上一小段解释性十足、暗示性十足的哑剧，以致法语知识最为粗浅的布莱登堡全听懂了，并且因此更喜欢这位年轻人了。
晚餐已毕，他们移到露台上去。月亮赶走了星星，孔查湾灯光闪烁，旅馆门前一群人懒洋洋地绕着圈，仿佛被节日累乏了。露台上也有许多人；从餐厅出来时，这一小队人不知是无意还是有意，被截成了两半。自那个春夜以来，玛伊达和伦诺克斯头一回单独在一起。诚然，他们的独处是公开的，可那无关紧要。
玛伊达抓住最早的一刻，问同伴他是怎么来的。「你不该同我说话，」不等他回答她又说道。
「玛伊达！」
「不，你必须走，你——」


===Original===
“But I only came to find you,” he whispered.
“To find me? How did you know where I was?”
“The *Morning News* told me. I was in Paris, on my way to Baden, for I heard you were there, and then, of course, when I saw in the paper that you were here, I followed after.”
“Then you are not going to Andalucia?”
“No, not unless you do.”

===Chinese===
「可我就是来找你的。」他低声道。
「找我？你怎么知道我在哪儿？」
「《晨报》（*Morning News*）告诉我的。我本在巴黎，正要动身去巴登——因为我听说你在那儿；后来在报上看见你在这儿，自然就追来了。」
「那你不去安达卢西亚了？」
「不去了，除非你去。」


===Original===
The girl wrung her hand. “Oh, Lenox, do go away!”
“I can’t, nor do you wish it. You must let me see you. I will come to you tomorrow﻿—he has an excellent voice, not so full as Gayarré’s, but his method is better.”
Mr. Incoul had suddenly approached them, and as suddenly Lenox’s tone had changed. To all intents and purposes he was relating the merits of a tenor.
“The carriage is here,” said Maida’s husband, “we must be going; I am sorry we can’t offer you a seat, Mr. Leigh, we are a trifle crowded as it is.”
“Thank you, you are very kind. The train will take me safely enough.”

===Chinese===
姑娘绞着手。「噢，伦诺克斯，快走吧！」
「我走不了，你也不愿我走。你得让我见你。我明天来找你——他的嗓音真不坏，不及加亚雷（Gayarré）的饱满，可路数更好。」
因库尔先生突然走近他们身边，伦诺克斯的口气也同样突然地变了。任谁听来，他都只是在数说一位男高音的长处。
「马车到了，」玛伊达的丈夫说，「我们得走了；很抱歉不能请你同乘，利先生，我们这已经挤了一点。」
「谢谢你，你太客气了。火车足能把我平安送回。」


===Original===
He walked with them to the carriage, and aided Maida to enter it. Karl, who had been standing at the door, mounted to the box. When all were seated, Mr. Incoul added: “You must come and see us.”
“Yes, come and see us, too,” Blydenburg echoed. “By the way, where are you stopping?”
“I shall be glad to do so,” Lenox answered; “I am at the Grand.” He raised his hat and wished them a pleasant drive. The moon was shining full in his face, and Miss Blydenburg thought him even handsomer than Mazzantini. His good wishes were answered in chorus, Karl nudged the driver, and in a moment the carriage swept by and left him standing in the road.
“What a nice, frank fellow he is,” Blydenburg began; “so different from the general run of young New Yorkers. There, I forgot to tell him I knew his sister; I am sorry, it would have seemed sort of friendly, made him feel more at home, don’t you think? Not but that he seemed perfectly at his ease as it was. I wonder why he doesn’t marry? None of those Leighs have money, have they? He could pick up an heiress, though, in no time, if he wanted to. Perhaps he prefers to be a bachelor. If he does I don’t blame him a bit, a good-looking young fellow﻿—”

===Chinese===
他陪他们走到马车前，扶玛伊达上车。一直立在门边的卡尔登上御座。待众人坐定，因库尔先生补了一句：「务必来看看我们。」
「对，也来看看我们。」布莱登堡附和道。「对了，你住在哪儿？」
「我很乐意。」伦诺克斯答道，「我住在大饭店（the Grand）。」他举帽祝他们一路愉快。月光正满满照在他脸上，布莱登堡小姐觉得他甚至比马赞蒂尼还俊。他的祝愿得到齐声回应，卡尔一碰车夫，马车随即掠过，把他一个人留在路上。
「多好的一个小伙子，多坦率，」布莱登堡开腔了；「同一般纽约年轻人全然两样。哎呀，忘了告诉他我认识他姐姐；真遗憾，那会显得怪亲热的，让他觉得自在些，你们说是不是？不过话说回来，他本来就自在得很。我纳闷他怎么不结婚？利家那些人都没有钱，是吧？他要愿意，转眼就能捡个女继承人。也许他情愿打光棍。他要真这样，我一点不怪他，一个漂亮小伙子——」


===Original===
And so the amiable gentleman rambled on. After a while finding that the reins of conversation were solely in his own hands, he took the fullest advantage of his position and discoursed at length on the bullfight, its history, its possibilities, the games of the Romans, how they fared under the Goths, what improvements came with the Moors, and wound up by suggesting an immediate visit to Fuenterrabia.
For the moment no enthusiasm was manifested. Mr. Incoul admitted that he would like to go, but the ladies said nothing, and presently the two men planned a little excursion by themselves.
Miss Blydenburg had made herself comfortable and fallen into a doze, but Maida sat watching the retreating uplands with unseeing eyes. Her thoughts had wandered, the visible was lost to her. Who knows what women see or the dreams and regrets that may come to the most matter-of-fact? Not long ago at the opera, in a little Italian town, the historian noticed an old lady, one who looked anything but sentimental, for that matter rather fierce than otherwise, but who, when Cherubino had sung his enchanting song, brushed away a furtive and unexpected tear. *Voi che sapete* indeed! Perhaps to her own cost she had learned and was grieving dumbly then over some ashes that the strain had stirred, and it is not impossible that as Maida sat watching the retreating uplands her own thoughts had circled back to an earlier summer when first she learned what Love might be.

===Chinese===
这位和善的先生就这样絮絮下去。过了一会儿，他发觉谈话的缰绳全握在自己手里，便充分利用起这地位，长篇大论地讲起斗牛：它的历史，它的前景，罗马人的竞技，哥特人治下它们如何光景，摩尔人又带来什么改进，末了建议立刻去一趟丰特拉维亚。
一时无人表现出热心。因库尔先生承认他愿意去，女士们却不置一词，于是不多时，两位先生自个儿计划起一次小小的远足。
布莱登堡小姐安置好自己，打起盹来；玛伊达却坐着，用一双视而不见的眼睛望着渐退的高地。她的心思游走了，可见之物已与她无关。谁知道女人们看见的是什么，谁知道连最讲实际的女人心里会来些什么梦、什么憾？不久前，在意大利一座小城的歌剧院里，本书的史家注意到一位老妇人——她的模样无论如何谈不上善感，毋宁说挺凶——可当凯鲁比诺（Cherubino）唱起他迷人的歌时，她拭去了一滴偷偷摸摸、出其不意的泪。*Voi che sapete*（你们可知道）！也许她曾付出代价方才学会，此刻正为那段旋律搅起的某堆死灰而黯然神伤；而玛伊达坐在那里望着渐退的高地时，她自己的心思，也未必没有兜回更早的一个夏天——她初初懂得爱情可能为何物的那个夏天。

## IX Mr. Incoul Dines in Spain / 第九章 因库尔先生在西班牙晚餐

===Original===
On the morrow Mr. Blydenburg consulted his guidebooks. The descriptions of Fuenterrabia were vague but alluring. The streets, he learned, were narrow; the roofs met; the houses were black with age; the doors were heavy with armorials; the windows barred﻿—in short, a medieval burg that slept on a blue gulf and let Time limp by unmarked. Among the inhabitants were some, he found, who accommodated travelers. The inns, it is true, were unstarred, but the names were so rich in suggestion that the neglect was not noticed. Mr. Blydenburg had never passed a night in Spain, and he felt that he would like to do so. This desire he succeeded in awakening in Mr. Incoul, and together they agreed to take an afternoon train, explore the town, pass the evening at the Casino and return to Biarritz the next morning. The programme thus arranged was put into immediate execution; two days after the bullfight they were again on their way to the frontier, and, as the train passed out of the station on its southern journey, Maida and Lenox Leigh were preparing for a stroll on the sands.
There is at Biarritz a division of the shore which, starting from the ruins of a corsair’s castle, extends on to Saint-Jean-de-Luz. It is known as the Côte des Basques. On one side are the cliffs, on the other the sea, and between the two is a broad avenue which almost disappears when the tide is high. The sand is fine as face powder, *nuance* Rachel, packed hard. From the cliffs the view is delicious: in the distance are the mountains curving and melting in the haze; below, the ocean, spangled at the edges, is of a milky blue. Seen from the shore, the sea has the color of absinthe, an opalescent green, entangled and fringed with films of white; here the mountains escape in the perspective, and as the sun sinks the cliffs glitter. At times the sky is flecked with little clouds that dwindle and fade into spirals of pink; at others great masses rise sheer against the horizon, as might the bastions of Titan homes; and again are gigantic cathedrals, their spires lost in azure, their turrets swooning in excesses of vermilion grace. The only sound is from the waves, but few come to listen. The Côte des Basques is not fashionable with the summer colony; it is merely beautiful and solitary.
It was on the downs that Maida and Lenox first chose to walk, but after a while a sloping descent invited them to the shore below. Soon they rounded a projecting cliff, and Biarritz was hidden from them. The background was chalk festooned with green; afar were the purple outlines of the Pyrenees, and before them the ocean murmured its temptations of couch and of tomb.
They had been talking earnestly with the egotism of people to whom everything save self is landscape. The encircling beauty in which they walked had not left them unimpressed, yet the influence had been remote and undiscerned; the effect had been that of accessories. But now they were silent, for the wonder of the scene was upon them.

===Chinese===
翌日，布莱登堡先生翻起了他的旅行指南。关于丰特拉维亚的记述语焉不详，却引人入胜。他得知：街道窄狭；屋檐相接；房宅老得发黑；门扇上纹章沉重；窗子装着铁栏——总之，一座在中世纪里睡着、任光阴一瘸一拐溜过去而不加记载的古堡。他还发现，居民中有几户可以接待旅客。旅馆固然没有星，店名却个个富含联想，足以令人忽略这一层。布莱登堡先生从未在西班牙过夜，他很愿意试它一试。这番欲望他成功地点燃了因库尔先生，两人议定：搭午后的火车，探一探那座城，晚上在赌场（Casino）消磨，次日早晨回比亚里茨。如此安排的日程随即付诸实行；斗牛之后两天，他们又踏上去往边境的路，而火车驶出车站一路南行之际，玛伊达和伦诺克斯·利正准备去沙滩上散步。
比亚里茨有一段海岸，从一座海盗城堡的废墟起，一直延伸到圣让-德吕兹，人称巴斯克海岸（Côte des Basques）。一侧是悬崖，一侧是大海，两者之间是一条宽阔的滨海道，涨潮时几乎整个没入水中。沙细得像敷面的香粉，是 *nuance* Rachel（「拉切尔色」）的那种，踩得瓷实。从崖上望去，景色妙极了：远处群山曲线蜿蜒，融进雾霭；脚下海面镶着闪光的边，是乳蓝一色。从岸边看，海水是艾酒的绿，一种乳光的绿，缠着、镶着一层白膜；此处山影遁入远景，夕阳西沉时，悬崖闪闪发光。有时天上缀着小小的云，渐渐缩小，淡成粉色的螺线；有时大团云块拔地而起直逼天际，仿佛提坦巨人家宅的堡楼；有时又是巨大的教堂，尖顶没入青空，塔尖因朱红的华彩太过而几欲晕去。唯一的声响来自海浪，可来听的没有几人。巴斯克海岸不入避暑客的时尚；它只是又美，又寂寞。
玛伊达和伦诺克斯起先拣的是丘岗上走，可走了一程，一道斜坡把他们引下了岸边。不多时他们绕过一座凸出的悬崖，比亚里茨从他们眼中隐去了。背景是缀满绿意的白垩；远处是比利牛斯紫色的轮廓；他们面前，海洋喃喃着床榻与坟墓的双重诱惑。
