方才他们谈得恳切，带着那种人特有的自我——于他们，除自身之外一切都是布景。环抱他们的美并非没有打动他们，只是那影响遥远而不自觉；效果不过是些点缀。此刻他们沉默了，景色的奇观已罩住他们。


===Original===
Presently Maida, finding a stone conveniently placed, sat down on the sand and used the stone for a back. Lenox threw himself at her feet. From the downs above there came now and then the slumberous tinkle of a bell, but so faintly that it fused with the rustle of the waves; no one heard it save a little girl who was tending cattle and who knew by the tinkle where each of her charges browsed. She was a ragged child, barefooted and not very wise; she was afraid of strangers with the vague fear that children have. And at times during the summer, when tourists crossed the downs where her cattle were, she would hide till they had passed.
On this afternoon she had been occupying herself with blades of grass, which she threw in the air and watched float down to the shore below, but at last she had wearied of this amusement and was about to turn and bully the cows in the shrill little voice which was hers, when Maida and her companion appeared on the scene. The child felt almost secure; nothing but a bird could reach her from the shore and of birds she had no fear, and so, being curious and not very much afraid, she watched the couple with timid, inquisitive eyes.
For a long time she watched and for a long time they remained motionless in the positions which they had first chosen. At times the sound of their voices reached her. She wished she were a little nearer that she might hear what they said. She had never seen people sit on the beach before, though she had heard that people sometimes did so, all night, too, and that they were called smugglers. But somehow the people beneath her did not seem to belong to that category. For a moment she thought that they might be guarding the coast, and at that thought an inherent instinctive fear of officials beat in her small breast. She had indeed heard of female smugglers; there was her own aunt, for instance; but no, she had never heard of a coastguard in woman’s clothes. That idea had to be dismissed, and so she wondered and watched until she forgot all about them, and turned her attention to a white sail in the open.
The white sail fainted in sheets of cobalt. The sun which had neared the horizon was dying in throes of crimson and gamboge. It was time she knew to drive the cattle home. She stood up and brushed her hair aside, and as she did so, her eyes fell again on the couple below. The man had moved; he was not lying as he had been with his back to the bluff; he was kneeling by his companion, her head was on his shoulder, her arms were about his neck, and his mouth was close to hers. The little maid smiled knowingly; she had seen others in much the same attitude; the mystery was dissolved; they were neither guards nor smugglers﻿—they were lovers; and she ran on at once through the bramble and called shrilly to the cows.

===Chinese===
不多时，玛伊达寻着一块搁得恰到好处的石头，在沙上坐下，拿石头当了靠背。伦诺克斯倒在她脚边。丘岗上不时传来昏昏欲睡的铃铛声，轻得与浪的絮语融为一体；除了一个看牛的小姑娘，谁也没听见——她凭那叮声，认得出她管的每头牛在哪里吃草。那是个衣衫褴褛的孩子，赤着脚，不太聪明；她怕生人，是孩子那种说不清的怕。夏天里有时游客穿过她的牛吃草的丘岗，她便躲起来等他们走过去。
这天下午她一直玩着草叶，往空中一扔，看它们飘到下面的岸上，可最后她也玩腻了，正要转过身去，用她那副尖细嗓门呵斥她的牛，玛伊达和她的同伴出现了。孩子觉得相当安全；从岸上够得着她的只有鸟，而鸟她不怕；于是她怀着好奇和不太多的害怕，用怯生生、探询的眼睛望着那一对。
她望了许久，他们也以起初挑定的姿势，久久一动不动。他们的说话声时时传到她耳朵里。她巴望离得近些，好听清他们说什么。她从没见过有人坐在海滩上，虽然听说有人这样坐过，还坐一整夜，那号人叫作走私犯。可底下那两人不知怎么不像那一类的。有一刻她想到他们也许是守海岸的，想到这里，一种对官家人天生的、本能的恐惧在她小小的胸膛里擂起鼓来。她确实听说过女走私犯；譬如她自己的姑姑；可是不，她从没听说有穿女人衣裳的缉私官。这个念头只得打消，于是她一边纳闷一边望着，直到把他们忘了个干净，把心思转到海上的一面白帆上去。
白帆在一层层的钴蓝里晕淡了。挨近地平线的太阳正在绯红与藤黄的阵痛里死去。她知道，该赶牛回家了。她站起来，把头发拂开，眼光又落到下面那对人身上。男人动了；他不再背靠断崖躺着；他跪在同伴身旁，她的头靠在他肩上，她的手臂搂着他的颈子，他的嘴凑近她的嘴。小丫头会心地笑了；她见过别人摆差不多的姿势；谜底揭开了：他们不是缉私官，也不是走私犯——他们是恋人。她立刻钻过荆棘丛跑去，尖声吆喝起牛来。


===Original===
The excursionists, meanwhile, had reached Hendaye and had been ferried across the stream that flows between it and Fuenterrabia. At the landing they were met by a gentleman in green and red who muttered some inquiry. The boatman undid the straps of the valise which they bore, and this rite accomplished, the gentleman in green and red looked idly in them and turned as idly away. The boatman shouldered the valises again, and started for the inn.
Mr. Incoul and his friend were both men to whom the visible world exists and they followed with lingering surprise. They ascended a sudden slope, bordered on one side by a high white wall in which lizards played, and which they assumed was the wall of some monastery, but which they learned from the boatman concealed a gambling-house, and soon entered a small grass-grown plaza. To the right was a church, immense, austere; to the left were some mildewed dwellings; from an upper window a man with a crimson turban looked down with indifferent eyes and abruptly a bird sang.
From the plaza they entered the main street and soon were at the inn. Mr. Incoul and Blydenburg were both men to whom the visible world exists, but they were also men to whom the material world has much significance. In the hall of the inn a chicken and two turkeys clucked with fearless composure. The public room was small, close and full of insects. At a rickety table an old man, puffy and scornful, was quarreling with himself on the subject of a peseta which he held in his hand. The innkeeper, a frowsy female, emerged from some remoter den, eyed them with unmollifiable suspicion and disappeared.
“We can’t stop here,” said Blydenburg with the air of a man denying the feasibility of a trip to the moon.

===Chinese===
与此同时，两位游士已到了昂代（Hendaye），被渡过横亘在它与丰特拉维亚之间的那条小河。登岸处迎接他们的是一位穿红着绿的先生，咕哝了几句盘问。船夫解开他们手提箱的皮带，这一仪式完毕，红绿先生懒洋洋地朝箱里张了张，又同样懒洋洋地转过身去。船夫重新扛起提箱，往客栈去。
因库尔先生和他的朋友都是肉眼世界于其为实有的人，他们带着迟疑的惊奇跟了上去。他们登上一道陡坡，一侧傍着高高的白墙，墙上有蜥蜴出没；他们猜那是某座修道院的墙，后来才从船夫嘴里得知，墙里藏的是一家赌场。随即他们走进一座青草滋蔓的小广场。右边是一座教堂，宏大而冷峻；左边是几所霉斑点点的小屋；一扇高窗里，一个裹深红头巾的男人用漠然的眼睛俯视，忽然有一只鸟叫起来。
出了广场他们进了正街，不多时便到了客栈。因库尔先生和布莱登堡都是肉眼世界于其为实有的人，但他们同时也是物质世界于其大有干系的人。客栈的堂屋里，一只母鸡和两只火鸡气定神闲地咯咯着。公共休息室又小又闷，虫子成群。一张摇摇晃晃的桌边，一个虚胖而倨傲的老头正自个儿跟自个儿吵，为着手里的一枚比塞塔（peseta）。老板娘是个邋遢女人，从更深处的什么窝里钻出来，用化不开的疑心瞟了他们一眼，又缩了回去。
「这儿没法住。」布莱登堡说，那副神气，像是在否认登月旅行之可行。


===Original===
On inquiry they learned that the town contained nothing better. At the Casino there were roulette tables, but no beds. Travelers usually stopped at Hendaye or at Irun.
“Then we will go back to Biarritz.”
They sent their valises on again to the landing place and then set out in search of Objects of Interest. The palace of Charlemagne scowled at them in a tottering, impotent way. When they attempted to enter the church, a chill caught them neck and crop and forced them back. For some time they wandered about in an aimless, unguided fashion, yet whatever direction they chose that direction fed them firmly back to the landing place. At last they entered the Casino.
The grounds were charming, a trifle unkempt perhaps, the walks were not free from weeds, but the air was as heavy with the odor of flowers as a perfumery shop in Bond Street. In one alley, in a bower of trees, was a row of tables; the covers were white and the glassware unexceptionable.

===Chinese===
一打听，才知道城里没有更好的去处。赌场里有轮盘赌台，却没有床铺。旅客照例在昂代或伊伦落脚。
「那我们就回比亚里茨。」
他们打发提箱先回登岸处，然后动身去寻「名胜」。查理曼（Charlemagne）的宫殿摇摇欲坠、有气无力地朝他们狞视。他们想进教堂，一股寒气兜头兜脑把他们攫住，逼了回来。有好一阵他们漫无目的、无人指引地乱转，可无论拣哪个方向，那方向都坚定地把他们送回登岸处。末了他们进了赌场。
园子很可爱，也许略嫌荒疏，步道上杂草未除，可空气里花香沉甸甸的，赛过邦德街（Bond Street）的一家香水铺。一条林荫小径上，树搭成凉棚，摆着一排桌位；桌布雪白，玻璃器皿无可挑剔。


===Original===
“We could dine here,” Blydenburg said in a self-examining way. A pretty girl of the manola type, dressed like a soubrette in a vaudeville, approached and decorated his lapel with a tuberose. “We certainly can dine here,” he repeated.
The girl seemed to divine the meaning of his words. “*Ciertamente, Caballero*,” she lisped.
Mr. Blydenburg had never been called *Caballero* before, and he liked it. “What do you say, Incoul?” he asked.
“I am willing, order it now if you care to.”

===Chinese===
「咱们可以在这儿吃晚饭。」布莱登堡用自问自证的口吻说。一个马诺拉型（manola）的标致姑娘——打扮得像杂耍戏里的俏丫鬟——走上前来，往他翻领上簪了一朵晚香玉。「咱们当然可以在这儿吃晚饭。」他重复道。
姑娘似乎参透了他的话。「*Ciertamente, Caballero*」（当然，老爷）。她口齿不清地说。
布莱登堡先生从前没人唤过他 *Caballero*（老爷），他喜欢这个。「你说呢，因库尔？」他问。
「我没意见，你高兴就现在点。」


===Original===
But the ordering was not easy. Mr. Blydenburg had never studied pantomime, and his gestures were more indicative of a patient describing a toothache to a dentist than of an American citizen ordering an evening meal. “*Kayry-Oostay*,” he repeated, and then from some abyss of memory he called to his aid detached phrases in German.
The girl laughed blithely. Her mouth was like a pomegranate cut in twain. She took a thin book bound in morocco from the table and handed it to the unhappy gentleman. It was, he found, a list of dishes and of wines. In his excitement, he pointed one after another to three different soups, and then waving the book at the girl as who should say, “I leave the rest to you,” he dared Mr. Incoul to go into the Casino and break the bank for an appetizer.
The Casino, a low building of leprous white, stood in the centre of the garden. At the door, a lackey, in frayed, ill-fitting livery, took their sticks and gave them numbered checks in exchange. The gambling-room was on the floor above, and occupied the entire length of the house. There, about a roulette table, a dozen men were seated playing in a cheap and vicious way for small stakes. They looked exactly what they were, and nothing worse can be said of them. “A den of thieves in a miniature paradise,” thought Mr. Blydenburg, and his fancy was so pleasured with the phrase that he determined to write a letter to the *Evening Post*, in which, with that for title, he would give a description of Fuenterrabia. He found a seat and began to play. Mr. Incoul looked on for a moment and then sought the reading-room. When he returned Blydenburg had a heap of counters before him.
“I have won all that!” he exclaimed exultingly. He looked at his watch, it was after seven. He cashed the counters and together they went down again to the garden.

===Chinese===
可点菜并不容易。布莱登堡先生没学过哑剧，他的手势与其说是一个美国公民在点晚餐，不如说是一个病人向牙医描述牙疼。「*Kayry-Oostay*（您老好啊）。」他反复念叨，又从记忆的某个深渊里搬来一句句支离的德语救场。
姑娘爽朗地笑了。她的嘴像剖成两半的石榴。她从桌上拿起一本摩洛哥皮的薄书，递给这位不幸的先生。他发现那是一本菜与酒的清单。情急之下，他挨个点了三种不同的汤，然后朝姑娘挥挥那本子，好比在说「其余交给你了」，便怂恿因库尔先生进赌场去，赢它一注当开胃酒。
赌场是一座低矮的建筑，白得像癞子，蹲在园子中央。门口一个小厮，号衣破旧不合身，收了他们的手杖，换了带号的筹码牌给他们。赌厅在楼上，占了整座屋子纵深。那里，一张轮盘台边坐着十几个人，用一种廉价而邪性的路数小注小赌。他们看上去分毫不差就是他们的本相——这话说到家了，再狠也说不出更狠的了。「小天堂里的一窝贼。」布莱登堡先生想，这措辞让他如此受用，以至决意给《晚邮报》（*Evening Post*）写一封信，就用它做标题，把丰特拉维亚描摹一番。他寻了个座位下注去了。因库尔先生看了一刻，便去找阅览室。他回来时，布莱登堡面前已堆起一小堆筹码。
「这些全是我赢的！」他扬扬得意地喊。他看了看表，已过七点。他把筹码兑成钱，两人一同又下到园子里。


===Original===
The dinner was ready. They had one soup, not three, and other dishes of which no particular mention is necessary. But therewith was a bottle of Val de Peñas, a wine so delicious that a temperance lecturer suffering from hydrophobia would have drunk of it. The manola with the pomegranate mouth fluttered near them, and toward the close of the meal Mr. Blydenburg chucked her under the chin. “Nice girl that,” he announced complacently.
“I dare say,” his friend answered, “but I have never been able to take an interest in women of that class.”
Blydenburg was flushed with winnings and wine. He did not notice the snub and proceeded to relate an after-dinner story of that kind in which men of a certain age are said to luxuriate. Mr. Incoul listened negligently.
“God knows,” he said at last, “I am not a Puritan, but I like refinement, and refinement and immorality are incompatible.”

===Chinese===
晚饭已经齐备。上的是一种汤，不是三种，另有几样菜，不必一一细表。但陪衬着一瓶巴尔德佩尼亚斯（Val de Peñas）——那酒之美，连害着恐水症的戒酒讲师也要喝的。石榴嘴的马诺拉姑娘在他们近旁翻飞，将近席终，布莱登堡先生托起她的下巴。「好姑娘，这一个。」他得意洋洋地宣布。
「想必是，」他的朋友答道，「可这类女人，我从来提不起兴致。」
布莱登堡被赢来的钱和酒熏红了脸。他没觉出这枚钉子，接着讲了一个饭后故事，就是据说某种年纪的男人最耽爱的那一路。因库尔先生心不在焉地听着。
「老天知道，」他终于开口，「我不是清教徒，可我喜欢教养，而教养与伤风败俗是不相容的。」


===Original===
“Fiddlesticks! Look at London, look at Paris, New York even; there are women whom you and I both know, women in the very best society, of whom all manner of things are said and known. You may call them immoral if you want to, but you cannot say that they are not refined.”
“I say this, were I related in any way, were I the brother, father, the husband of such a woman, I would wring her neck. I believe in purity in women, and I believe also in purity in men.”
“Yes, it’s a good thing to believe in, but it’s hard to find.”
Mr. Incoul had spoken more vehemently than was his wont, and to this remark he made no answer. His eyes were green, not the green of the cat but the green of a tiger, and as he sat with fingers clinched, and a cheerless smile on his thin lips, he looked a modern hunter of the Holy Grail.

===Chinese===
「胡扯！看看伦敦，看看巴黎，甚至看看纽约；有些女人你我全都认得，是顶顶上流圈里的女人，什么闲话都有人说、有人信。你愿意，尽可以叫她们不道德，可你总不能说她们没有教养。」
「我只说一句：我若同这样一个女人沾上一点亲，做她的兄弟、父亲、或丈夫，我就拧断她的脖子。我相信女人的纯洁，也相信男人的纯洁。」
「是啊，信这个是好事，只是难找。」
因库尔先生这话说得比平日激越，对这一句他没有作答。他的眼睛是绿的，不是猫的绿，是虎的绿；他坐在那里，手指攥紧，薄薄的唇上挂一线冷冷的笑，活像个现代的圣杯骑士。


===Original===
The night train leaves Hendaye a trifle after ten, and soon a *sereno* was heard calling the hour, and declaring that all was well. It was time to be going, they knew, and without further delay they had themselves ferried again across the stream. The return journey was unmarked by adventure or incident. Mr. Blydenburg fell into a doze, and after dreaming of the pomegranate mouth awoke at Biarritz, annoyed that he had not thought to address the manola in Basque. At the station they found a carriage, and, as Blydenburg entered it, he made with himself a little consolatory pact that some day he would go back to Fuenterrabia alone.
The station at Biarritz is several miles from the town, and as the horses were slow it was almost twelve o’clock before the Continental was reached. Blydenburg alighted there and Mr. Incoul drove on alone to the villa. As he approached it he saw that his wife’s rooms were illuminated. For the moment he thought she might be waiting for him, but at once he knew that was impossible, for on leaving he had said he would pass the night in Spain.
The carriage drew up before the main entrance. He felt for small money to pay the driver, but found nothing smaller than a louis. The driver, after a protracted fumbling, declared that in the matter of change he was not a bit better off. Where is the cabman who was ever supplied? Rather than waste words Mr. Incoul gave him the louis and the man drove off, delighted to find that the old trick was still in working order.
Mr. Incoul looked up again at his wife’s window, but during his parley with the driver the lights had been extinguished. He entered the gate and opened the door with a key. The hall was dark; he found a match and lit it. On the stair was Lenox Leigh. The match flickered and went out, but through the open door the moon poured in.

===Chinese===
夜班火车十点过一点从昂代开出；不多时便听见一个 *sereno*（更夫）喊报时辰，宣告天下太平。该走了，他们知道，于是不再耽搁，又渡过那条小河。归途无惊无险。布莱登堡先生打起了盹，梦见了那张石榴嘴，一觉醒来已到比亚里茨，懊恼自己没想到用巴斯克语同马诺拉姑娘搭话。在车站他们雇到一辆车；布莱登堡上车时，暗自同自己订了个聊以自慰的小约：改日要独自重回丰特拉维亚。
比亚里茨的车站离城有好几英里，马又走得慢，到大陆旅馆（the Continental）时已近午夜。布莱登堡在那儿下车，因库尔先生独自驱车去别墅。快到时，他看见他妻子的房间亮着灯。有那么一瞬他想她也许在等他，可他立刻明白不可能——走时他说过，他要在西班牙过夜。
马车在正门前停住。他摸零钱付车夫，却发现最小的是一枚路易（louis）。车夫摸索了半晌，宣布找零上他也一样毫无办法。天下哪个车夫备过零钱？因库尔先生懒得费口舌，把那枚路易给了他。车夫赶车离去，乐得发现这套老把戏依然灵验。
因库尔先生又抬头望妻子的窗子，可就在他同车夫交涉的当口，灯已经熄了。他进了门，用钥匙开了锁。门厅黑着；他摸出一根火柴点燃。楼梯上站着伦诺克斯·利。火柴一闪，灭了，可月光从敞开的门里倾泻进来。


===Original===
The young man rubbed his hat as though uncertain what to do or say. At last he reached the door, “I am at the Grand, you know,” he hazarded.
“Yes, I know,” Mr. Incoul answered, “and I hope you are comfortable.”
Leigh passed out. Mr. Incoul closed and bolted the door behind him. For a moment he stood very still. Then turning, he ascended the stair.

===Chinese===
年轻人蹭着他的帽檐，像是不知道该做什么、说什么。末了他走到门边。「我住在大饭店，你知道。」他试探着说。
「是的，我知道，」因库尔先生答道，「希望你住得舒服。」
利出去了。因库尔先生随手关门，上了闩。他一动不动地站了片刻。然后转过身，上楼去了。

## X The Point of View / 第十章 视角

===Original===
On leaving the villa Lenox Leigh experienced a number of conflicting emotions, and at last found relief in sleep. The day that followed he passed in chambered solitude; it was possible that some delegate from Mr. Incoul might wish to exchange a word with him, and in accordance with the unwritten statutes of what is seemly, it behooved him to be in readiness for the exchange of that word. Moreover, he was expectant of a line from Maida, some word indicative of the course of conduct which he should pursue, some message, in fact, which would aid him to rise from the uncertainty in which he groped. As a consequence he remained in his room. He was not one to whom solitude is irksome, indeed he had often found it grateful in its refreshment, but to be enjoyable solitude should not be coupled with suspense; in that case it is uneasiness magnified by the infinite. And if fear be analyzed, what is it save the dread of the unknown? When the nerves are unstrung a calamity is often a tonic. The worst that can be has been done, the blow has fallen, and with the falling fear vanishes, hope returns, the healing process begins at once.
The uneasiness which visited Lenox Leigh came precisely from his inability to determine whether or not a blow was impending. As to the blow, he cared, in the abstract, very little. If it were to be given, let it be dealt and be done with; that which alone troubled him was his ignorance of what had ensued after his meeting with Mr. Incoul, and his incapacity to foresee in what manner the consequences of that meeting would affect his relations with Mr. Incoul’s wife.
In this uncertainty he looked at the matter from every side, and, that he might get the broadest view, he recalled the incidents connected with the meeting. The facts of the case seemed then to resolve themselves into this: Mr. Incoul had unexpectedly returned to his home after midnight, and had met a friend of his wife’s descending the stair. Their greeting, if formal, had been perfectly courteous. The departing guest had informed the returning husband at what hotel he was stopping, and that gentleman had expressed the hope that he was comfortable. Certainly there was nothing extraordinary in that. People who dwelled in recondite regions might see impropriety in a call that extended up to and beyond midnight, whereas others who lived in more liberal centres might consider it the most natural thing in the world. It was, then, merely the point of view, and what was the point of view which Mr. Incoul had adopted? If he considered it an impropriety why had he seemed so indifferent? And, if he considered it natural and proper, why should he have been so damned civil? Why should he have expressed the hope that his wife’s guest was comfortable at a hotel? Was the expression of that hope merely a commonplace rejoinder, or was it an intentional slur? Surely, everyone possessed of the brain of a medium-sized rabbit feels that it is as absurd to expect an intelligent being to be comfortable in a hotel as it is to suppose that he can find enjoyment in an evening party or amusement in a comic paper. Then again, and this, after all, was the great question: was the return of Mr. Incoul intentional or accidental? If it was intentional, if he had gone away intending that he would be absent all night merely that by an unexpected return he might verify any suspicions which he may have harbored, then in driving to his door in a rumbling coach he had shown himself a very poor plotter. On the other hand, if the return were accidental had it served to turn a suspicionless husband into a suspicious one, and if it had so served, how far did those suspicions extend? Did he think that his wife and her guest had been occupied with aimless chitchat, or did he believe that their conversation had been of a personal and intimate nature?
As Lenox pondered over these things it seemed to him that, let Mr. Incoul suspect what he might, the one and unique cause for apprehension lay in the attitude which Maida had assumed when her husband, after closing the door, had gone to her in search of an explanation. That he had so gone there was to him no possible doubt. And it was in the expectancy of tidings as to the result of that explanation that he waited the entire day in his room.

===Chinese===
离开别墅之后，伦诺克斯·利心里掠过种种相互抵触的情绪，末了是在睡眠里寻得解脱的。第二天他在与外界隔开的独处中度过：因库尔先生没准会派个什么代表来，想同他交换一句话，而依照体面的不成文法，他理应待命，好完成那句话的交换。再说，他还指望玛伊达来一行字，某个示意他此后行止的字眼，某条实际能帮他从未知的迷雾里站起来的消息。于是他待在房间里。他不是那种觉得独处难熬的人，独处的滋养他常觉受用；只是独处要滋味好，便不该掺着悬心；一掺，不安就被无限放大了。再说恐惧若加以剖析，除了对未知的惧怕还能是什么？神经一垮，灾祸倒往往是补剂。最坏的既已发生，打击既已落下，随着那一落，恐惧烟消云散，希望回头，疗愈立刻开始。
造访伦诺克斯·利的不安，恰恰来自他断不定有没有一场打击正在逼近。至于打击本身，抽象地说，他并不大在乎。要来，就让它来，来过拉倒；唯一搅扰他的，是他不知道同因库尔先生碰面之后接着发生了什么，是他无从预见那次见面的后果会以何种方式影响他与因库尔先生之妻的关系。
在这份悬而未决里，他把事情翻来覆去看了个遍；为了看得最全，他把与那次见面相关的种种细节回想了一遍。案情的实情似乎归结如下：因库尔先生午夜之后出乎意料地回了家，在楼梯上遇见一位正下楼的他太太的朋友。两人的寒暄纵然拘礼，却客气到家。离去的客人告知归来的丈夫自己下榻在哪座旅馆，那位先生则表示希望他住得舒服。这当然毫无出奇之处。住在幽僻地方的人，兴许会觉得一场拖到午夜之后还在继续的拜访有失体统；而住在风气更开通的地方的人，则会认为这是天下再自然不过的事。可见这不过是视角问题，而因库尔先生采用的是哪个视角？他若认作失体统，何以显得那样淡漠？他若认作自然得体，又何以那样见鬼的客气？他何以要表示希望太太的客人在旅馆里住得舒服？那希望是一句随口的客套呢，还是一记存心的挖苦？当然，凡是脑子不小于中等兔子的人都知道：指望一个有灵性的人在旅馆里舒服，同指望他从晚会里得乐趣、从滑稽小报里得消遣一样荒唐。再者——这才是要害——因库尔先生的回返，是蓄意还是偶然？若是蓄意，若他出门时便存心整夜不归，只为杀个回马枪好坐实他或许早怀着的疑心，那么他坐着隆隆作响的马车赶回自家门前，就把自己证明成一个很不入流的设局者。反过来，若是偶然，这次回返有没有把一个无疑心的丈夫变成一个起疑的丈夫？若变了，那疑心又到了几分？他是以为太太和她的客人尽在闲扯些没要紧的，还是认定他们的谈话已经私人而亲密？
伦诺克斯盘算着这些，觉得不论因库尔先生疑什么，唯一堪忧的，是玛伊达在丈夫关门之后到她那里讨说法时所摆的态度。他确曾去了她那里，这一点毫无疑问。而伦诺克斯一整天守在房里等的，正是那场说法的结果的消息。


===Original===
But the afternoon waned into dusk and still no tidings came. As the hours wore on his uneasiness decreased. “Bah!” he muttered to himself at last, “in the winter I gave all my mornings to Pyrrho and Aenesidemus, and here six months later during an entire day I bother myself about eventualities.”
He sighed wearily with an air of self-disgust, and rising from the sofa on which his meditations had been passed he went to the window. The Casino opposite was already illuminated. “They will be there tonight,” he thought. “I have been a fool for my pains. If Maida hasn’t written it is because there has been nothing to write. I will look them up after dinner and everything will be as before.” He took off his morning suit and got himself into evening dress. He tied his white cravat without emotion, with a precision that was geometric in its accuracy, and to hold the tie in place he ran a silver pin through the collar without so much as pricking his neck. He was thoroughly at ease. The fear of the blow had passed. Pyrrho, Aenesidemus, the whole corps of ataraxists had surged suddenly and rescued him from the toils of the inscrutable.
At a florist’s in the street below he found an orchid with which he decked his buttonhole, and then in search of dinner he sauntered into Helder’s, a restaurant on the main street, a trifle above the Grand Hôtel. It was crowded; there did not seem to be a single table unoccupied. He hesitated for a moment, and was about to go elsewhere when he noticed someone signaling to him from the remoter end of the garden. He could not at first make out who it was and it was not until he had made use of a monocle that he recognized a fellow Baltimorean, Mr. Clarence May, with whom in days gone by he had been on terms approaching those of intimacy.
Mr. Clarence May, more familiarly known as Clara, was a pigeon-shooter who for some years past had been promenading the side scenes of continental life. He was well known in the penal colonies of the Riviera, and hand-in-glove with some of the most distinguished *rastaquouères*, yet did he happen in a proscenium it was by accident. In appearance he was not beautiful: he was a meagre little man, possessed of vague features and an allowance of sandy hair so undetermined that few were able to remember whether or not he wore any on his face. When he spoke it was with a slight stutter, a trick of speech which he declared he had inherited from his wet-nurse.

===Chinese===
可下午沉入暮色，消息还是没有来。随着时辰流转，他的不安反倒淡了。「呸！」他末了自言自语，「冬天里我把所有的上午都献给了皮浪（Pyrrho）和埃奈西德穆（Aenesidemus），六个月后的今天，倒为些个未然之事犯了一整天的愁。」
他厌弃着自己，疲倦地叹了口气，从冥想了大半天的沙发上起身，走到窗前。对面的赌场已经亮了灯。「他们今晚会在那儿，」他想，「我是自讨没趣犯傻。玛伊达若没写，便是因为没什么可写的。晚饭后我去找他们，一切照旧。」他脱下日间装束，换上晚礼服。他系白领结时心如止水，精确得像几何作图；为了固定领结，他把一枚银别针穿过领子，脖颈上连个针眼都没扎着。他通身熨帖。对打击的惧怕过去了。皮浪、埃奈西德穆、整个不动心派的大军蓦地涌来，把他从莫测的罗网里救了出去。
在楼下街上一家花店，他寻着一朵兰花簪上纽孔，然后去寻晚饭，信步进了埃尔德（Helder’s）——大街上一家餐馆，比大饭店略高一线。馆子里挤挤挨挨，竟像没有一张空桌。他迟疑了一下，正要换地方，忽见园子深处有人朝他打手势。起初他认不出是谁，直到用上单片眼镜，才认出一位巴尔的摩同乡，克拉伦斯·梅先生（Mr. Clarence May）——早年他同此人的交情，近乎亲密。
克拉伦斯·梅先生，俗呼克拉拉（Clara）的，是个打泥鸽子的好手，几年来一直逡巡于欧陆生活的侧幕边。在里维埃拉的几处「苦役营」里他很有名气，同几个最显赫的 *rastaquouères*（暴发的外国阔佬）勾肩搭背；只是他偶尔露到台前，那纯属偶然。论仪表他不美：瘦小个人，五官含糊，一头沙色头发稀薄得没个准数，以致很少有人记得他脸上究竟有没有。他说起话来微微结巴，这一口吃的，照他自己的说法，是从奶妈那里继承来的。


===Original===
He rose from his seat, and hurrying forward, greeted Lenox as though he had seen him the week before. He was anything but an idealist, yet he treated Time as though it were the veriest fiction of the nonexistent, and he bombarded no one with questions as to what had become of them, or where had they been.
“I have just ordered dinner,” he said, in his amusing stammer, “you must share it with me.” And Lenox, who had not a prejudice to his name, accepted the invitation as readily as it was made.
“I don’t know,” May continued, when they were seated﻿—“I don’t know whether you will like the dinner﻿—I have ordered very little. No soup, too hot, don’t you think? No oysters, there are none; all out visiting, the man said; for fish I have substituted a melon; fish, at the seaside, is never good; then we are to have white truffles, with a plain sauce, a chateaubriand, salad, a bit of cheese﻿—voilà! How will that suit you?”
Lenox nodded, as who should say, had I ordered it myself it could not be more to my taste, and thus encouraged, May offered him a glass of Amer Picon, a beverage that smells like an orange and looks like ink.

===Chinese===
他从座位上起身，抢步上前招呼伦诺克斯，仿佛上礼拜才见过面。他绝非理想主义者，待时间却像待一个最虚幻的乌有，也从不用「后来怎么样了」「去了哪儿」之类的问题轰炸任何人。
「我刚点了晚饭，」他用他那逗人的结巴说，「你务必同我分享。」而伦诺克斯名下连一条成见都没有，这邀请发得多爽快，他接受得就有多爽快。
「说不好，」落座后梅接着说——「说不好你合不合口味——我点得很少。没有汤，天太热，你说是不是？没有牡蛎，没有货；都出门做客去了，那人说；鱼我拿甜瓜顶了；海边的鱼从来不灵；然后是白松露，配素汁，一份夏多布里昂牛排（chateaubriand），沙拉，一小角奶酪——*voilà*！你看行么？」
伦诺克斯点点头，那意思是：就算我自己点，也点不出更合口味的了。受了这番鼓励，梅敬他一杯阿美尔皮康酒（Amer Picon）——一种闻着像橙子、看着像墨水的饮料。


===Original===
The dinner passed off pleasantly enough. The white truffles were excellent, and the chateaubriand cooked to a turn. The only fault to be found was with the Brie, which May seemed to think was not as flowing as it should be.
“By the way,” he said at last when coffee was served, “you know Mirette is here?”
“Mirette? Who is Mirette?”
“Why, good gad! My dear fellow, Mirette is Mirette; the one adorable, unique, divine Mirette. You don’t mean to say you never heard of her!”

===Chinese===
晚饭吃得相当惬意。白松露极好，夏多布里昂牛排火候正好。唯一可挑剔的是布里（Brie）奶酪，梅似乎觉得它流得不够透。
「对了，」咖啡端上来时他终于说，「你知道米蕾特在这儿么？」
「米蕾特？米蕾特是谁？」
「嗐，老天！亲爱的伙计，米蕾特就是米蕾特；那独一无二的、可爱的、神一样的米蕾特。你总不至于说从没听说过她吧！」


===Original===
“I do, though perhaps she may have had the good fortune to hear of me.”
“Heavens alive, man! don’t you read the papers?”
Lenox smiled. “Why should I? I am not interested in the community. It might be stricken with dry-rot, elephantiasis and plica polonica for ought I care. Besides, there is nothing in them; the English papers are all advertisements and aridity, the French are frivolous and obscene. I mind neither frivolity nor obscenity; both have their uses, as flowers and cesspools have theirs; but I object to them served with my breakfast. I think if once a year a man would read a summary of the twelvemonth, he would get in ten minutes a digest of all that might be necessary to know, and what is more to the point, he would have to his credit a clear profit of two hundred hours at the very least, and two hundred hours rightly employed are sufficient for the acquirement of such a knowledge of a foreign language as will permit a man to make love in it gracefully. No, I seldom read the papers, so forgive my ignorance as to Mirette.”
“After such an explanation I shall have to. But if you care to learn by word of mouth that which you decline to read in print, Mirette is *premier sujet*.”

===Chinese===
「还真是，不过也许她有幸听说过我。」
「我的老天，伙计！你不看报么？」
伦诺克斯笑了。「为什么要看？我对社会没有兴趣。它就是害了干腐病、象皮病、波兰纠结发（plica polonica），也不干我事。再说，报上什么也没有：英国报纸全是广告和干燥无味，法国报纸轻佻而猥亵。轻佻也罢，猥亵也罢，我一概不嫌；两样各有各的用处，正如花有花的、粪坑有粪坑的；可我不愿它们随早餐端上来。我想，一个人一年若读一次全年摘要，十分钟里便能消化一年里所有值得知道的事；更要紧的是，他名下还净赚两百个钟头，两百个钟头用得其所，足够把一门外语学到能优雅地用它调情。不，我难得看报，所以米蕾特的事，恕我无知。」
「这样的解释之下，我只好原谅你。不过你既不肯读白纸黑字，若愿意听口传：米蕾特是头牌（*premier sujet*）。」


===Original===
“In the ballet, you mean.”
“Yes, in the ballet, and I can’t for the life of me think of a ballet without her.”
“She must have gone to your head.”
“And to everyone’s who has seen her.”

===Chinese===
「你是说，芭蕾里。」
「对，芭蕾里；而且打死我也想不出一台没有她的芭蕾。」
「她准是钻进你脑袋里去了。」
「钻进每一个见过她的人的脑袋里。」


===Original===
“You say she is here?”
“Yes, she’ll be at the Casino tonight; I’ll present you if you say so.”
“I might take a look at her, but I fancy I shalt be occupied elsewhere.”
“As you like.” May drew out his watch. “It’s after nine,” he added, “if we are going to the Casino we had better be t-toddling.”

===Chinese===
「你说她在这儿？」
「对，她今晚在赌场；你一句话，我就给你引见。」
「我可以看她一眼，不过我估摸我另有要忙的。」
「随你。」梅掏出怀表。「过九点了，」他补一句，「要去赌场，咱们最、最好这就开拔。」


===Original===
On the way there May entered a tobacconist’s, and Lenox waited for him without. As he loitered on the curb, Blydenburg rounded an adjacent corner.
“Well,” exclaimed the latter, “you didn’t see our friends off.”
“What friends?”
“The Incouls of course; didn’t you know that they had gone?”

===Chinese===
半路梅进了一家烟店，伦诺克斯在外头等他。他在道边闲站着，布莱登堡从邻近的拐角转了出来。
「哎呀，」后者嚷道，「你没去送咱们的朋友。」
「什么朋友？」
「自然是因库尔夫妇；他们走了，你不知道？」


===Original===
Lenox looked at him blankly. “Gone,” he echoed.
“Yes, they must have sent you word. Incoul seemed to expect you. They have gone up to Paris. If I had known beforehand﻿—”
Mr. Blydenburg rambled on, but Lenox no longer listened. It was for this then that he had been bothering himself the entire day. The abruptness of the departure mystified him, yet he comforted himself with the thought that had there been anything abnormal, it could not have escaped Blydenburg’s attention.
“And you say they expected me?” he asked at last.

===Chinese===
伦诺克斯木然看着他。「走了。」他喃喃重复。
「走了，他们总该给你捎了话。因库尔像是还等着你。他们上巴黎去了。我要是早知道——」
布莱登堡先生絮絮地说下去，伦诺克斯却不再听了。原来他为这个犯了一整天的愁。这走得太急，叫他纳闷；可他又宽慰自己：若真有什么反常，断逃不过布莱登堡的眼睛。
「你说他们等着我？」他终于问。


===Original===
“Yes, they seemed to. Incoul left goodbye for you. When you get to Paris look them up.”
While he was speaking May came out from the tobacconist’s.
“I will do so,” Lenox said, and with a parting nod he joined his friend.
As he walked on down the road to the Casino, Mr. Blydenburg looked musingly after him. He would not be a bad match for Milly, he told himself, not a bad match at all; and thinking that perhaps it might be but a question of bringing the two young people together, he presently started off in search of his daughter and led her, lamblike, to the Casino. But once there he felt instinctively that for that evening at least any bringing together of the young people was impossible. Lenox was engaged in an animated conversation with a conspicuously dressed lady, whom, Mr. Blydenburg learned on inquiry, was none other than the notorious Mlle. Mirette, of the Théâtre National de l’Opéra.

===Chinese===
「对，像是等着。因库尔给你留了句再见。你到了巴黎去找他们。」
他说话这工夫，梅从烟店里出来了。
「我会去的。」伦诺克斯说，点头作别，归入他的朋友一边。
伦诺克斯沿街往赌场去，布莱登堡先生若有所思地目送他。他配米莉倒不坏，他心里说，一点也不坏；转念这没准只消把两个年轻人凑到一处，他便随即动身去找他的女儿，像牵羊羔似的把她牵去赌场。可一到那儿，他本能地觉得，至少那一晚，任何撮合都不可能了。伦诺克斯正同一位衣着惹眼的女士谈得火热；布莱登堡先生一打听，得知她不是别人，正是国家歌剧院（Théâtre National de l’Opéra）那位大名鼎鼎的米蕾特小姐。

## XI The House in the Parc Monceau / 第十一章 蒙梭公园的宅邸

===Original===
There had been a crash in Wall Street. Two of the best houses had gone under. Of one of these the senior partner had had recourse to the bare bodkin. For several years previous his wife had dispensed large hospitality from a charming hotel just within the gates of the Parc Monceau. At the news of her ruined widowhood she fled from Paris. In a week it was only her creditors that remembered her. The hotel was sold under the hammer. A speculator bought it and while waiting a chance to sell it again at a premium, offered it for rent, fully furnished, as it stood. This by the way.
After the dinner in Spain, Mr. Incoul passed some time in thought. The next morning he sent for Karl, and after a consultation with him, he went to the square that overhangs the sea, entered the telegraph office, found a blank, wrote a brief message, and after attending to its despatch, returned to the villa. His wife was in the library, and as he entered the room the maître d’hôtel announced that their excellencies were served.
Maida had never been more bewildering in her beauty. Her lips were moist, and under her polar-blue eyes were the faintest of semicircles.
“Did you enjoy your trip to Fuenterrabia?” she asked.

===Chinese===
华尔街崩了一记。两家上等商号栽了进去。其中一家的资深合伙人求助于那把赤裸的锥子（自戕了）。此前好几年，他的太太一直在蒙梭公园（Parc Monceau）大门内侧一座迷人的宅邸里广开盛宴。丈夫殒命、家业荡尽的消息传来，她逃离了巴黎。不出一星期，记得她的只剩债主。宅子落了锤，被一个投机客买去；此人一边等机会转手卖个好价，一边把它原样带全副家具出租。这是闲话一笔。
西班牙那顿晚饭之后，因库尔先生出了一会儿神。第二天早晨他叫来卡尔，同他计议了一番，便去了俯临大海的那片广场，走进电报局，寻一张空白电报纸，写下一条简短的电文，发出去之后，回了别墅。他太太在书房里；他进屋时，管家（maître d’hôtel）通报：两位阁下，饭已备好。
玛伊达的美从未如此惑人。她唇上润润的，极地蓝的眼睛底下，浮着两弯最淡最淡的半圆。
「丰特拉维亚之行尽兴么？」她问。


===Original===
“Exceedingly,” he answered. But he did not enter into details and the breakfast was done before either of them spoke again.
At last as Maida rose from the table Mr. Incoul said: “We leave for Paris at five this afternoon. I beg you will see to it that your things are ready.”
She steadied herself against a chair, she would have spoken, but he had risen also and left the room.
For the time being her mind refused to act. Into the fibres of her there settled that chill which the garb and aspect of a policeman produces on the conscience of a misdemeanant. But the chill passed as policemen do, and a fever came in its place.

===Chinese===
「尽兴极了。」他答。可他没有细说，早餐吃完，两人才又开口。
末了，玛伊达离席起身时，因库尔先生说：「我们今天下午五点动身去巴黎。请你把自己的东西料理妥当。」
她扶住一把椅子才站稳；她本要说话，可他也已起身，出了房间。
一时间她的头脑转不动了。一股寒意渗进她的纤维——就是警察的制服与嘴脸加在轻罪犯良心上的那一种。可寒意像警察一样总会过去，接替它的是一阵发热。


===Original===
To hypnotize her thoughts she caught up an English journal. She read of a cocoa that was grateful and comforting, the praises of Pear’s Soap, an invitation from Mr. Streeter to view his wares, a column of testimonials on the merits of a new pill, appeals from societies for pecuniary aid. She learned that a Doré was on exhibition in New Bond street, that Lady Grenville, The Oaks, Market Litchfield, was anxious to secure a situation for a most excellent under-housemaid, that money in large amounts or small could be obtained without publicity on simple note of hand by applying personally or by letter to Moss & Lewes, Golden Square. She found that a harmless, effective and permanent cure for corpulency would be sent to any part of the world, postpaid, on receipt twelve stamps, and that the Junior Macready Club would admit a few more members without entrance fee. She read it all determinedly, by sheer effort of will, and at last in glancing over an oasis her eye fell upon a telegram from Madrid which stated cholera had broken out afresh.
She took the paper with her and hurried from the room. In the hall her husband stood talking to Karl. She went to him and pointed to the telegram. “Is it for this we are to leave?” she asked.
He read the notice and returned it. “Yes,” he answered, “it is for that.” And then it was that both chill and fever passed away.
The journey from Biarritz was accomplished without incident. On their previous visit to Paris, they had put up at the Bristol and to that hostelry they returned. The manager had been notified and the yellow suite overlooking the Place Vendôme was prepared for their reception. On arriving, Maida went at once with her maid to her room. Mr. Incoul changed his clothes, passed an hour at the Hamman, breakfasted at Voisin’s, and then had himself driven to a house-agent.

===Chinese===
为了催眠自己的念头，她抓起一份英文期刊。她读到一种可可如何受用如何暖心，皮尔斯香皂（Pear’s Soap）的颂词，斯特里特先生请人去看货的请帖，一整栏吹捧新药丸的证词，还有各色团体请求资助的呼吁。她得知新邦德街正展出一幅多雷（Doré）；利奇菲尔德市集「橡园」的格伦维尔夫人急着给一位极其出色的二等女仆觅个职位；巨款小额，只凭一张字据，无须声张，亲诣或致函金广场的莫斯-卢威斯商行（Moss & Lewes）即可到手。她了解到，一种无害、灵验、一劳永逸的减肥妙方，收到十二枚邮票即邮资已付寄往世界任何角落；麦克雷迪少年俱乐部（Junior Macready Club）还将再收几名会员，免收入会费。她全凭一股意志硬读下去，最后翻到一片绿洲时，眼光落在一条马德里电讯上：霍乱再度爆发。
她拿了报纸，匆匆出了房间。门厅里，她丈夫正同卡尔说话。她走到他跟前，指着那条电讯。「我们走，是因为这个么？」她问。
他读了那条消息，递还给她。「是的，」他答，「为的是它。」就在这一刻，寒热一齐退尽了。
从比亚里茨起的这趟旅程无事可记。上次来巴黎，他们下榻于布里斯托尔旅馆（the Bristol），这一回仍回了那家客栈。经理早得了通知，俯瞰旺多姆广场的那套黄色房间已备好迎候。一到，玛伊达便带着贴身女仆进房去了。因库尔先生换了衣服，在一家土耳其浴室（Hamman）泡了一个钟头，到沃尚（Voisin’s）用了早餐，然后驱车去见一处房屋经纪人。


===Original===
The clerk, a man of fat and greasy presence, gave him a list of apartments, marking with a star those which he thought might prove most suitable. Mr. Incoul visited them all. He had never lived in an apartment in Paris and the absence of certain conveniences perplexed him. The last apartment of those that were starred was near the Arc de Triomphe. When he had been shown it over he found a seat, and heedless of the volubility of the concierge, rested his head in his hand and thought. For the moment it seemed to him as though it would be best to return to New York, but there were objections to that, and reflecting that there might be other and better arranged apartments, he left the chattering concierge and drove again to the agent’s.
“I have seen nothing I liked,” he said simply.
At this the clerk expressed his intense surprise. The apartment in the Avenue Montaigne was everything that there was of most fine, and wait, the Hospodar of Wallachia had just quitted the one in the Rue de Presbourg. “It astonishes me much,” he said.
The astonishment of the clerk was to Mr. Incoul a matter of perfect indifference. “Have you any private houses?” he asked.

===Chinese===
那位职员，一个肥腻腻在场的人，递给他一份公寓清单，给他认为最合宜的几处标了星。因库尔先生一一看过。他从没在巴黎住过公寓，若干便利设施的缺席叫他困惑。标星的公寓里最后一处在凯旋门附近。看完了，他寻个座位坐下，全不理门房的聒噪，手托着头想。那一刻他觉得似乎最好还是回纽约去，可这也有难处；转念或许还有别家布置更好的公寓，他便撇下喋喋的门房，又驱车回经纪人那里。
「我没有看到中意的。」他简单地说。
这话让那职员惊讶不置。蒙田大道那套公寓好到了顶点；还有，瓦拉几亚总督（Hospodar of Wallachia）刚退掉普雷斯堡街那一套。「这真叫我吃惊。」他说。
职员的惊讶于因库尔先生全然无关痛痒。「你们有独门独院的宅子么？」他问。


===Original===
“Ah, yes, particular hotels.” Yes, there was one near the Trocadero, but for his part he found that the apartment in the Avenue Montaigne would fit him much better. “But now that I am there,” he continued, “I recall myself of one that is *enchanter* as a subjunctive. I engage you to visit it.” And thereupon he wrote down the address of the house in the Parc Monceau.
It was not, Mr. Incoul discovered, a large dwelling, but the appointments left little to be desired. In the dressing-rooms was running water, and each of the bedrooms was supplied with gas-fixtures. He touched one to see if it were in working order, and immediately the escaping ether assured him that it was. He sniffed it with a feeling akin to pleasure. One would have thought that since he left Madison Avenue he had not enjoyed such a treat. There was gas to be found in the dining-room, but the reception-rooms were furnished with lamps and candelabras. The bedrooms were on the floor above. One of these overlooked the park. There was a dressing-room next to it, but to the two rooms there was but one entrance, and that from the hall. This little suite, Mr. Incoul resolved, should be occupied by his wife. Beyond, across the hall, was a sitting-room, and at the other end of the house was a second suite, which Mr. Incoul mentally selected for himself.
He returned to the agent, and informed him that the house suited him, an announcement which the man received with an air of personal sympathy.
“Is it not!” he exclaimed, “it made the mouth champagne nothing but to think there. And again, one was at home with one’s self. Truly, the hotel was beautiful as a boulevard. Monsieur would never regret himself of it. And had Monsieur servants? No, good then. Let Monsieur not disquiet himself. He who spoke knew of a cook, veritably a blue ribbon, and as to masters of hôtel, why, anointed name of a dog, not later than yesterday, he had heard that Baptiste﻿—he who had served the family of Cantacuzène﻿—Monsieur knew her, without doubt, came to be free.”

===Chinese===
「啊，有的，私家公馆。」有，特罗卡德罗（Trocadero）附近倒有一座；不过就他个人而言，他觉得蒙田大道那套公寓合适得多。「不过既然说到了这儿，」他接着说，「我想起一座宅子，迷人得像一个虚拟式（*enchanter*）。我劝您务必去看一看。」说着他写下蒙梭公园那座宅子的地址。
因库尔先生发现，宅子算不得大，可陈设几乎无可挑剔。梳妆室里有自来水，每间卧室都装着煤气灯。他碰了碰一盏，看灵不灵，立刻漏出的乙醚气告诉他灵。他嗅着它，有一种近乎愉快的感觉。人家会以为，自打离开麦迪逊大道，他再没享受过这样的口福。饭厅里也用煤气，客厅里摆的却是灯与烛台。卧室在上一层。其中一间俯瞰公园，紧邻一间梳妆室；可两间房合用一个入口，开在过厅上。这套小间，因库尔先生决定，给他太太住。再过去，隔着过厅，是一间起居室；宅子另一头还有第二套，因库尔先生在心里划给了自己。
他回到经纪人处，告知宅子合意；那人领受这话时，一脸个人的同情。
「可不是么！」他嚷道，「一想到在那儿住着，嘴里就净是香槟味儿了。再说，人在那儿是同自己在家一样自在。说真的，那公馆美得像一条林荫大道。先生住下绝不会后悔的。先生有仆人了么？没有？那正好。请先生不要烦心。说话的这个人是识得一位厨娘的，真正的蓝绶带级；至于管家，咳，狗儿的圣名哪，就在昨天，他还听说巴蒂斯特——伺候过坎塔库津（Cantacuzène）府上的那位——先生必定知道她——刚得空咧。」


===Original===
In many respects Paris is not what it might be. The shops are vulgar in their ostentation. Were Monte Cristo to return he would find his splendor cheap and commonplace. In a city where Asiatic magnificence is sold from misfit and remnant counters by the ton, where emeralds large as swallows’ eggs are to be had in the side-streets at a discount, where agents are ready to provide everything from an opéra-seria to a shoelace, the *badauds* have lost their ability to be startled. Paris, moreover, is not what it was. The suavity and civility for which it was proverbial have gone the way of other old-fashioned virtues; the wit which used to run about the streets never by any chance enters a salon; save in China a more rapacious set of bandits than the restauranteurs and shopkeepers do not exist; the theatres are haunts of ennui; the boulevards are filled with the worst-dressed set of people in the world. As for Parisian gaiety, there is nothing duller﻿—no, not even a carnival. In winter the city is a tomb; in summer a furnace. In fact, there are dozens and dozens of places far more attractive, but there is not one where housekeeping is easier. The butcher and baker are invisible providers of the best of fare. The servants understand their duties and attend to them, and, given a little forethought and a good bank account, the palace of the White Cat is there the most realizable of constructions.
In a week’s time the house in the Parc Monceau ran in grooves. To keep it running the tenants had absolutely nothing to do but to pay the bills. For this function Mr. Incoul was amply prepared, and, that the establishment should be on a proper footing, he furnished an adjacent stable with carriages, grooms and horse.

===Chinese===
巴黎在许多方面不像它本可以的那样。店铺炫耀得俗气。基督山伯爵若再回来，会发现自己的排场又便宜又寻常。在一座亚洲式的豪奢论吨从处理品柜台上卖出的城里，在一座大如燕卵的祖母绿打折兜售在小街上的城里，在一座经纪人随时能从正歌剧供到鞋带的城里，*badauds*（看客闲人）早已失去了吃惊的能力。况且巴黎也不复旧观。它素来闻名的和气与客气，同别的过时美德一道进了老路；从前满街乱跑的机智，如今绝无机会进沙龙；除中国而外，天下再寻不出比饭馆老板和店主更狠的一伙强盗；剧院是烦闷的巢穴；林荫大道上走着全世界穿得最糟的一群人。至于巴黎的欢乐，再没有比它更闷的东西了——不，连狂欢节都比不上。冬天这城是一座坟；夏天是一座炉。事实上，比它动人的去处成打成打，可持家如此省事的，独此一处。肉贩和面包师是无形的供应商，供的是最好的伙食。仆人各明其责、各行其事；只要略加远虑，再有一笔好存款，白猫的宫殿在这儿是天下最容易兑现的建筑。
不出一星期，蒙梭公园的宅子便上了轨道。要它继续运转，房客们毫无别的事可做，只须付账。这一职能因库尔先生绰有余裕；为使这个家的门面立得端正，他又给毗邻的马厩配齐了车马、马夫和马。

## XII Mr. Incoul Is Preoccupied / 第十二章 因库尔先生心事重重

===Original===
Mr. Incoul’s attitude to his wife had, meanwhile, in no wise altered. To an observer, nay, to Maida herself, he was as silent, methodical and self-abnegatory as he had been from the first. He had indeed caused her to send a regret to Ballister without giving any reason why the regret should be sent, but otherwise he showed himself very indulgent.
He cared little for the stage, yet to gratify Maida he engaged boxes for the season at the Français and at the Opéra. Now and then in the early autumn when summer was still in the air he took her to dine in the Bois, at Madrid or Armenenville, and drove home with her in the cool of the evening, stopping, perhaps, for a moment at some one of the different concerts that lined the Champs-Élysées. And sometimes he went with her to Versailles and at others to Vincennes, and one Sunday to Bougival. But there Maida would never return; it was crowded with a set of people the like of which she had never seen before, with women whose voices were high pitched and unmodulated, and men in queer coats who stared at her and smiled if they caught her eye.
But with the first tingle that accompanies the falling leaves, the open-air restaurants and concerts closed their doors. There was a succession of new plays which Vitu always praised and Sarcey always damned. The verdict of the latter gentleman, however, did not affect Maida in the least. She went bravely to the Odéon and liked it, to the Cluny where she saw a shocking play that made her laugh till she cried. She went to the Nations and saw Lacressonière and shuddered before the art of that wonderful actor. At the Gymnase she saw the *Maître des Forges* and when she went home her eyes were wet; she saw *Nitouche* and would have willingly gone back the next night to see it again; even Mr. Incoul smiled; nothing more irresistibly amusing than Baron could be imagined; she saw, too, Bartet and Delaunay, and for the first time heard French well spoken. But of all entertainments the Opéra pleasured her most. Already, under Mapleson’s reign, she had wearied of mere sweetness in music; she felt that she would enjoy Wagner and even planned a pilgrimage to Bayreuth, but meanwhile Meyerbeer had the power to intoxicate her very soul. The septette in the second act of *L’Africaine* affected her as had never anything before; it vibrated from her fingertips to the back of her neck; the entire score, from the opening notes of the overture to the farewell of Zuleika’s that fuses with the murmur of the sea, thrilled her with abrupt surprises, with series and excesses of delight.

===Chinese===
其间，因库尔先生对太太的态度分毫未改。在一个旁观者眼里，不，就在玛伊达自己眼里，他也还是同最初一样：沉默、有条不紊、克己忘我。不错，他曾让她给巴利斯特（Ballister）家送去一封婉辞的回帖，却不曾给出半点该辞的理由；但除此之外，他表现得十分宽纵。
他本不大在意戏园子，但为讨玛伊达欢心，他在法兰西剧院（the Français）和歌剧院（the Opéra）订下了整季的包厢。初秋时节，夏意还浮在空气里，他时不时带她去布洛涅森林（the Bois）进晚餐，上马德里（Madrid）或阿默农维尔（Armenonville）；晚间凉下来，再同她驱车回家，或许还在爱丽舍田园大街（Champs-Élysées）两旁一家家音乐会跟前略停片刻。有时他陪她去凡尔赛，有时去万塞讷（Vincennes），某个星期天还去了布日瓦勒（Bougival）。可布日瓦勒那地方，玛伊达说什么也不肯再去了；那里挤满了她平生未见过的一路人等：女人的嗓音尖利而不懂抑扬，男人穿着古怪的外套，直勾勾盯着她，一旦接上她的目光，便咧嘴一笑。
可等落叶时节那最初的一阵砭肤凉意袭来，露天餐馆和音乐会便都关了门。新戏一出接一出，维图（Vitu）回回捧，萨尔塞（Sarcey）回回骂。不过后一位先生的判词，于玛伊达毫无影响。她大着胆子去奥德翁剧院（Odéon），一看便喜欢上了；去克吕尼剧院（Cluny），在那里看了出骇人的戏，笑得眼泪都出来了。她去各国剧院（the Nations），看了拉克雷索尼埃（Lacressonière），在那位奇才的艺术面前浑身一颤。在吉姆纳斯剧院（Gymnase）她看了《铁厂主》（*Maître des Forges*），到家时眼睛是湿的；看了《尼图什》（*Nitouche*），巴不得第二天晚上再回去重看一遍；连因库尔先生都笑了；再没有比巴龙（Baron）更逗人绝倒的了；她还看了巴尔泰（Bartet）和德洛奈（Delaunay），头一回听到说得那样好的法语。可在一切消遣里，最叫她愉悦的还是歌剧院。早在马普尔森（Mapleson）当政的年月，她已听腻了音乐里一味的甜；她觉得自己会喜欢瓦格纳，甚至筹划过到拜罗伊特去朝一趟圣；可眼下，能把她的灵魂整个灌醉的，是梅耶贝尔（Meyerbeer）。《非洲女郎》（*L’Africaine*）第二幕的七重唱打动了她，从前没有任何东西这样打动过她；那震颤从她的指尖一路传到后颈；整部总谱，从序曲开头的几个音符，到祖莱卡（Zuleika）那与海的低语融成一片的诀别，都以猝不及防的惊奇、以一串串一阵阵过了量的欢愉，让她战栗。


===Original===
There were, of course, many evenings when neither opera nor theatre was attractive, and on such evenings invitations from resident friends and acquaintances were sometimes accepted and sometimes open house was held.
On these occasions, Maida found herself an envied bride. It was not merely that her husband was rich enough to buy a principality and hand it over for charitable purposes, it was not merely that he was willing to give her everything that feminine heart could desire, it was that, however crowded the halls might be, he seemed conscious of the existence of but one woman, and that woman was his wife. There were triflers who said that this attitude was bourgeois; there were others﻿—more witty﻿—who said that it was immoral; but, be this as it may, the South American highwaymen, who called themselves generals, the Russian princesses, the Romanian boyards, the attachés, embassadors, and other accredited bores, the contingent from the Faubourg, the American residents, who, were they sent in a body to the rack, could not have confessed to an original thought among them, all these, together with a sprinkling of Spaniards and English, the Tout-Paris, in fact, agreed, as it was intended they should, on this one point, to wit, that Mr. Incoul was the most devoted of husbands.
And such apparently he was. If Maida had any lingering doubts as to the real reason of their return to Paris, little by little they faded. After her fright she made with herself several little compacts, and that she might carry them out the better she wrote to Lenox a short, decisive note. She determined that he should never enter her life again. It was no longer his, he had let it go without an effort to detain it, and in Biarritz if it had seemed that he still held the key of her heart, it was owing as much to the unexpectedness of his presence as to the languors of the afternoons. In marrying, she had meant to be brave; indeed, she had been so﻿—when there was no danger; and if in spite of her intentions she had faltered, the faltering had at least served as a lesson which she would never need to learn again. Over the cinders of her youth she would write a Requiescat. Her girlhood had been her own to give, but her womanhood she had pledged to another.

===Chinese===
当然，也有许多晚上，歌剧和戏园都引不起兴致；这样的晚上，定居此地的朋友故旧的邀请，有时应下了，有时便自家敞门待客。
在这些场合，玛伊达发觉自己是位受人艳羡的新娘。不光是她丈夫阔得买得下一个公国、转手捐给慈善事业，也不光是他愿意把她女人心里想要的一切都给她，而是：无论厅堂里多么人满为患，他似乎只意识到一个女人的存在，而这女人便是他的妻子。有轻浮之辈说，这种态度市侩气；另有几位——更俏皮些——说它伤风败俗；可无论如何，那些自称将军的南美剪径强人、俄国的王妃公主、罗马尼亚的波雅尔贵人（boyards）、使馆随员、大使，连同其余一切领了国书的讨厌人物、圣日耳曼老城（the Faubourg）来的一支人马、那些美国侨民——这批人纵然成群结队送上拉肢刑架，也拷问不出他们中间谁曾有过一个独创的念头——所有这些人，再撒上零星几个西班牙人和英国人，事实上，整个花都名流（*Tout-Paris*），都正如人家指望的那样，在这一点上众口一词，那就是：因库尔先生是天下最挚爱妻子的丈夫。
而他显然也真是这样的人。倘若玛伊达对于他们回巴黎的真实缘由还有什么残存的疑心，这疑心也一点一点淡了。受了那场惊吓之后，她同自己私下订下几条小小的盟约；为了更好地履行它们，她给伦诺克斯写去一封简短决绝的字条。她打定主意，永远不许他再走进她的生命。那生命已不再属于他；他曾由它去，不曾费一点力气挽留；而在比亚里茨，他若显得仍握着她心扉的钥匙，那既是因为他的不期而至，也一样是因为那些午后的困慵。出嫁时，她本打算拿出勇气来；她确实勇敢过——在没有危险的时候；倘若违背本意她动摇过，那动摇至少成了一课，一课她此生无须再学的功课。她要在青春的余烬上，写一篇《愿灵安息》（Requiescat）。她的少女时代是她自己的，尽可以由她拿出来赠人；而她的妇人岁月，她已许给了另一个人。


===Original===
As she thought of these things she wondered at her husband. He had done what she had hardly dared to expect﻿—he had observed their antenuptial agreement to the letter. A brother could not have treated her with greater respect. Surely if ever a man set out to win his wife’s affection he had chosen the surest way. And why had he so acted if it were not as he had said, that given time and opportunity he *would* win her affection. He was doing so, Maida felt, and with infinitely greater speed than she had ever deemed possible. Beside, if the mangled remnants of her heart seemed attractive, why should he be debarred from their possession? Yet, that was precisely the point; he did not know of the mangled remnants, he thought her heart-whole and virginal. But what would he do if he learned the truth? And as she wondered, suddenly the consciousness came to her that she was living with a stranger.
Heretofore she had not puzzled over the possible intricacies of her husband’s inner nature. She had known that he was of a grave and silent disposition, and as such she had been content to accept him, without question or query. But as she collected some of the scattered threads and memories of their life in common, it seemed to her that latterly he had become even graver and more silent than before. And this merely when they were alone. In the presence of a third person, when they went abroad as guests, or when they remained at home as hosts, he put his gravity aside like a garment. He encouraged her in whatever conversation she might have engaged in, he aided her with a word or a suggestion, he made a point of consulting her openly, and smiled approvingly at any bright remark she chanced to make.
But when they were alone, unless she personally addressed him, he seldom spoke, and the answers that he gave her, while perfectly courteous in tone and couching, struck her, now that she reflected, as automatic, like phrases learned by rote. It is true they were rarely alone. In the mornings he busied himself with his correspondence, and in the afternoons she found herself fully occupied with shops and visits, while in the evenings there was usually a dinner, a play, or a reception, sometimes all three. Since the season had begun, it was only now and then, once in ten days perhaps, that an evening was passed *en tête-à-tête*. On such occasions he would take up a book and read persistently, or he would smoke, flicking the ashes from the cigar abstractedly with his little finger, and so sit motionless for hours, his eyes fixed on the cornice.

===Chinese===
想到这些，她对丈夫暗暗称奇。他做到了她几乎不敢指望的事——他们婚前的那纸约定，他一字一句都照办了。就是亲哥哥待她，也敬重不过如此。倘若世上真有男人存心要赢取妻子的爱，那他选中的无疑是最稳当的一条路。而若不是如他自己所说——只消给予时间和机会，他*定然*能赢得她的爱——他又为何如此行事呢？玛伊达觉得，他正在赢得，而且快得远远出于她一向所以为的可能。再者，倘若她那颗心血肉模糊的残块在他眼里仍有吸引，他为何不该据而有之？可症结恰恰在此；他并不知道那些血肉模糊的残块，他以为她的心完好无损、纯洁无瑕。可他一旦得知真相，会怎么做呢？她正纳闷，忽然心里明白过来：她是同一个陌生人生活在一起。
在此之前，她从不曾为丈夫内心深处可能藏着的曲折费过思量。她知道他性情庄重而寡言，她也满足于照这样接纳他，不疑不问。可当她把两人共同生活里散乱的线头和记忆拾拢起一些，她便觉得，他近来比先前更庄重、更寡言了。而这偏偏只在两人独处的时候。一旦有第三人在场——出门做客也好，在家做东也好——他便把那份庄重像件衣裳似的卸在一旁。她谈着什么，他都从旁鼓劲，用一个字、一句提点帮衬她，特意当众同她商量事情，她偶尔说出一句妙语，他便含笑赞许。
可两人独处时，除非她先开口，他很少说话；而他给她的答话，语气措辞虽则礼数周全，如今回想起来，却叫她觉得机械，像背熟了的套话。的确，他们难得独处。上午他忙着料理信件，下午她的时光叫铺子和拜客占得满满当当，晚上照例有宴席、有戏，或是一场招待会，有时三样俱全。社交季开始以来，能*en tête-à-tête*（两人对坐独处）过一个晚上的，不过偶尔一回，兴许十天一次。逢这种时候，他便拿起一本书孜孜地读，或者抽雪茄，心不在焉地用小指弹去烟灰，就这么几个钟头坐着不动，眼睛定定地望着檐板。


===Original===
It was this silence that puzzled her. It was evident that he was thinking of something, but of what? It could not be archaeology, he seemed to have given it up, and he was not a metaphysician, the only thinker, be it said, to whom silence is at all times permissible.
At first she feared that his preoccupation might in some way be connected with the episodes at Biarritz, but this fear faded. Mr. Incoul had been made a member of the Cercle des Capucines, and now and then looked in there ostensibly to glance at the papers or to take a hand at whist. One day he said casually, “I saw your friend Leigh at the club. You might ask him to dinner.” The invitation was sent, but Lenox had regretted. After that incident it was impossible for her to suppose that her husband’s preoccupation was in anywise connected with the intimacy which had subsisted between the young man and herself.
There seemed left to her then but one tenable supposition. Her husband had been indulgence personified. He had been courteous, refined and foreseeing, in fact a gentleman, and, if silent, was it not possible that the silence was due to a self-restraining delicacy, to a feeling that did he speak he would plead, and that, perhaps, when pleading would be distasteful to her?
To this solution Maida inclined. It was indeed the only one at which she could arrive, and, moreover, it conveyed that little bouquet of flattery which has been found grateful by many far less young and feminine than she. And so, one evening, for the further elucidation of the enigma, and with the idea that perhaps it needed but a word from her to cause her husband to say something of that which was on his mind, and which she was at once longing and dreading to hear﻿—one evening when he had seemed particularly abstracted, she bent forward and said, “Harmon, of what are you thinking?”

===Chinese===
叫她纳闷的正是这份沉默。他分明在思量一件事，可思量的是什么呢？总不会是考古学，他似乎早已撂下了；他又不是形而上学家——容我说一句，那是唯一一种无论何时沉默都情有可原的思想家。
起初她怕他那桩心事同比亚里茨的几段过节有几分牵连，可这层怕意渐渐消了。因库尔先生被接纳为嘉布遣会俱乐部（Cercle des Capucines）的会员，时不时去那儿露一面，名义上是翻翻报纸，或凑一手惠斯特牌（whist）。有一天他随口说道：「在俱乐部看见你的朋友利了。你不妨请他来吃顿饭。」请帖发了出去，伦诺克斯却婉辞了。经此一节，她再没法设想，丈夫的心事同那年轻人与她之间旧日的那段亲昵还有什么相干。
这样看来，她手里似乎只剩下一个立得住的猜想。她丈夫一直是宽纵的化身。他殷勤、雅致、处处有先见，十足一位绅士；倘若他沉默，这沉默会不会正出于一种自我克制的体贴，出于一种感觉——生怕一开口便成了恳求，而恳求，或许又要惹她生厌？
玛伊达偏向于这个解答。这也确乎是她所能得出的唯一一个；何况其中还带着那一小束奉承的芬芳——多少远不如她年轻、远不如她女儿气的人，都曾领受得满心熨帖。于是，一天晚上，为进一步参透这个谜，又想着或许只消她一句话，便能让丈夫吐露一点他的心事——那心事她既渴望听到，又生怕听到——那天晚上他显得格外出神，她便俯身向前，说道：「哈蒙，你在想什么？」


===Original===
She had never called him by his given name before. He started, and half turned.
“Of you,” he answered.
But Maida’s heart sank. She saw that his eyes were not in hers, that they looked over and beyond her, as though they followed the fringes of an escaping dream.

===Chinese===
她从没叫过他的教名。他一惊，侧过半个身子。
「想的是你。」他答。
可玛伊达的心沉了下去。她看见他的眼睛并没有落在她眼里，而是越过她、望到她身后去，仿佛追随着一场正在遁走的梦的边影。

## XIII What May Be Heard in a Greenroom / 第十三章 后台听得见的

===Original===

One evening in November a new ballet was given at the Opéra. Its production had been heralded in the manner which has found most favor with Parisian impressarii. The dead walls of the capital were not adorned with colored lithographs. The advertising sheets held no notice of the coming performance. But for several weeks previous the columns of the liveliest journals had teemed with items and discreet indiscretions.

Through these measures the curiosity of the Tout-Paris had been coerced afresh, and, when the curtain, after falling on the second act of the *Favorite* parted again before the new ballet, there was hardly a vacant seat in the house.

The box which Mr. Incoul had taken for the season was on what is known as the grand tier. It was roomy, holding eight comfortably and twelve if need be. But Maida, who was adverse to anything that suggested crowding, was always disinclined to ask more than five or six to share it with her, and on the particular evening to which allusion is made she extended her hospitality to but four people: Mr. and Mrs. Wainwaring and their daughter, New Yorkers like herself, and the Duc de la Dèche, a nobleman who served as figurehead to the Cercle des Capucines, and who, so ran the gossip, was anxious to effect an exchange of his coroneted freedom for the possession of Miss Wainwaring and a bundle or two of her father’s securities.

===Chinese===

十一月的一个晚上，歌剧院（the Opéra）上演了一出新芭蕾。它的推出所用的宣传之道，正是最讨巴黎主办人们欢心的那一种。首都一面面无窗的空白墙上，并没有彩色石版画的装点；广告报页上，也登不出这场演出的消息。只是在此前好几个星期里，几家最热闹的报纸专栏早已塞满了种种短讯，以及点到即止的“无意泄露”。

靠这些手段，整个上流巴黎（*Tout-Paris*）的好奇心被重新吊了起来；当大幕在歌剧《宠姬》（*Favorite*）第二幕后落下、又在新芭蕾开幕前提起时，剧场里几乎寻不出一个空座。

因库尔先生为整个演出季包下的包厢，在所谓的“大包厢层”（grand tier）。包厢宽绰，坐八个人舒舒服服，必要时挤十二个也行。但玛伊达对一切让人想到拥挤的东西都心怀抵触，总不肯邀超过五六个的人来同坐；在所说的这个晚上，她招待的客人只有四位：韦恩韦林先生和太太以及他们的女儿——和她一样都是纽约人——还有德·拉·德什公爵。这位贵族是嘉布遣会俱乐部（Cercle des Capucines）挂名的招牌人物；据流言说，他正急于做成一笔交易：拿他这顶冠冕之下的自由身，换韦恩韦林小姐到手，外带她父亲的一两捆证券。

===Original===

During the entr’acte that preceded the ballet the box was invaded by a number of visitors, young men who were indebted to Maida for a dinner or a cup of tea and by others who hoped that such indebtedness was still in store for them; there came, too, a popular artist who wished to paint Maida’s portrait for the coming Salon and an author who may have had much cleverness, but who never displayed it to anyone.

As the invasion threatened to continue Mr. Incoul went out in the corridor, where he was presently joined by the duke, who suggested that they should visit the foyer. They made their way down the giant stair and turning through the lobby passed on through the corridor that circles the stalls until they reached a door guarded from non-subscribers by a Suisse about whose neck there drooped a medallioned chain of silver. By him the door was opened wide and the two men passed on through a forest of side scenes till the *foyer de la danse* was reached.

It was a spacious apartment, well lighted and lined with mirrors; the furniture was meagre, a dozen or more chairs and lounges of red plush. It was not beautiful, but then what market ever is? To Mr. Incoul it was brilliant as a café, and equally vulgar. From dressing-rooms above and beyond there came a stream of willowy girls. Few among them were pretty, and some there were whose faces were repulsive, but the majority were young; some indeed, the rats, as they are called, were mere children. Here and there was a mother of the Mme. Cardinal type, armed with an umbrella and prepared to listen to offers. As a rule, however, the young ladies of the ballet were quite able to attend to any little matter of business without maternal assistance. The Italian element was easily distinguishable. There was the ultra darkness of the eye, the faint umber of the skin, the richer vitality, in fact, of which the anemic daughters of Paris were unpossessed. And now and then the Gothic gutturals of the Spanish were heard, preceded by a wave of garlic.

===Chinese===

在芭蕾开演之前的那段幕间休息（*entr’acte*）里，包厢让一群访客给占了：有欠过玛伊达一顿晚餐或一杯茶的年轻人，也有巴望这份人情日后还会轮到自己头上的；来的还有一位走红画家，想为下一届沙龙（Salon）画玛伊达的肖像；以及一位作家——此君或许满腹聪明，只是从不曾向任何人展露分毫。

眼见这场“入侵”大有蔓延下去之势，因库尔先生便踱到走廊里去，公爵随即也跟了出来，提议两人到后台的休息室走走。他们走下那道巨大的阶梯，穿过前厅拐了个弯，沿环绕正厅座席的走廊一路走去，来到一扇门前。守门的是一名瑞士门房（*Suisse*），防的是没订正厅季票的人；他的脖子上垂着一条坠满徽牌的银链。他把门大大敞开，两个人穿行在侧幕的森林里，一直走到舞蹈休息室（*foyer de la danse*）。

那是一间宽敞的厅堂，灯火通明，四壁镶着镜子；家具却颇简陋，十几把红长毛绒的椅子和躺榻而已。它算不上美——可话说回来，哪个市场又美过？在因库尔先生看来，它像咖啡馆一样辉煌，也一样俗气。楼上下和尽头的化妆间里，源源走出一批批身条柔软的姑娘。她们中间漂亮的寥寥无几，有几张脸甚至惹人嫌恶，但大多数人都年轻；有几个——就是人称“小耗子”（rats）的那些——干脆还是孩子。间或也有一位卡迪纳尔太太（Mme. Cardinal）式的母亲，手里撑一把伞，随时预备听取开价。不过照例说来，芭蕾舞团的小姐们全然能够不用母亲帮忙，自行料理任何一桩小小的生意。意大利血统一眼便能认出：眼珠黑到极处，皮肤带一抹淡淡的赭色，总之具备巴黎那些贫血的女儿们所欠缺的更丰盈的元气。不时还可听见西班牙语那种哥特式的喉音，先遣开道的是一股大蒜的浪潮。

===Original===

That night the subscribers to the stalls were out in full force. There were Jew bankers in plenty, there were detachments from the Jockey and the Mirletons, one or two foreign representatives, a few high functionaries, the Minister of the Interior, and he of the Fine Arts, a member of the imperial family of Russia, a number of stock brokers and an Arab Sheik flanked by an interpreter.

Before the curtain rose, battalions of ballerines formed on the stage, and after the performance began they were succeeded by others, the first contingent returning to the dressing-rooms or loitering in the foyer. In this way there was a constant coming and going accompanied by the murmur of the spectators beyond and the upper notes of the flute.

Mr. Incoul was growing weary; he would have returned to the box, but he was joined by acquaintances that he had made at the club, Frenchmen mainly, friends of his companion, and presently he found himself surrounded by a group of *viveurs*, men about town, who had their Paris at the end of their gloves, and to whom it held no secrets. They had dined and talked animatedly in ends and remnants of phrases in a sort of verbal telegraphy; an exclamation helped by a gesture sufficing as often as not for the full conveyance of their thought.

===Chinese===

这天晚上，订正厅季票的先生们倾巢出动。犹太银行家多的是；有赛马俱乐部（Jockey）和米勒通俱乐部（Mirletons）来的小队人马，有一两位外国代表，几名高级官员，内政部长和美术部长各一位，一位俄国皇室成员，若干股票经纪人，还有一位阿拉伯酋长，旁边站着给他传话的译员。

开演之前，一队队舞女已在台上列阵；演出开始后，另一批接了上去，头一批人便回化妆间去，或在休息室里闲荡。就这样人来人往川流不息，伴着幕外观众的低语和笛声的高音。

因库尔先生渐渐腻了；他本想回包厢去，却被他在俱乐部结识的几个熟人缠住——多半是法国人，是他同伴的朋友——不多时，他发觉自己已被一群*浪荡子*（*viveurs*）围在中央。这帮城中闲人把巴黎把玩于手套指尖之上，巴黎对他们毫无秘密可言。他们吃过晚饭，正以一种言语电报，拿半截句子和字眼的残片谈得热火朝天；往往一声感叹，再帮上一个手势，便足以把意思传个十足。

===Original===

Mr. Incoul spoke French with tolerable ease, but having nothing of moment to say, he held his tongue, contenting himself with listening to the words of those who stood about him. And as he listened, the name of Mirette caught his ear. The programme had already informed him that it was she who was to assume the principal role in the new ballet, consequently he was not unfamiliar with it, but of the woman herself he knew nothing, and he listened idly, indifferent to ampler information. But at once his interest quickened; his immediate neighbor had mentioned her in connection with one whom he knew.

“They came up from Biarritz together,” he heard him say. “She went there with Chose, that Russian.”

“Balaguine?”

“Precisely.”

===Chinese===

因库尔先生法文说得相当流利，但因无要事可说，便三缄其口，只管听周围那些人讲话。听着听着，米蕾特的名字钻进了他的耳朵。节目单早已告诉他，新芭蕾的主角便由她担任，因此这名字他并非不熟；至于这女子本人，他却一无所知，只懒懒地听着，并不在意打听得更详。可他的兴味立刻提了起来：紧挨着他的那人提到她时，把她同他认识的一个名字连在了一起。

“他们是一起从比亚里茨来的，”他听见那人说。“她是跟某君（Chose）去的，就是那个俄国人。”

“巴拉金（Balaguine）？”

“正是。”

===Original===

“What did she do with him?”

“Found the Tartar, I fancy.”

“And then?”

“Voilà, this young American is mad about her.”

“He is rich then?”

===Chinese===

“她把他怎么样了？”

“依我看，她碰上硬对手了。”

“后来呢？”

“*Voilà*，这个美国青年为她发狂呢。”

“那么说，他有钱？”

===Original===

“What would you? An American! They are it all.”

“Yes, a rich one always wins.”

“How mean you?”

“This: he plays bac at the Capucines. His banks are fructuous.”

===Chinese===

“有什么办法？美国人嘛！他们包办一切。”

“是啊，有钱的总是赢家。”

“此话怎讲？”

“这么说吧：他在嘉布遣会玩巴卡拉牌局，他坐的庄生利丰厚。”

===Original===

“Ah, as to that﻿—” And the first speaker shrugged his shoulders.

A rustle circled through the foyer, men stood aside and nodded affably. The lights took on a fairer glow. “Stay,” murmured the second speaker, “she is there.”

Through the parting crowd Mirette passed with a carriage such as no queen, save perhaps Semiramis, ever possessed. She moved from the hips, her body was erect and unswayed. It was the perfection of artificial grace. Her features were not regular, but there was an expression in them that stirred the pulse. “*Je suis l’Amour*,” she seemed to say, and to add “*prends garde à toi*.” As she crossed the room men moistened their lips, and when she had gone they found them still parched.

===Chinese===

“啊，这个嘛——”第一个说话的人耸了耸肩。

一阵窸窣声在休息室里绕行，男人们闪到一旁，和气地点头。灯光也添了一层更柔美的光辉。“慢着，”第二个说话的人低声道，“她在那儿呢。”

米蕾特穿过分开的人群，那步态仪容，除了塞弥拉弥斯（Semiramis），怕是任何王后都不曾有过。她走路从胯上发力，上身笔直，纹丝不摇。那是人工的优雅登峰造极之作。她的五官并不端正，可眉目间有一种叫脉搏加快的神情。“*Je suis l’Amour*（我是爱神），”她仿佛这样说，接着又补一句“*prends garde à toi*（你可要当心）。”她穿过厅堂时，男人们都舔了舔嘴唇；待她走远，他们发觉嘴唇还是干的。

===Original===

Mr. Incoul followed her with his eyes. She had not left him unimpressed, but his impression differed from that of his neighbors. In her face his shrewdness had discerned nothing but the animal and the greed of unsatiated appetites. He watched her pass, and stepped from the group in which he had been standing that he might the better follow her movements.

From the foyer she floated on into a side scene, yet not near enough to the stage to be seen by the audience. A few machinists moved aside to let her pass, and as they did so Mr. Incoul saw Lenox Leigh. It was evident that he had been waiting there for her coming. There was a scarf about her neck, and as the young man turned to greet her, she took it off and gave it into his keeping. They whispered together. Beyond, Mr. Incoul could see the tulle of the ballet rising and subsiding to the rhythm of the orchestra. Then came a sudden blare of trumpets, the measure swooned, and as it recovered again the ballet had faded to the back of the stage. Abruptly, as though sprung from a trapdoor, a régisseur appeared, and at a signal from him Mirette, with one quick backward stroke to her skirt, bounded from the side scene and fluttered down to the footlights amid a crash and thunder of applause.

Mr. Incoul had heard and seen enough. His mind was busy. He felt the need of fresh air and of solitude. He turned into the corridor and from there went through the vestibule until he reached an outer door, which he swung open and passed out into the night. He was thinly clad, in evening dress, and the air was chilly, but he thought nothing of his dress nor of the warmth or chill of the air. He walked up and down before the building with his head bent and his hands behind his back. A *camelot* offered him a pack of transparent cards, a vender of programmes pestered him to buy, but he passed them unheeding. For fully half an hour he continued his walk, and when he reentered the box, Maida, who of late had given much attention to his moods, noticed that his face was flushed, and that about his lips there played the phantom of a smile.

===Chinese===

因库尔先生目送着她。她并非没有给他留下印象，只是这印象与身边众人不同。凭着他的精明，他从她脸上只看出兽性，和永不餍足的胃口所生的贪求。他望着她走过去，便从站着的人群里跨出来，好把她的一举一动看得更真切。

她出了休息室，又飘进一处侧幕，那里离舞台尚远，观众看不见。几名置景工闪身让路；就在他们让开的一瞬，因库尔先生看见了伦诺克斯·利。他分明是在那里候她前来。她颈间围着一条纱巾；年轻人转身迎她时，她把纱巾解下，交给他保管。两人低低说着体己话。再远处，因库尔先生能望见芭蕾舞群的纱裙随乐队的节拍起落。忽然铜号齐鸣，乐句一阵晕厥；等它缓过神来，群舞已淡成舞台深处的背景。一位舞台监督（régisseur）像从活板门里弹出来似的蓦地现身；他一打手势，米蕾特向后迅捷一拂裙裾，从侧幕一跃而出，翩然飞落到脚灯前，掌声轰雷般炸响。

因库尔先生听得够了，也看得够了。他的心念转个不停。他需要新鲜空气，需要独自一人。他转身进了走廊，从那里穿过前厅，直到一扇临街的门前；他把门推开，走进夜色里。他衣衫单薄，只穿着晚礼服，夜气又寒，可他既不想身上的衣着，也不管空气的冷暖。他在剧院前来回踱步，垂着头，两手抄在背后。一个街头小贩（*camelot*）向他兜售一包透明纸牌，一个卖节目单的纠缠他购买，他都恍若无闻地走过。他足足踱了半个钟头；待回到包厢，近来对他的心境颇为留意的玛伊达，看出他脸上泛着红，唇边浮着一抹幽灵般的笑意。

## XIV Karl Grows a Moustache / 第十四章 卡尔蓄起了小胡子

===Original===

For several days Mr. Incoul was much occupied. He left the house early and returned to it late. One afternoon he sent for Karl. Since the return to Paris the courier’s duties had not been arduous; they consisted chiefly in keeping out of the way. On this particular afternoon he was not immediately discoverable, and when at last he presented himself it was in the expectation that the hour of his dismissal had struck. He bowed, nevertheless, with the best grace in the world, and noticing that his employer’s eyes were upon him, gazed deferentially at the carpet.

Mr. Incoul looked at him in a contemplative way for a moment or two. “Karl,” he said at last, and Karl raised his eyes.

“Yes, sir.”

“Have you any objections to shaving your whiskers?”

“I, sir? not the slightest.”

“I will be obliged if you will do so. This afternoon you might go to Cumberland’s and be measured. I have left orders there. Then take a room at the Meurice; you have money, have you not? Very good, keep an account of your expenditures. In a week I will send you my instructions. That will do for today.”

===Chinese===

接连好几天，因库尔先生忙得不可开交。他一早出门，很晚才归。一天下午，他派人去叫卡尔。自从回到巴黎以来，这位随行侍从的差事并不繁重，主要内容就是别碍事。这天下午，他一时没能随叫随到；及至终于露面，心里想的却是自己被辞退的时辰已经敲响。不过他仍旧鞠了一躬，那仪态堪称天下第一等优雅，又察觉雇主的眼睛正望着自己，便恭恭敬敬地盯住了地毯。

因库尔先生若有所思地打量了他一两分钟。“卡尔，”他终于开口，卡尔抬起了眼睛。

“是，先生。”

“把你的连鬓胡子剃掉，你有意见吗？”

“我吗，先生？一点也没有。”

“你若肯剃，我就感激不尽。今天下午你去坎伯兰（Cumberland’s）量尺寸，我已在那里留了话。然后到默里斯旅馆（the Meurice）开个房间；你有钱，是吧？很好，把你的开销一一记账。一周之内我会给你送来我的吩咐。今天就到这里。”

===Original===

An hour later Mr. Incoul was watching a game of baccarat at the Cercle des Capucines.

Meanwhile Lenox Leigh had given much of his time to the pleasures of Mirette’s society. In making her acquaintance at Biarritz he had been actuated partly by the idleness of the moment and partly by the attracting face of celebrity. He had never known a danseuse; indeed, heretofore, his acquaintance with women had been limited to those of his own *monde*, and during the succeeding days he hovered about her more that he might add a new photograph to a mental album than with any idea of conquest. She amused him extremely. In her speech she displayed a recklessness of adjective such as he had never witnessed before. It was not that she was brilliant, but she possessed that stereotyped form of repartee which is known as *bagou*, and which the Parisian takes to naturally and without effort. Mirette seemed to have acquired it in its supremest expression. One day, for instance, the curiosity of her circle of admirers was aroused by a young actress who, while painfully plain, squandered coin with remarkable ease. “Whom do you suppose she gets the money from?” someone asked, and Mirette without so much as drawing breath answered serenely, “A blind man.” In spite of the bagou Mirette was not a Parisian. She was born in the provinces, at Orléans, and was wont to declare herself a lineal descendant of Joan of Arc. She lied with perfect composure; if reproached she curled her lips. “Lies whiten the teeth,” she would say, an argument which it was impossible to refute.

Under the empire she would have been a success; under a republic she complained of the difficulty of making two ends meet. Now Lenox was not rich, but he was an American, and the Americans have assumed in Paris the position which the English once held. Their coffers are considered inexhaustible. On this subject, thanks to Mrs. Mackay, Mr. Incoul, the Vanderbilts, the Astors and a dozen others, there is now no doubt in the mind of the French. To be an American is to be a Vesuvius of gold pieces.

===Chinese===

一个钟头之后，因库尔先生已在嘉布遣会俱乐部看人打巴卡拉牌局了。

与此同时，伦诺克斯·利把大把时光消磨在与米蕾特交往的乐趣里。在比亚里茨结识她，一半是出于当时的无所事事，一半是名人那张有引力的脸孔在作祟。他从没结识过舞女；说真的，在此之前，他认识的女人仅限于他自己那个 *monde*（社交圈）里的，此后那几天他围着她打转，与其说存着什么征服的念头，不如说是想在一本心里的相册里再添一张新照片。她让他觉得有趣极了。她言谈间挥霍形容词的那种放肆，他前所未见。倒不是她如何机敏，而是她掌握着那一套程式化的应对，即人称 *bagou*（巴黎式油嘴）的东西——巴黎人生来就会，毫不费力。米蕾特似乎把这套功夫炼到了极致。譬如有一天，她那一圈崇拜者的好奇心被一个年轻女演员勾了起来：那演员长得其貌不扬到可怜，花钱却大方得惊人。“你猜她的钱是哪儿来的？”有人问。米蕾特连气都不换一口，安安静静地答：“一个瞎子。”尽管满口 bagou，米蕾特却不是巴黎人。她生在外省的奥尔良（Orléans），惯于自称是圣女贞德的嫡系后裔。她撒谎时镇定自若；若受责备，便撇撇嘴唇。“谎话能使牙齿变白，”她爱这么说——这论断叫人无从驳起。

在帝政时代她本会大红大紫；到了共和时代，她便抱怨日子入不敷出。伦诺克斯并不阔，可他是美国人，而美国人已在巴黎接了英国人当年坐的那把交椅。他们的钱柜被看作取之不竭。多亏麦凯太太、因库尔先生、范德比尔特家、阿斯特家以及其他一打人的示范，法国人在这一点上如今再无怀疑：做一个美国人，就是做一座金币的维苏威火山。

===Original===

As a native of the land of millions, Lenox found that his earliest attentions were received with smiles, and in time when a Russian became so scratched that the Tartar was visible, Mirette welcomed him with undisguised favor.

Like many another, Lenox had his small vanities; he would have liked to have thought himself indispensable to Maida’s happiness, but in her absence he did not object to being regarded as the *cavaliere servente* of the first lady of the ballet. Between the two women the contrast was striking. Mirette, as has been hinted, was reckless of adjective; she was animal, imperious, and at times frankly vulgar. Maida was her antithesis. She shrank from coarseness as from a deformity. Both represented Love, but they represented the extremes. One was as ignorant of virtue as the other was unconscious of vice. One was Mylitta, the other Psyche. Had the difference been less accentuated, it would have jarred. But the transition was immeasurable. It was like a journey from the fjords of Norway to the jungles of Hindustan. That Psyche was regretted goes without the need of telling, but Mylitta has enchantments which are said to lull regret.

In the second week of October the bathing was still delicious. The waves encircled one in a large, abrupt embrace. Mirette would have liked to remain, the beach was a daily triumph for her. There was not a woman in the world who could have held herself in the scantiest of costumes, under the fire of a thousand eyes, as gracefully as she. No sedan-chair for her indeed. No hurrying, no running, no enveloping wrap. No pretense or attempt to avoid the scrutiny of the bystanders. There was nothing of this for her. She crossed the entire width of sand, calmly, slowly, an invitation on her lips and with the walk and majesty of a queen. The amateurs as usual were tempted to applaud. It was indeed a triumph, an advertisement to boot, and one which she would have liked to prolong. But she was needed at the Opéra and so she returned to Paris accompanied by Lenox Leigh.

===Chinese===

作为百万金元之邦的子民，伦诺克斯发现自己最初献上的殷勤便博得了微笑；待到那位俄国人被刮蹭得连鞑靼人原形都露了出来，米蕾特便毫不掩饰地对他大加垂青。

同许多人一样，伦诺克斯也有他自己小小的虚荣；他很乐意自以为是玛伊达的幸福里不可或缺的人，但她不在跟前时，他也不反对被人看作芭蕾首席女伶的 *cavaliere servente*（护花骑士）。两个女人之间的反差触目惊心。米蕾特，如前文所暗示，挥霍起形容词来毫无节制；她肉感、专横，有时俗气得坦坦荡荡。玛伊达恰是她的反面，她避粗鄙如避畸形。两人都是爱的化身，却各执一端：一个对美德一无所知，正如另一个对罪恶浑然不觉。一个是米利塔（Mylitta），另一个是普赛克（Psyche）。倘若这差别不那么悬殊，本会叫人刺耳；可这转换无可计量，就像从挪威的峡湾一跃而至印度斯坦的丛林。普赛克令人想念，这自不待言；可米利塔自有种种魅术，据说能把想念哄睡。

十月的第二个星期，海水浴仍旧其乐无穷。浪头把人围在一个大大的、劈头盖脸的拥抱里。米蕾特本想留下，海滩于她是每天的凯旋。天底下再没有一个女人，能像她那样，穿得那么少，在千只眼睛的火力之下，还仪态万方。她自然不要轿椅。不匆匆，不疾走，不带裹身的浴袍，不假装、也不企图躲开旁观者的审视——这些一概与她无缘。她横穿过整片沙滩，从容地，缓缓地，唇边挂着一道邀请，迈着王后的步子，带着王后的威仪。行家们照例忍不住要鼓掌。这确乎是一场胜利，兼而是一幅活广告，一幅她巴不得延长下去的广告。可歌剧院等着她回去，于是她带着伦诺克斯·利返回了巴黎。

===Original===

In Paris it is considered inconvenient for a pretty woman to go about on foot, and as for cabs, where is the self-respecting chorus-girl who would consent to be seen in one? Mirette was very positive on this point and Lenox agreed with her thoroughly. He did not, however, for that reason offer to provide an equipage. Indeed the wherewithal was lacking. He had spent more money at Biarritz than he had intended, perhaps ten times the amount that he would have spent at Newport or at Cowes, and his funds were nearly exhausted.

As everyone is aware a banker is the last person in the world to be consulted on matters of finance. If a client has money in his pocket a banker can transfer it to his own in an absolutely painless manner, but if the client’s pocket is empty what banker, out of an opéra-bouffe, was ever willing to fill it? Lenox reflected over this and was at a loss how to act. The firm on whom his drafts were drawn held nothing on their ledgers to his credit. He visited them immediately on arriving and was given a letter which for the moment he fancied might contain a remittance. But it bore the Paris postmark and the address was in Maida’s familiar hand. As he looked at it he forgot his indigence, his heart gave an exultant throb. He had promised himself that when he met her again matters should go on very much as they had before, and he had further promised himself that so soon as his former footing was reestablished he would give up Mirette. He was therefore well pleased when the note was placed in his hands. It had a faint odor of orris, and he opened it as were he unfolding a lace handkerchief. But from what has gone before it will be understood that his pleasure was short lived. The note was brief and categoric, he read it almost at a glance, and when he had possessed himself of the contents he felt that the determination conveyed was one from which there was no appeal, or rather one from which any appeal would be useless. He looked at the note again. The handwriting suggested an unaccustomed strength, and in the straight, firm strokes he read the irrevocable. “It is done,” he muttered. “I can write Finis over that.” He looked again at the note and then tore it slowly into minute scraps, and watched them flutter from him.

He went out to the street and there his earlier preoccupation returned. It would be a month at least before a draft could be sent, and meanwhile, though he had enough for his personal needs, he had nothing with which to satisfy Mirette’s caprices. *Et elle en avait, cette dame!* The thought of separating from her did not occur to him, or if it did it was in that hazy indistinguishable form in which eventualities sometimes visit the perplexed. If Maida’s note had been other, he would have washed his hands of Mirette, but now apparently she was the one person on the Continent who cared when he came and when he went. In his present position he was like one who, having sprained an ankle, learns the utility of a crutch. The idea of losing it was not agreeable. Beside, the knowledge that his intimacy with the woman had been envied by grandees with unnumbered hats was to him a source of something that resembled consolation.

===Chinese===

在巴黎，一个漂亮女人步行出门被认为是不便的；至于出租马车，哪一位自重的合唱队姑娘肯让人瞧见自己坐在里面？在这一点上米蕾特态度极其坚决，伦诺克斯也全然赞同。不过他并不因此就提出由自己备一辆车。实在说，钱袋不允许。他在比亚里茨花的钱超出了本意，也许十倍于他在纽波特（Newport）或考斯（Cowes）会花的数目，他的资金已近告罄。

众所周知，世上最不该向之请教财务问题的就是银行家。若客户口袋里有钱，银行家自能以绝对无痛的手段把它挪进自己口袋；可若客户口袋空空，除了轻歌剧里，哪个银行家肯替他填满？伦诺克斯思前想后，一筹莫展。他的汇票所支取的那家商行，账簿上并没有存在他名下的分文。他一到巴黎便登门造访，接到了一封信，一时还以为里面或有汇款。可信盖的是巴黎邮戳，信封上的地址出自玛伊达那熟悉的笔迹。他望着它，忘了自己的拮据，心口得意地一跳。他早已暗自许定，再见她时，一切要照旧如初；又进一步许定，只消旧日的地位恢复，他便撇了米蕾特。因此短信递到手里时，他满心欢喜。信纸带着一缕淡淡的鸢尾根香，他拆信的样子，就像在展开一方蕾丝手帕。可是由前文可知，他的欢喜短命。短信简短而决绝，他差不多一眼就读完了；待领会了内容，他觉出信中的决计是无可上诉的——或者说，任何上诉都属徒劳。他把信又看了一遍。那笔迹透着一股不惯见的力度，在笔画端直坚定之间，他读出了无可挽回。“了结了，”他喃喃道，“我可以给它题上‘终卷’（Finis）二字了。”他再看看那短信，然后把它慢慢撕成细小的碎片，望着它们从手边飘散。

他走到街上，先前的心事又回来了。汇款至少要一个月后才能寄到，而此间他虽够自己开销，却拿不出什么来满足米蕾特的任性。*Et elle en avait, cette dame!*（这位太太的任性可真不少！）同她分手的念头他并不曾起过；即便掠过，也是模模糊糊、辨不真切的那一种——不测之事有时就是这样造访心烦意乱的人。倘若玛伊达的短信写的是另一副样子，他早把米蕾特撇清了；可如今，在欧洲大陆上，她分明是唯一在乎他来去的人了。他眼下的处境，就像一个崴了脚踝的人，方才领教了拐杖的好处——想到失去它，心里很不受用。再说，想到同这女人的亲密曾招来多少帽子数不清的贵人的艳羡，也足以给他一点近于安慰的东西。

===Original===

Presently he reached the boulevard. He was undecided what to do or where to turn, and as he loitered on the curb the silver head of a stick was waved at him from a passing cab; in a moment the vehicle stopped. May alighted and shook him by the hand.

“I am on my way to the Capucines,” he explained, in his blithesome stutter. “There’s a big game on; why not come, too?”

“A big game of what?”

“B-b, why baccarat of course. What did you suppose? M-marbles?”

Lenox fumbled in his waistcoat pocket. “Yes, I’ll go,” he said.

===Chinese===

不多时他走到了林荫大道。做什么、往哪儿去，他都拿不定主意；正傍着路牙子闲晃，一辆驶过的马车里，一根手杖的银头朝他挥了挥。车子随即停住，梅（May）跳下车来，同他握手。

“我正要到嘉布遣会去，”他结结巴巴、兴致勃勃地解释。“今晚有一场大牌局，何不同去？”

“什么大牌局？”

“巴、巴——自然是巴卡拉。你以为是什么？打、打弹珠吗？”

伦诺克斯在背心口袋里摸了摸。“好，我去，”他说。

===Original===

Five minutes later he was standing in a crowded room before a green table. He had never gambled, and hardly knew one card from another, but baccarat can be learned with such facility that after two deals a raw recruit can argue with a veteran as to whether it is better to stand on five or to draw. Lenox watched the flight of notes, gold and counters. He listened to the monotonous calls: *J’en donne! Carte! Neuf!* The end of the table at which he stood seemed to be unlucky. He moved to the other, and presently he leaned over the shoulder of a gamester and put down a few louis. In an hour he left the room with twenty-seven thousand francs.
